Maximal regularity estimates for the abstract Cauchy problems Thanks: The first and third author were partially supported by the NAWA/NSF grant BPN/NSF/2023/1/00001. The second author was supported by the National Science Centre, Poland, Project no. 2019/33/B/ST1/00165.
Sebastian Król & Mieczysław Mastyło & Jarosław Sarnowski

Abstract
. In this work, we extend the Da Prato–Grisvard theory of maximal regularity estimates for sectorial operators in interpolation spaces. Specifically, for any generator − 𝒜 -\mathcal{A} of an analytic semigroup on a Banach space 𝒳 \mathcal{X} , we identify the interpolation spaces between 𝒳 \mathcal{X} and the domain D 𝒜 D_{\mathcal{A}} of 𝒜 \mathcal{A} in which the part of 𝒜 \mathcal{A} satisfies certain maximal regularity estimates. We also establish several new results concerning both homogeneous and inhomogeneous L 1 L^{1} -maximal regularity estimates, extending and completing recent findings in the literature. These results are motivated not only by applications to problems in areas such as fluid mechanics but also by the intrinsic theoretical interest of the subject. In particular, we address the optimal choice of data spaces for the Cauchy problem associated with 𝒜 \mathcal{A} , ensuring the existence of strong solutions with global-in-time control of their derivatives. This control is measured via the homogeneous parts of the interpolation norms in the spatial variable and weighted Lebesgue norms over the time interval. Furthermore, we characterize weighted L 1 L^{1} -estimates and establish their relationship with unweighted estimates. Additionally, we reformulate the characterization condition for L 1 L^{1} -maximal regularity due to Kalton and Portal in a priori terms that do not rely on semigroup operators. Finally, we introduce a new interpolation framework for L p L^{p} -maximal regularity estimates, where p ∈ ( 1 , ∞ ) p\in(1,\infty) , within interpolation spaces generated by non-classical interpolation functors.



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
35B65, 35B40, 47D06, 46N20

1.
Introduction

In this paper, we study the solvability and maximal regularity estimates for solutions to the following abstract inhomogeneous Cauchy problem:

(CP)_{A,f,x}

u^{\prime}+Au=f\,\textrm{ on }(0,\tau)\quad\textrm{ and }\quad u(0)=x,

where
A
denotes a closed, linear operator on a complex Banach space
(X,\|\cdot\|_{X})
,

f
is the forcing term,
x\in X
represents the initial value, and
\tau\in(0,\infty]
is given.
To outline our main results we briefly present some facts that motivated our study.

The basic concept we deal with is the notion of
L^{p}
-maximal regularity estimates
; see (MRE) for
E=L^{p}
,
p\in[1,\infty]
, in Subsection
2.1
for the corresponding definition.
In the case when an operator
A
satisfy such
L^{p}
-estimates, we say that it has the
L^{p}
-maximal regularity on
(0,\tau)
(in short,
L^{p}
-m.r. on
(0,\tau)
). This concept has a rich history, and its interest stems from the fact that it is an important tool in the theory of non-linear evolution equations arising, e.g. in fluid mechanics. We refer the reader to, e.g.
[
2
]
,
[
39
]
,
[
40
]
,
[
47
]
and
[
32
]
, for the abstract theory and its application; see also references therein. The developed theory combines techniques and methods from harmonic analysis (singular integral operators, Fourier multipliers), the geometry of Banach spaces, the interpolation theory, and abstract evolution equations (semigroup theory). Some of these connections are further explored in this paper.

In this paper, we primarily study
L^{p}
-maximal regularity estimates when
p=1
and
p=\infty
. Such estimates are more restrictive for the geometry of
X
than those for
p\in(1,\infty)
. Indeed, the classical result by Baillon
[
7
]
(resp. by Guerre-Delabrière
[
29
]
) asserts that the existence of an unbounded generator of an analytic semigroup on a Banach space
X
which has
L^{\infty}
-m.r. (resp.
L^{1}
-m.r.) is equivalent to the fact that
X
contains an isomorphic copy of
c_{0}
(resp. complemented copy of

\ell^{1}
). In particular, when
X
is reflexive, no unbounded operator
A
does have
L^{1}
- and
L^{\infty}
-m.r.
Even in the case when
X
contains such copies, there exist
examples of concrete differential operators
A
, which play a crucial role in applications to PDEs, that do not satisfy the corresponding estimates. For instance,
the generators of the heat and Poisson semigroups on
X:=C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
or
X=L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
; see, e.g.
[
32
, Subsection 17.4.a, p.657]
.

One way to overcome these limitations is by employing interpolation techniques. The well-known result by Da Prato and Grisvard
[
17
,
18
]
states that for any generator
-{\mathcal{A}}
of an analytic semigroup
{\mathcal{T}}
on any Banach space
{\mathcal{X}}
, the part
A
of
{\mathcal{A}}
in the classical real interpolation spaces
X:=({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})_{\theta,p}
with
\theta\in(0,1)
and
p\in[1,\infty]
has
L^{p}
-maximal regularity on finite intervals. Recall that by the definition of the part of an operator,
D(A):=\{x\in D({\mathcal{A}})\cap X:{\mathcal{A}}x\in X\}
and
Ax:={\mathcal{A}}x
for
x\in D(A)
. Here,
D_{\mathcal{A}}
stands for the domain
D({\mathcal{A}})
of
{\mathcal{A}}
equipped with the graph norm, and
(\,\cdot\,)_{\theta,p}
is the real interpolation functor.
An adaptation of the Kalton-Lancien result
[
36
, Theorem 3.3]
shows that, in general, we do not have
L^{p}
-maximal regularity estimates over
\mathbb{R}_{+}
in the Da Prato-Grisvard theorem; see Remark
3.2
(b).
While this example may be considered artificial, we demonstrate that such a lack of
L^{1}
-estimates over
\mathbb{R}_{+}
occurs in the case of the part
A
of the negative Laplacian
{\mathcal{A}}=-\Delta
on
{\mathcal{X}}:=L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
; see Theorem
3.1
(iii). In addition, we study the measuring of the global time-regularity via weighted
L^{1}
norms; cf. the assertions (iii), (v) and (vi) of Theorem
3.1
. For, we provide a weighted counterpart of the Kalton-Portal result
[
37
, Theorem 3.6]
in Theorem
2.8
and explore its connection to the unweighted one in Corollary
2.9
and Proposition
2.12
. In particular, Proposition
2.12
provides the converse implication to
[
32
, Proposition 17.2.36(2), p.610]
, which has been left open therein. We treat this example as motivation for studying estimates over
\mathbb{R}_{+}
with respect to weaker (semi)norms than the original interpolation norms.

In this article, we examine the possibility of extending the Da Prato-Grisvard result to interpolation functors other than the classical real interpolation functors
(\cdot)_{\theta,p}
.
We restrict our studies mainly to a class of functors
K_{\Phi}
of
K
-method (our abstract results, Theorem
4.2
and Corollary
4.3
also refer to other class of functors).
The class of admissible parameters
\Phi
, which could
a priori
range among all Banach function spaces over
(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathrm{d}t)
, depends essentially on
p
and/or the geometry of an underlying Banach space
{\mathcal{X}}
.
In the case of
p=1,\infty
, on the one hand, we have to take into account the results of Baillon and Guerre-Delabrière mentioned above. It connects the problem of describing admissible
\Phi
to Lěvy’s type results from the interpolation theory (see, e.g.
[
12
, Theorems 4.6.25 and 4.6.28]
).
On the other hand we have to ensure an appropriate, working representation of the corresponding interpolation norm
\|\cdot\|_{K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})}
; see Proposition
2.23
.
It naturally imposes certain constrains on the class of
\Phi
, and consequently we deal here with weighted Lebesgue spaces
L^{1}_{w}
and
L^{\infty}_{w}
for arbitrary weight functions
w
; see also complementary results Propositions
2.15
and
2.22
.

In Subsection
2.7
, we discuss equivalent representations of the
homogeneous part
(see (
23
)) of the classical real interpolation norms to the one provided in Proposition
2.23
. Our Proposition
2.24
extends the result due to Haase
[
30
, Theorem 6.4.2]
and shows the sharpness of the assumptions of some its ingredients. Furthermore, Proposition
2.26
allows to express the characterization condition for the
L^{1}
-m.r. due to Kalton and Portal
[
37
]
in
a priori
terms of
A
(i.e. its resolvent). Moreover, in Theorem
2.28
, for the negative generator
{\mathcal{A}}
of an analytic semigroup on a Banach space
{\mathcal{X}}
, we introduce a subspace
X
of
{\mathcal{X}}
, which becomes a Banach space with respect to a natural norm and such that the part
A
of
{\mathcal{A}}
in
X
satisfies
L^{1}
-m.r. estimates on whole
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.
The space
X
can be naturally seen as an end-point of the real interpolation scale
({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})_{\theta,1}
with
\theta\in(0,1)
; cf. Remark
2.29
. In comparison with the Da Prato-Grisvard theorem, a remarkable phenomenon occurs here.

We also study an optimal choice of the space of forcing terms
f
in
(CP)_{A,f,x}
, which guarantees the existence of strong solutions with prescribed global-in-time control of their derivatives, measured via the homogeneous parts of the corresponding interpolation norms (in the space variable) and weighted Banach function norms (in the time variable); see Theorems
3.1
(ii) and
3.3
(ii), Corollary
3.8
, Propositions
3.5
and
3.6
, as well as Remark
3.7
. Our assumptions on
f
for deriving the homogeneous estimates (
32
), (
38
) and (
45
) essentially relax those stated, e.g. in
[
51
, Proposition 2.14 and Theorem 2.20]
.

In Section 5, we apply our abstract results to study the maximal regularity estimates for the negative Laplace operator in homogeneous Besov spaces. With the help of the main preparatory result, Proposition
5.2
, we establish the maximal regularity of the negative homogeneous Laplacian on homogeneous Besov spaces in Corollary
5.5
(i). Additionally, part (ii) of this corollary identifies a
limiting
space of force terms
f
, which, on the one hand, ensures the existence of strong solutions to the Cauchy problem associated with the Laplace operator on
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, where
p\in[1,\infty)
, or
C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. On the other hand, compared to the results in Section 3, it provides additional control over the time behavior of the homogeneous norms of the solutions
u
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

2.
Preliminaries

In this section we provide auxiliary results for the proofs of our main results in the following sections. Some of these preparatory results may be of independent interest in the context of the general theory; see e.g. Lemma
2.2
, Propositions
2.6
,
2.12
,
2.15
,
2.22
,
2.24
,
2.26
, and Theorem
2.28
.
The subsections 2.1-2.3 are devoted to the maximal regularity. The subsections 2.5-2.8 deal with interpolation spaces and their corresponding (semi)-norms.
The particular context of these subsections is well-reflected by their corresponding titles.

2.1.
The maximal regularity

Consider the abstract Cauchy problem
(CP)_{A,f,x}
associated with a closed, linear operator
A
on a complex Banach space
X
.
A function
u\in W^{1,1}_{loc}([0,\tau);X)
is called a
strong solution
to
(CP)_{A,f,x}
if
u(0)=x
,
u(t)\in D(A)
and
u^{\prime}(t)+Au(t)=f(t)
for a.e.
t\in(0,\tau)
.

In this paper we also consider the notion of
maximal regularity on
I=(0,\tau)
with respect to function spaces
E
other than the Lebesgue
L^{p}
spaces. The standard notion of
L^{p}
-maximal regularity is reformulated verbatim if
E
represents, for instance, Banach function space such as a weighted
L^{p}
space or more general symmetric space (e.g. Lorentz or Orlicz spaces).

For completeness, recall that a Banach space
E=(E,\|\cdot\|_{E})
is called a
Banach function space over
\mathbb{R}_{+}:=(0,\infty)
if
E
is an order ideal in the space
L^{0}:=L^{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathrm{d}t)
of all Lebesgue measurable, complex functions on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
, equipped with the topology of convergence in Lebesgue measure, and if the norm
\|\cdot\|_{E}
is monotone. This means that if
|h|\leq|g|
for
g\in E
and
h\in L^{0}
, then
h\in E
and
\|h\|_{E}\leq\|g\|_{E}
.

Suppose that
1\leq p<\infty
and that
v
is a
weight function
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
(
weight
for short), that is, a positive, measurable function on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.
We denote by
L^{p}_{v}
(or
L^{p}(v)
) the (weighted) Lebesgue space
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}_{+},v\mathrm{d}t)
with the standard norm. In the case
p=\infty
, we denote by
L_{v}^{\infty}
(or
L^{\infty}(v)
)
the Banach function space over
\mathbb{R}_{+}
of all functions
f\in L^{0}

such that
fv\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathrm{d}t)
, equipped with the norm

\|f\|_{L_{v}^{\infty}}:=\textrm{ess\,sup}_{t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}|f(t)v(t)|.

If
(X,\|\cdot\|_{X})
is a Banach space, then the
X
-valued variant of
E
is defined to be the space
E(\mathbb{R}_{+};X)
(
E(X)
for short) of all strongly measurable functions
f\colon\mathbb{R}_{+}\to X
such that
\|f(\cdot)\|_{X}\in E
. This is a Banach space equipped with the norm

\|f\|_{E(X)}:=\|\|f(\cdot)\|_{X}\|_{E}.

where, as usual, we identify the space of measurable functions with the space of equivalence classes of measurable functions.
For the interval
I=(0,\tau)
,
\tau\in(0,\infty)
, let
E(I;X)
(or
E(0,\tau;X)
) denote the space of all functions from
I
into
X
such that their extensions by
0\in X
to functions on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
belong to
E(X)
. As usual, one can identify
E(I;X)
with a subspace of
E(X)
.
We write
L^{1}_{loc}
to denote
L^{1}_{loc}([0,\infty),\mathrm{d}t)
, that is, the space of all locally integrable, complex functions on
[0,\infty)
. For simplicity, we often write
\|f\|_{X}
instead of
\|f(\cdot)\|_{X}
below.

For a Banach function space
E
which embeds continuously into
L^{1}_{loc}
and
I=(0,\tau)
,
\tau\in(0,\infty]
, we say that the operator
A
has the
E
-maximal regularity on
I
(for short,
E
-m.r. on
I
), if for every
f\in E(I;X)
there exists a unique
strong
solution
u
to
(CP)_{A,f,0}
such that
u^{\prime},Au\in E(I;X)
. In this case, by the closedness of
A
, the following
E
-maximal regularity estimate
holds:

(MRE)

\big\|u^{\prime}\big\|_{E(I;X)}+\big\|Au\|_{E(I;X)}\leq C\big\|f\big\|_{E(I;X)}

for some constant
C
independent of
f\in E(I;X)
. We say that
A
has ’
E
-m.r.’ if
A
has
E
-m.r. on
(0,\tau)
for some
\tau\in(0,\infty]
.

Some caution is required concerning this notion in the case
I=\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Note that we do not require that
u\in E(\mathbb{R}_{+};X)
.
In the case when
E=L^{p}
,
p\in[1,\infty]
, the definition stated above is consistent with the one from, e.g. Kalton and Portal
[
37
]
or from the recent monograph on the subject
[
32
, Chapter 17]
,
but differs to that considered, e.g. by Dore in
[
21
]
, where
u\in W^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}_{+};X)
is assumed. This variant is more adequate in some situations,
for instance, in the case when
A
is not invertible, but the estimates on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
of the type (
MRE
) play a role; see e.g.
[
51
,
44
]
and the references therein.
Examples of this arise naturally from differential operators on unbounded regions, such as the Laplace operator or the Stokes operator on exterior domains; cf. Giga and Sohr
[
26
]
.

To clarify the novelty of our results and their proofs, we provide the following remark, which reformulates Dore’s results on
L^{p}
-maximal regularity and adapts them to our framework.

Remark 2.1
.

As mentioned above, the notion of
L^{p}
-maximal regularity on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
in Dore
[
21
]
differs from the one that we make use of in this work. However, by Lebesgue’s integral representation of the strong solutions, it is readily seen that, in the case of finite intervals, both definitions coincide.

In particular, for all
\tau\in(0,\infty]
, the
L^{p}
-m.r. on
(0,\tau)
for a given operator
A
(the notion we work with here), yields
u\in L^{p}_{loc}([0,\tau);X)
for the corresponding solution
u
of
(CP)_{A,f,x}
with
f\in L^{p}(0,\tau;X)
.
Furthermore, by
[
21
, Corollary 5.4]
, if
A
has the
L^{p}
-m.r. on
(0,\tau)
for some
\tau
, then it has
L^{p}
-m.r. on each finite interval, that is, for all
\tau\in(0,\infty)
.

Consequently, for each
\tau
and
p\in[1,\infty]
, by
[
21
, Corollary 4.4]
, the
L^{p}
-m.r. on
(0,\tau)
implies that
-A
is the generator of an analytic (but not necessarily strongly continuous at
0
) semigroup
T=(T(t))_{t>0}
on
X
. In addition, in the case when
\tau=\infty
, an analysis analysis of the proof of
[
21
, Theorem 4.3]
yields that the generated semigroup is uniformly bounded, i.e.
\sup_{t>0}\|T(t)\|_{{\mathcal{L}}(X)}<\infty
; see also
[
32
, Theorem 17.2.15(2), p. 586]
.

Following
[
4
, p. 128]
, for a Banach space
X
and a strongly continuous function
T:(0,\infty)\to{\mathcal{L}}(X)
, we say that
T
is a
semigroup on
X
, if
T(t+s)=T(t)T(s)
for all
s,t>0
,
\sup_{t\in(0,1)}\|T(t)\|_{{\mathcal{L}}(X)}<\infty
, and
T(t)x=0
for all
t>0
implies
x=0
.
From
[
4
, Proposition 3.2.4, p. 126]
it follows that there exists an operator
A
on
X
, such that
(-\infty,\omega)\subset\rho(A)
for some
\omega\in\mathbb{R}
and

(\lambda+A)^{-1}=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}T(t)\mathrm{d}t,\qquad\lambda>\omega.

The operator
-A
is called a
generator
of
T
. For the generator
-A
of a semigroup
T
on
X
, we set for every
f\in L^{1}_{loc}([0,\infty);X)

(1)

Uf(t):=U_{A}f(t):=\int_{0}^{t}T(t-s)f(s)\mathrm{d}s,\qquad t\geq 0.

We call
U
the
solution operator
corresponding to the operator
A
.
The above-mentioned independence of the property of the
L^{p}
-m.r. on
(0,\tau)
for
\tau\in(0,\infty)
, shows that for any operator
A
with
L^{p}
-m.r. on a finite interval, the corresponding solution operator
U
is continuous on
L^{p}_{loc}([0,\infty);X)
and, in addition, for every
f\in L^{p}_{loc}([0,\infty);X)

(2)

Uf(t)\in D(A)\quad\textrm{ for a.e. }t>0\quad\textrm{and}\quad AUf,\,Uf\in L^{p}_{loc}([0,\infty);X).

In addition, according to Dore
[
21
, Theorem 4.1]
, if
U
is bounded on
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}_{+};X)
, then
0\in\rho(A)
, or, equivalently, the semigroup
T
has the negative exponential growth bound. Conversely, if
A
has
L^{p}
-m.r. on a finite interval and
0\in\rho(A)
, then
U
is bounded on
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}_{+};X)
. In particular, if we a priori know that
-A
generates a bounded, analytic semigroup on
X
and
0\in\rho(A)
, then the both definitions of the
L^{p}
-m.r. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
coincide; see
[
21
, Theorem 5.2]
.

In addition, according to Dore
[
21
, Theorem 4.1]
, if
U
is bounded on
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}_{+};X)
, then
0\in\rho(A)
, or, equivalently, the semigroup
T
has the negative exponential growth bound. Conversely, if
A
has
L^{p}
-m.r. on a finite interval and
0\in\rho(A)
, then
U
is bounded on
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}_{+};X)
. In particular, if we a priori know that
-A
generates a bounded, analytic semigroup on
X
and
0\in\rho(A)
, then both definitions of the
L^{p}
-m.r. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
coincide; see
[
21
, Theorem 5.2]
.

2.2.
The mild solutions

For any densely defined generator
-A
of a semigroup
T
(not necessarily analytic), it is a standard fact of the semigroup theory (following from the infinitesimal characterization of
A
), that the regularity of the function
Uf
(see (
1
)) is in a correspondence with the condition:
Uf(t)\in D(A)
and
AUf(t)=(Uf)^{\prime}(t)+f(t)
for a.e.
t>0
; see e.g. Pazy
[
45
, Theorem 2.9, p.109]
.
In the case of non-densely defined
-A
(which naturally arise in the context of the Da Prato-Grisvard result for the functor
(\,\cdot\,)_{\theta,\infty}
; see Lemma
2.30
) a corresponding statement can be found in
[
32
, Proposition 17.1.3, p.572]
.

Let
\tau>0
, following
[
4
, p.130]
, we say that a function
u\in C([0,\tau];X)
is a mild solution to
(CP)_{A,f,x}
, if
\int_{0}^{t}u(s)\mathrm{d}s\in D(A)
and

u(t)=A\int_{0}^{t}u(s)\mathrm{d}s+x+\int_{0}^{t}f(s)\mathrm{d}s,\qquad t\in[0,\tau].

In the case when
\tau=\infty
, the definition is analogous.

The following lemma is a refined version of
[
32
, Proposition 17.1.3, p.572]
and
[
45
, Theorem 2.9, p.109]
. In particular, the statement (iii) of Lemma
2.2
, which says that for any generator of a semigroup the function
v(t):=T(t)x+Uf(t)
,
t>0
, is always a mild solution to
(CP)_{A,f,x}
if
x\in\overline{D(A)}
, seems to be not stated in the literature on the semigroup theory, and may be of an independent interest. In particular, it extends the Da Prato-Sinestrari result, see
[
4
, Proposition 3.7.22, p.162]
, by showing that the assumption of the analyticity of
T
is redundant.
Furthermore, notice that assertion (ii) enables an equivalent reformulation of the definition of
L^{p}
-m.r., provided we already know that
-A
is the generator of a semigroup.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
-A
be the generator of a semigroup
T
on a Banach space
X
.
Let
f\in L^{1}_{loc}([0,\infty);X)
and set

(3)

u(t):=Uf(t)=\int_{0}^{t}T(t-s)f(s)\mathrm{d}s,\qquad t>0.

The following assertions hold.

(i)

If
u
has the strong derivative at Lebesgue’s point
r>0
of the function
f
, then
u(r)\in D(A)
and
Au(r)=f(r)-u^{\prime}(r)
.

(ii)

For every
r>0
,
\int_{0}^{r}u(s)\mathrm{d}s\in D(A)
and

u(r)+A\int_{0}^{r}u(s)\mathrm{d}s=\int_{0}^{r}f(s)\mathrm{d}s.

In particular, if
u(t)\in D(A)
for a.e.
t>0
and
Au\in L^{1}_{loc}([0,\infty);X)
, then for each point
r
which is Lebesgue’s point of
f
and
Au
, the function
u
has a strong derivative at
r
and
u^{\prime}(r)=f(r)-Au(r)
.

(iii)

For every
x\in X
the function
v(t):=T(t)x+u(t)
,
t>0
, is continuous and bounded on each interval
(0,\tau)
,
\tau>0
, and for every
r>0
,

\int_{0}^{r}v(s)\mathrm{d}s\in D(A)\quad\textrm{and}\quad v(r)+A\int_{0}^{r}v(s)\mathrm{d}s=\int_{0}^{r}f(s)\mathrm{d}s.

In particular, if
x\in\overline{D(A)}
, then
v
is a mild solution to
(CP)_{A,f,x}
, that is, in addition,
\lim_{t\rightarrow 0\raisebox{0.95pt}{$+$}}v(t)=x
.

The proof of (i) makes use of the basic properties of the vector-valued Riemann-Stieltjes integral; see, e.g.
[
4
, Chapter 1.9]
. It clearly shows how the definition of the generator
A
via Laplace’s transform still allows one to involve the continuity of the generated semigroup only on
(0,\infty)
.

For the proof of (ii) one could follow the idea of that of (i), but details are more technical. We obtained it as a direct consequence of the (vector) Fubini theorem and the following auxiliary result, which also plays a crucial role in the proofs of some results presented in the next sections; see, e.g. Propositions
2.23
.

Lemma 2.3

(
[
30
, Proposition A.8.2, p.298]
)
.

Let
-A
be the generator of a semigroup
T
on a Banach space
X
. Then, for every
x\in X
and
t>0
we have that

\int_{0}^{t}T(s)x\mathrm{d}s\in D(A)\quad\textrm{ and }\quad A\int_{0}^{t}T(s)x\mathrm{d}s=x-T(t)x.

For any generator
-A
of a
C_{0}
-semigroup the conclusion of Lemma
2.3
is the well-known fact from the semigroup theory, which simply follows from the infinitesimal characterization of
-A
.

Proof of Lemma
2.2
.

The proof of the both statements is based on the following characterization of the elements from the graph of
A
: for each
x,y\in X
and
\lambda\in\rho(A)
, the equality

(4)

\lambda(\lambda+A)^{-1}x-x=(\lambda+A)^{-1}y

is equivalent to
x
belonging to
D(A)
with
Ax=-y
.

Fix
\lambda>\omega
, where
\omega>0
is such that
\sup_{t\geq 0}e^{-\omega t}\|T(t)\|_{{\mathcal{L}}(X)}<\infty
.
Then,
(\lambda+A)^{-1}=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-t\lambda}T(t)\mathrm{d}t
with the convergence in the strong operator topology of
X
.

For (i), it is sufficient to show (
4
) for
x
and
y
replaced with
u(r)
and
u^{\prime}(r)-f(r)
, respectively. Note that the integration by parts formula for the Riemann-Stieltjes integral we get that

\displaystyle\lambda(\lambda+A)^{-1}u(r)-u(r)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0\raisebox{0.95pt}{$+$}}\int_{\epsilon}^{1/\epsilon}\Bigl(\int_{0}^{r}T(t+r-s)f(s)\mathrm{d}s-u(r)\Bigr)\mathrm{d}(-e^{-\lambda t})

\displaystyle=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0\raisebox{0.95pt}{$+$}}e^{-\lambda/\epsilon}\Bigl(\int_{0}^{r}T(1/\epsilon+r-s)f(s)\mathrm{d}s-u(r)\Bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad-\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0\raisebox{0.95pt}{$+$}}e^{-\lambda\epsilon}\Bigl(\int_{0}^{r}T(\epsilon+r-s)f(s)\mathrm{d}s-u(r)\Bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad+\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0\raisebox{0.95pt}{$+$}}\int_{\epsilon}^{1/\epsilon}e^{-\lambda t}{\mathrm{d}}\left(\int_{0}^{r}T(t+r-s)f(s)\mathrm{d}s\right).

It is readily seen that the first two limits are equal to zero (with the help of Lebesgue’s domination theorem for the second one).

Therefore, by, e.g.
[
4
, Proposition 1.9.11, p.65]
, it is sufficient to show that the function
G(t):=\int_{0}^{r}T(t+r-s)f(s)\mathrm{d}s
,
t>0
, is an antiderivative of
g(t):=T(t)u^{\prime}(r)-T(t)f(r)
,
t>0
, which is continuous, and consequently Bochner integrable on finite intervals. Then, since the function
e^{-\lambda(\cdot)}g(\cdot)
is Bochner integrable on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
, we get that

\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0\raisebox{0.95pt}{$+$}}\int_{\epsilon}^{1/\epsilon}e^{-\lambda t}g(t){\mathrm{d}}t=(\lambda+A)^{-1}(u^{\prime}(r)-f(r)).

Moreover, it is easily seen that by the continuity of
g
, if we show that the right derivative of
G
exists and is equal to
g
on
(0,\infty)
, then its (full) derivative does.
For, first note that for all
h>0
we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{r}

\displaystyle T(t+r+h-s)f(s)\mathrm{d}s-\int_{0}^{r}T(t+r-s)f(s)\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=T(t)[u(r+h)-u(r)]-\int_{r}^{r+h}T(t+r+h-s)f(s)\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=T(t)[u(r+h)-u(r)]-\int_{r}^{r+h}T(t+r-s)f(s)\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{r}^{r+h}[T(t+r+h-s)-T(t+r-s)]f(s)\mathrm{d}s

Since
\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}\frac{1}{h}\int_{r}^{r+h}\|f(s)-f(r)\|_{X}\mathrm{d}s=0
, by the strong continuity of
T
on
(0,\infty)
, we easily get

\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}\frac{1}{h}\int_{r}^{r+h}T(t+r-s)f(s)\mathrm{d}s=T(t)f(r)

\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}\frac{1}{h}\int_{r}^{r+h}[T(t+r+h-s)-T(t+r-s)]f(s)\mathrm{d}s=0.

Therefore, it gives that
G^{\prime}_{+}(t)=g(t)
, and as was noted above, concludes the proof of (i).

For (ii), let
r>0
. By Fubini’s theorem we get

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{r}Uf(s)\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{r}\int_{0}^{s}T(s-\tau)f(\tau)\mathrm{d}\tau\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{r}\int_{0}^{r-\tau}T(s)f(\tau)\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}\tau.

Note that the function
\phi:[0,r]\ni\tau\mapsto\int_{0}^{r-\tau}T(s)f(\tau)\mathrm{d}s\in X
is in
L^{1}(0,r;X)
. Moreover, Lemma
2.3
gives that
\phi(\tau)\in D(A)
with
A\phi(\tau)=f(\tau)-T(r-\tau)f(\tau)
,
\tau\in[0,r]
. Therefore, by Hille’s theorem (see, e.g.
[
4
, Proposition 1.1.7]
) we get that
\int_{0}^{r}\phi(\tau)\mathrm{d}\tau\in D(A)
and

\displaystyle A\int_{0}^{r}\phi(\tau)\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle=\int^{r}_{0}A\phi(\tau)\mathrm{d}\tau=\int^{r}_{0}(f(\tau)-T(r-\tau)f(\tau))\mathrm{d}\tau

It gives the first statement of (ii).
The second one is a direct consequence of Hille’s theorem and
[
4
, Proposition 1.2.2]
.

For (iii), by
[
30
, Proposition A.8.2, p.298]
, for every
x\in X
and
r>0
we have that

\int_{0}^{r}T(s)x\mathrm{d}s\in D(A)\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad A\int_{0}^{r}T(s)x\mathrm{d}s=x-T(r)x.

Combining it with (ii) we easily see that the function
v
has the desired properties stated in (iii); for the last statement therein see, for instance,
[
4
, Lemma 3.3.12, p.149]
.
Therefore, the proof is complete.
∎

For further references we extract useful consequences in the following lemma.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
-A
be the generator of an analytic semigroup
T
on a Banach space
X
. Then the following statements hold.

(i)

For every
f\in L^{1}_{loc}([0,\infty);X)
and every
t>0
such that
Uf(t)\in D(A)
we have that

(5)

\bigl\|AUf(t)\big\|_{X}\leq\int_{0}^{t}\|AT(t-s)f(s)\|_{X}\mathrm{d}s.

(ii)

If
A
has
L^{1}
-m.r., then for any
f\in L^{1}_{loc}([0,\infty);X)
and for a.e.
t>0
the function
(0,t)\ni s\mapsto AT(t-s)f(s)\in X
is Bochner integrable.

(iii)

Let
\tau>0
. If for every
f\in L^{1}(0,\tau;X)
and for a.e.
t\in(0,\tau)
the function
(0,t)\ni s\mapsto AT(t-s)f(s)\in X
is Bochner integrable on
(0,t)
with
\|\int_{0}^{t}AT(t-s)f(s)\mathrm{d}s\|_{L^{1}(0,\tau;X)}<\infty
, then
A
has
L^{1}
-m.r. on
(0,\tau)

(
on each finite interval
)
.

Proof.

(i) For all
t>0
, the function
(0,t)\ni s\mapsto T(t-s)f(s)\in X
is Bochner integrable and
(0,t)\ni s\mapsto AT(t-s)f(s)\in X
is strongly measurable (see, e.g.
[
4
, Proposition 1.3.4]
).
By Bochner’s theorem, for
t>0
such that
(0,t)\ni s\mapsto\|AT(t-s)f(s)\|_{X}
is Lebesgue integrable, we have

\Big\|\int_{0}^{t}AT(t-s)f(s)\mathrm{d}s\Big\|_{X}\leq\int_{0}^{t}\|AT(t-s)f(s)\|_{X}\mathrm{d}s.

Moreover, by Hille’s theorem
[
4
, Proposition 1.1.7]
, we get for such
t
that

A\int_{0}^{t}T(t-s)f(s)\mathrm{d}s=\int_{0}^{t}AT(t-s)f(s)\mathrm{d}s.

It gives (
5
) in such case. Otherwise, that is, for
t>0
such that
(0,t)\ni s\mapsto\|AT(t-s)f(s)\|_{X}
is not Lebesgue integrable, i.e.
\int_{0}^{t}\|AT(t-s)f(s)\|_{X}\mathrm{d}s=\infty
, if we know that
Uf(t)\in D(A)
, or in the other words,
AUf(t)
is identified as a vector in
X
, then (
5
) holds trivially.

(ii) By Remark
2.1
(b), for
\lambda>0
large enough the operator
A+\lambda
has
L^{1}
-m.r. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
. The Kalton-Portal characterization (see
[
37
, Theorem 3.6]
, or Theorem
2.8
with
v\equiv 1
below) yields the existence of a constant
C_{\lambda}
, which may depend on
\lambda
, such that

\int^{\infty}_{0}\|(A+\lambda)e^{-\lambda s}T(s)x\|_{X}\leq C_{\lambda}\|x\|_{X},\qquad x\in{X}.

In particular, for every
\tau>0
and
x\in{X}
, we easily get

\displaystyle\int^{\tau}_{0}\|AT(s)x\|_{X}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq e^{\tau}\lambda\int^{\tau}_{0}\|(A+\lambda)e^{-\lambda s}T(s)x\|_{X}\mathrm{d}s+\int^{\tau}_{0}\|\lambda T(s)x\|_{X}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq e^{\tau\lambda}\int^{\infty}_{0}\|(A+\lambda)e^{-\lambda s}T(s)x\|_{X}\mathrm{d}s+\tau\lambda\sup_{0\leq s\leq\tau}\|T(s)\|_{{\mathcal{L}}(X)}\,\|x\|_{X}

(6)

\displaystyle\leq(C_{\lambda}e^{\tau\lambda}+\tau\lambda\sup_{0\leq s\leq\tau}\|T(s)\|_{{\mathcal{L}}(X)})\,\|x\|_{X}.

Further, since for every
f\in L^{1}_{loc}([0,\infty);X)
and
\tau>0
, the function
(0,\tau)^{2}\ni(t,s)\mapsto\|AT(t-s)f(s)\|_{X}\chi_{\{0<s\leq t\leq\tau\}}
is measurable, by the scalar Fubini theorem we have that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{0}^{t}\|AT(t-s)f(s)\|_{X}\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{s}^{\tau}\|AT(t-s)f(s)\|_{X}\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{0}^{\tau-s}\|AT(t)f(s)\|_{X}\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{0}^{\tau}\|f(s)\|_{X}\mathrm{d}s<\infty

for
C
specified in (
6
). Since
\tau>0
is arbitrary here, we get the desired claim.

For (iii), Lemma
2.2
shows that
Uf
is in
W^{1,1}(0,\tau;X)
and
(Uf)^{\prime}(t)+AUf(t)=f(t)
for a.e.
t\in(0,\tau)
. The closed graph theorem applied to the operator
AU
gives the estimate (
MRE
), therefore
A
has
L^{1}
-m.r. on
(0,\tau)
.
∎

Remark 2.5
.

Recall that for the generator
-A
of an analytic semigroup
T
on
X
,
T(t)X\subset D(A)
for all
t>0
and for all
x\in D(A)
the function
\mathbb{R}_{+}\ni t\mapsto AT(t)x=T(t)Ax\in X
is in
L^{1}_{loc}([0,\infty);X)
.

However, by the Kalton-Portal characterisation of
L^{1}
-m.r. (on a finite interval) and the closed graph theorem, if
A
does not have
L^{1}
-m.r., then there exists
x\in X
such that the function
AT(\cdot)x
is not Bochner integrable on
[0,t]
for any
t>0
, more precisely,
\int_{0}^{t}\|AT(s)x\|_{X}\mathrm{d}s=\infty
for all
t>0
.
Therefore, for some vectors
x\notin D(A)
and all
t>0
the symbol
\int_{0}^{t}AT(t-s)f(s)\mathrm{d}s
for
f(s)=x
may not have any meaning (as a Bochner integral). On the other hand, by Lemma
2.3
, for any
x\in X
if
f(s)=x

(s>0)
, then
A\int_{0}^{t}T(t-s)f(s)\mathrm{d}s=A\int_{0}^{t}T(s)x\mathrm{d}s
is well defined (cf. Lemma
2.4
(iii)).

Combining Lemma
2.4
(i) with Remark
2.1
(b), if
A
has
L^{p}
-m.r. for some
p\in[1,\infty]
, then for any
f\in L^{p}_{loc}([0,\infty);X)
the inequality (
5
) holds for a.e.
t>0
.
The following result, which can also be considered as an extension of Dore
[
21
, Theorem 7.1]
and Hytönen
[
33
, Theorem 1.4]
, in particular, shows that it holds for every
f\in L^{1}_{loc}([0,\infty);X)
.
Below, for a weight function
v
and a measurable subset
B
of
\mathbb{R}_{+}
we set
v(B):=\int_{B}v(t)\mathrm{d}t
.

Proposition 2.6
.

Let
p\in[1,\infty]
and let
A
be a linear operator on a Banach space
X
with
L^{p}
-m.r. on a finite interval.

Then, for every Muckenhoupt weight
v\in A_{1}(\mathbb{R})
and every
\tau>0
, the operator
AU
is continuous operator from
L^{1}_{v}(0,\tau;X)
to
L^{1,\infty}_{v}(0,\tau;X)
, i.e. for every
f\in L^{1}_{loc}([0,\infty),v\mathrm{d}t;X)
,
Uf(t)\in D(A)
for a.e.
t>0
and there exists a constant
C_{\tau}
independent on
f
such that for every
\lambda>0

v(\{t\in(0,\tau):\|AUf(t)\|_{X}\geq\lambda\})\leq C_{\tau}\lambda^{-1}\|f\|_{L^{1}_{v}(0,\tau;X)}.

Proof.

By Dore
[
21
, Corollary 4.4]
,
-A
is the generator of an analytic semigroup
T
on
X
.
Suppose first, that the exponential growth bound of
T
is negative. Then, by Dore
[
21
, Theorem 5.2]
, the operator
AU
is bounded on
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}_{+};X)
.

Naturally identifying
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}_{+};X)
with a subspace of
L^{p}(\mathbb{R};X)
,
AU
can be consider as an operator
{\mathcal{S}}
on
L^{p}(\mathbb{R};X)
, i.e.
{\mathcal{S}}f:=eAU(rf)
,
f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R};X)
, where
e
stands for the extension by
0\in X
from
\mathbb{R}_{+}
onto
\mathbb{R}
and
r
for the restriction from
\mathbb{R}
to
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.
Of course,
{\mathcal{S}}
is bounded on
L^{p}(\mathbb{R};X)
and for every
f:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow X
which is bounded with the compact support
\operatorname{supp}f
and for every
t\notin\operatorname{supp}f
we have

{\mathcal{S}}f(t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathcal{K}}(t-s)f(s)\mathrm{d}s,

where
{\mathcal{K}}(t):=AT(t)
if
t>0
and
{\mathcal{K}}(t):=0\in{\mathcal{L}}(X)
otherwise.
Since
T
is a bounded, analytic semigroup on
X
,
{\mathcal{K}}
satisfies the Calderón-Zygmund conditions. Therefore, by weighted variant of the result by Benedek, Calderón, Panzone
[
8
]
, see
[
23
, Chapter V, Theorem 3.4]
,
{\mathcal{S}}
satisfies the weak
(1,1)
estimates on
L^{p}(\mathbb{R};X)\cap L^{1}_{v}(\mathbb{R};X)
. In particular, there exists a constant
C
such that for every
f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}_{+};X)\cap L^{1}_{v}(\mathbb{R}_{+};X)
and all
\lambda>0

(7)

v\left(\{t>0:\|AUf(t)\|_{X}>\lambda\}\right)\leq C\lambda^{-1}\|f\|_{L^{1}_{v}(\mathbb{R}_{+};X)}.

First, we apply it to show that for all
f\in L^{1}_{loc}([0,\infty),v\mathrm{d}t;X)
,
Uf(t)\in D(A)
for a.e.
t>0
.
Suppose by contradiction that there exists
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that

B:=\{t\in[0,N]:Uf(t)\notin D(A)\}
has the positive Lebesgue’s measure.
Note that
U(f\chi_{[0,N]})=Uf
on
[0,N]
.
Let
f_{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, be a sequence of functions with compact supports from
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+};X)\cap L^{1}_{v}(\mathbb{R}_{+};X)
which approximate
f\chi_{[0,N]}
in
L^{1}_{v}(\mathbb{R}_{+};X)
. By (
7
), for every
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
and
\lambda>0
we have that

v\left(\{t>0:\|AUf_{n}(t)-AUf_{m}(t)\|_{X}>\lambda\}\right)\leq C\lambda^{-1}\|f_{n}-f_{m}\|_{L^{1}_{v}(\mathbb{R}_{+};X)}.

It gives an increasing sequence
(N_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathbb{N}
such that for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
:

v\left(B_{k}\right)\geq v([0,N])-2^{-k-1}v(B),

where

B_{k}:=\{t\in[0,N]:\|AUf_{N_{k+1}}(t)-AUf_{N_{k}}(t)\|_{X}\leq 2^{-k}\}.

In particular, for every
t\in\bigcap_{k\in\mathbb{N}}B_{k}
:

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\|AUf_{N_{k+1}}(t)-AUf_{N_{k}}(t)\|_{X}\leq 1.

Consequently, the sequence
AUf_{N_{k}}(t)
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, is convergent as
k\rightarrow\infty
.
Since
L^{1}_{v}\subset L^{1}_{loc}
, the sequence
Uf_{n}(t)
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, is convergent to
Uf(t)
for every
t>0
as
n\rightarrow\infty
. Therefore, the closedness of
A
shows that
Uf(t)\in D(A)
for all
t\in\bigcap_{k\in\mathbb{N}}B_{k}
. But
v(\bigcap_{k\in\mathbb{N}}B_{k})\geq v([0,N])-v(B)/2
, so we get a contradiction.
Therefore, for all
f\in L^{1}_{loc}([0,\infty),v\mathrm{d}t;X)
the function
AUf
is well-defined and, in addition, there exists a sequence of bounded, compactly supported functions
f_{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, such that
f_{n}
converges to
f
in
L^{1}_{loc}([0,\infty),v\mathrm{d}t;X)
, and
AUf_{n}
converges to
AUf
a.e. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.
In particular, for each
\tau>0
and
\epsilon>0
we get

v\left(\{t\in(0,\tau):\|AUf_{n}(t)-AUf(t)\|_{X}>\epsilon\}\right)\rightarrow 0\textrm{ as }n\rightarrow\infty.

Combining it with (
7
) for
f_{n}\chi_{[0,N]}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we easily get

v\left(\{t\in(0,\tau):\|AUf(t)\|_{X}>\lambda\}\right)\leq C\lambda^{-1}\|f\|_{L^{1}_{v}(0,\tau;X)}.

Additionally, it shows that in the case when
f\in L^{1}_{v}(\mathbb{R}_{+};X)
and
(f_{n})
is convergent to
f
in
L^{1}_{v}(\mathbb{R}_{+};X)
, then (
7
) holds for
f
too.

In general case, i.e. when
T
does not have the negative exponential growth bound, the above argument works for
A+\lambda
with
\lambda>0
large enough, which generates the semigroup
(e^{-\lambda t}T(t))_{t>0}
. Since
f\in L^{1}_{loc}([0,\infty),v\mathrm{d}t;X)
if and only if
e^{-\lambda(\cdot)}f\in L^{1}_{loc}([0,\infty),v\mathrm{d}t;X)
, and
e^{t\lambda}U_{A+\lambda}(e^{-\lambda(\cdot)}f)(t)=e^{-\lambda t}U_{A}f(t)
for all
t>0
, the proof is complete.
∎

Remark 2.7
.

Note that, in the conclusion of Proposition
2.6
, we do not claim that for
f\in L^{1}_{loc}([0,\infty);X)
the corresponding mild solution
Uf
is strongly differentiable almost everywhere on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

If, in addition to the assumptions stated there, we know that
A
is densely defined, then for every
f\in L^{1}_{loc}([0,\infty);X)
,
Uf
has strong right-side derivative a.e. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.
Indeed, it follows directly from the infinitesimal characterization of densely defined generators of semigroups, which gives that
Uf(t)\in D(A)
implies

\frac{1}{h}(T(h)Uf(t)-Uf(t))\rightarrow-AUf(t)
as
h\rightarrow 0\raisebox{0.95pt}{$+$}
.
Moreover, for
h,t>0
,

\frac{1}{h}\bigl(Uf(t+h)-Uf(t)\bigr)=\frac{1}{h}\bigl(T(h)Uf(t)-Uf(t)\bigr)+\frac{1}{h}\int_{t}^{t+h}T(t+h-s)f(s)\mathrm{d}s.

By the strong continuity of
T
at
0
, for each Lebesgue’s point
t
of
f
we get

(Uf)^{\prime}_{+}(t)=-AUf(t)+f(t)
.
We do not know if the
full
derivative of
Uf
exists a.e. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

2.3.
The characterization of weighted
L^{1}
-m.r.

The following result provides a weighted counterpart of the Kalton-Portal characterization of
L^{1}
-m.r. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
; see
[
37
, Theorem 3.6]
.

Theorem 2.8
.

Let
-A
be the generator of a bounded analytic semigroup
T
on
X
. Let
v
be a locally integrable weight function on
(0,\infty)
and such that
L^{1}_{v}\subset L^{1}_{loc}
.
Then, the following assertions are equivalent.

(i)

The operator
A
has
L^{1}_{v}
-m.r. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
, that is, for every
f\in L^{1}_{v}(\mathbb{R}_{+};X)
there exists a function
u\in W^{1,1}_{loc}([0,\infty);X)
such that
u(0)=0
,
u(t)\in D(A)
and
u^{\prime}(t)+Au(t)=f(t)
for a.e.
t>0
, and

\|u^{\prime}\|_{L^{1}_{v}(\mathbb{R}_{+};X)}+\|Au\|_{L^{1}_{v}(\mathbb{R}_{+};X)}\leq C\|f\|_{L^{1}_{v}(\mathbb{R}_{+};X)}

for some constant
C
independent on
f
.

(ii)

There exists a constant
C
such that for every Lebesgue’s point
s\in(0,\infty)
of
v
and every
x\in X
we have that

(8)

\int_{0}^{\infty}\|AT(t)x\|_{X}v(t+s)\mathrm{d}t\leq C\|x\|_{X}v(s).

A direct consequence of Theorem
2.8
is the following extrapolation result; cf. Proposition
2.6
.

Corollary 2.9
.

(i)
The
L^{1}
-m.r. implies
L^{1}_{v}
-m.r. for any non-increasing weight
v
on
(0,\infty)
.

(ii)
Suppose that
-A
is the generator of an analytic semigroup
T
on a Banach space
X
. Let
v
be a non-decreasing weight on
(0,\infty)
with
\lim_{t\rightarrow 0\raisebox{0.95pt}{$+$}}v(t)=0
. Then, if (
8
) holds, then
Ax=0
for all
x\in D(A)
.

Proof.

The first assertion
(i) follows easily via the characterization condition of Theorem
2.8
. For (ii), by the standard rescaling argument one can assume that
T
exponentially decreases. By (
8
) and the monotone convergence theorem we get

\int_{0}^{\infty}\|AT(t)x\|_{X}v(t)\mathrm{d}t\leq 0.

The assumption on
v
implies for every
\tau>0

0=\int_{0}^{\infty}\|AT(t)x\|_{X}v(t)\mathrm{d}t\geq v(\tau)\int_{\tau}^{\infty}\|AT(t)x\|_{X}\mathrm{d}t.

Hence, for all
x\in X
and
t>0
we get
AT(t)x=0
. In particular, for
x\in D(A)
,
R(\lambda,A)Ax=0
, or equivalently,
R(\lambda,A)x=\frac{1}{\lambda}x
, for all
\lambda>\omega(T)
, where
\omega(T)
stands for the exponential growth bound of
T
. Therefore, by the uniqueness theorem for the Laplace transform (see, e.g.
[
4
, Theorem 1.7.3, p.51]
),
T(t)x=x
for every
t>0
and
x\in D(A)
. It completes the proof of the corollary.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.8
.

For (i)
\Rightarrow
(ii), let
s>0
be a Lebesgue point of
v
. For any
\epsilon\in(0,1)
we set

u_{\epsilon,s}(t):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0,&t\in[0,s],\\
\min(\frac{t-s}{\epsilon},1)T(t-s)x,&t>s.\end{array}\right.

Note that
u_{\epsilon}(t)\in D(A)
for all
t\geq 0
and
u_{\epsilon}\in W^{1,1}_{loc}([0,\infty);X)
with

u_{\epsilon,s}^{\prime}(t)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}0,&t\in[0,s],\\
-\min(\frac{t-s}{\epsilon},1)AT(t-s)x+\frac{1}{\epsilon}T(t-s)x,&t\in(s,s+\epsilon],\\
-AT(t-s)x,&t>s+\epsilon.\end{array}\right.

Therefore,
u_{\epsilon,s}
is a solution of
(CP)_{A,f,0}
with

f_{\epsilon,s}(t):=\frac{1}{\epsilon}T(t-s)x\chi_{[s,s+\epsilon]}(t)
,
t>0
.
Note that

(9)

\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\|f_{\epsilon}(t)\|_{X}v(t)\mathrm{d}t=\sup_{t\in(0,1]}\|T(t)\|_{{\mathcal{L}}(X)}\|x\|_{X}\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{s}^{s+\epsilon}v(t)\mathrm{d}t.

On the other side we get

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\|Au_{\epsilon}(t)\|_{X}v(t)\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\geq\int_{s+\epsilon}^{\infty}\|AT(t-s)x\|_{X}v(t)\mathrm{d}t=

\displaystyle=\int_{\epsilon}^{\infty}\|AT(t)x\|_{X}v(t+s)\mathrm{d}t.

Therefore, letting with
\epsilon\rightarrow 0\raisebox{0.95pt}{$+$}
we get (
8
). It completes the proof of this implication.

For (ii)
\Rightarrow
(i), let
f\in L^{1}_{v}(\mathbb{R}_{+};X)\subset L^{1}_{loc}([0,\infty);X)
.
Note that the function
(0,\infty)^{2}\ni(t,s)\mapsto\chi_{\{s<t\}}(t,s)\|AT(t-s)f(s)\|_{X}
is measurable, therefore combining Fubini’s theorem with (
8
), we easily get

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\int_{0}^{t}\|AT(t-s)f(s)\|_{X}\mathrm{d}sv(t)\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\int_{s}^{\infty}\|AT(t-s)f(s)\|_{X}v(t)\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\|AT(t)f(s)\|_{X}v(t+s)\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\|f(s)\|_{X}v(s)\mathrm{d}s

In particular, the function
(0,t)\ni s\mapsto AT(t-s)f(s)\in X
is Bochner integrable for a.e.
t>0
. Consequently, by Hille’s theorem

Uf(t)=\int_{0}^{t}T(t-s)f(s)\mathrm{d}s\in D(A)
, and Bochner’s theorem gives

\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\left\|AUf(t)\right\|_{X}v(t)\mathrm{d}t\leq C\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\|f(t)\|_{X}v(t)\mathrm{d}t.

The remaining part of the statement (i) is a standard consequence of our assumption that
L^{1}_{v}\subset L^{1}_{loc}
, the above estimate and Lemma
2.2
(ii).
∎

Remark 2.10
.

Note that in (
9
) we use only the boundedness of
T
on
[0,1]
, but we apply the maximal regularity estimate in (i) for a family of functions supported on
[s,s+\epsilon]
, where
s
ranges over a.a. points of
\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Such
uniform
estimate requires the boundedness of
T
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
; see Remark
2.1
(a).

We conclude this subsection with a complementary result to Proposition
2.6
and Corollary
2.9
, which completes also
[
32
, Proposition 17.2.36]
. More precisely, the statement (1) of
[
32
, Proposition 17.2.36]
says that for
p\in(1,\infty)
(resp.
p=\infty
), the
L^{p}_{v}
-m.r. for the power weights
v(t)=t^{\mu}
,
\mu\in(-1,p-1)
(resp.
\mu\in(0,1)
) is equivalent to the (unweighted)
L^{p}
-m.r. The case
p=1
has been left open therein. Hereby, with the help of Theorem
2.8
, we provide the affirmative answer to it. In fact, our result identifies a more general class of weights
v
for which (weighted)
L_{v}^{1}
-m.r. is equivalent to (unweighted)
L^{1}
-m.r. More importantly, this result should be read in the context of a negative example presented in Theorem
3.1
(iii). Roughly, it says that, in the absence of the
L^{1}
-m.r. estimates, the weighted
L^{1}_{v}
-m.r. estimates do not hold for a large class of weights
v
as well.
We start with an auxiliary result.

Lemma 2.11
.

Let
v\colon(0,\infty)\to(0,\infty)
be such that
\log\circ v
is a convex function. Then, for every
t>0
the function
\frac{v(t+\cdot)}{v(\cdot)}
is increasing.

Proof.

Let
0<r<s
and
t>0
. Since
\log\circ v
is convex, we get

\displaystyle\log(v(r+t))+\log(v(s))

\displaystyle\leq\frac{s-r}{y-s+t}\log(v(r))+\frac{t}{s-r+t}\log(v(s+t))

\displaystyle+\frac{t}{s-r+t}\log(v(r))+\frac{s-r}{s-r+t}\log(v(s+t))

\displaystyle=\log(v(r))+\log(v(s+t)).

Therefore, by the monotonicity of an exponential function we get

v(r+t)\cdot v(s)\leq v(r)\cdot v(s+t).

and this yields

\frac{v(t+r)}{v(r)}\leq\frac{v(t+s)}{v(s)}.

This finishes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Proposition 2.12
.

Let
v
be a non-increasing function on
(0,\infty)
such that
\log\circ v
is convex and for some
\epsilon>0
and for a.e.
t>0

(10)

\lim_{s\to\infty}\frac{v(t+s)}{v(s)}\geq\epsilon.

Let
-A
be a generator of a bounded analytic semigroup on
X
.
Then,
A
has
L^{1}_{v}
-m.r. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
if and only if it has
L^{1}
-m.r. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
. In particular, the statement holds for
v(t)=t^{-\theta}
with
\theta\in(0,\infty)
.

Proof.

Since
v
is non-increasing, by Corollary
2.9
, the
L^{1}
-m.r. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
of
A
gives its
L^{1}_{v}
-m.r. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.
Suppose that
A
has
L^{1}_{v}
-m.r. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
. By the condition (
8
), there exist a constant
C>0
such that for all
x\in X
and
s>0

\int_{0}^{\infty}\|AT(t)x\|_{X}\frac{v(t+s)}{v(s)}\mathrm{d}t\leq C\|x\|_{X}.

By the Lemma
2.11
we get that for all
t>0
,
\lim_{s\to\infty}\frac{v(t+s)}{v(s)}\in(0,\infty]
exists, hence the function
\mathbb{R}_{+}\ni t\mapsto\lim_{s\to\infty}\frac{v(t+s)}{v(s)}
is measurable and Fatou’s lemma gives

\int_{0}^{\infty}\|AT(t)x\|_{X}\mathrm{d}t\leq\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{0}^{\infty}\|AT(t)x\|_{X}\lim_{s\to\infty}\frac{v(t+s)}{v(s)}\mathrm{d}t\leq\frac{C}{\epsilon}\|x\|_{X}.

Therefore, by the Kalton-Portal characterization,
A
has
L^{1}
-m.r. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.
∎

Remark 2.13
.

(a) In the Proposition
2.12
the assumption that
-A
generates a bounded analytic semigroup on
X
is not necessary in the case
\theta\in(0,1)
, since it follows from
L^{1}_{v}
-m.r. of
A
, as it was noted in the proof of
[
32
, Proposition 17.2.36]
.

It is worth mentioning that in Theorem
3.1
(vi) below, we show that the condition (
10
) is, in a sense, sharp for establishing the equivalence between
(unweighted)
L^{1}
-maximal regularity and (weighted)
L^{1}_{v}
-maximal regularity.

(b) In Theorem
3.1
(vi) below, we show that the condition (
10
) is, in a sense, sharp for establishing the equivalence between (unweighted)
L^{1}
-maximal regularity and (weighted)
L^{1}_{v}
-maximal regularity.

For the further references we conclude this subsection with the characterization of the
L^{\infty}
-m.r. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
due to Kalton and Portal
[
37
]
.

Lemma 2.14

(
[
37
, Theorem 3.7]
)
.

Let
-A
be the generator of a bounded analytic
C_{0}
-semigroup
T
on a Banach space
X
.
Then,
A
has
L^{\infty}
-m.r. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
if and only if there exists a constant
C>0
such that for every
x\in X

(11)

\|x\|_{X}\leq C\sup_{t>0}\|tAT(t)x\|_{X}+\limsup_{t\rightarrow\infty}\|T(t)x\|_{X}.

We point out that it can be shown that the condition (
11
) is sufficient for
L^{\infty}
-m.r. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}

even for non-densely defined generators of bounded analytic semigroups.

2.4.
The K-method

Let
(X,Y)
be an interpolation couple of Banach spaces and let
\Phi
be a Banach function space.
Recall first the definition of the
K
-functional
, that is, for every
x\in X+Y
and
t>0
put

K(t,x):=K(t,x,X,Y):=\inf\left\{\|a\|_{X}+t\|b\|_{Y}:a\in X,b\in Y,a+b=x\right\}.

With the help of the
K
-functional, we define the space

K_{\Phi}(X,Y):=\big\{x\in X+Y:\;[(0,\infty)\ni t\mapsto t^{-1}K(t,x)]\in\Phi\big\},

and equip it with the norm

\|x\|_{K_{\Phi}}:=\|x\|_{K_{\Phi}(X,Y)}:=\left\|(\cdot)^{-1}K(\cdot,x)\right\|_{\Phi}\,,\quad\quad x\in{K_{\Phi}(X,Y)}.

This definition of the functor
K_{\Phi}
differs very slightly from the one in
[
12
, Section 3.3]
, where
\Phi
runs through Banach function spaces containing the function
\min(1,\cdot)
, and
x\in K_{\Phi}(X,Y)
if and only if
K(\cdot,x)\in\Phi
. The point is only the factor
\frac{1}{t}
between the two definitions.
For instance, by
[
14
, Lemma 2.2]
,
K_{\Phi}
is an interpolation functor if and only if
\min(1,(\cdot)^{-1})\in\Phi
, in the other case,
K_{\Phi}(X,Y)=\{0\}
.

It is clear that for any
q\in[1,\infty)
and
\theta\in(0,1)
, if
\Phi:=L^{q}(\mathbb{R}_{+},t^{q(1-\theta)-1}\,\mathrm{d}t)
, then
K_{\Phi}(X,Y)
coincides with the classical interpolation space
(X,Y)_{\theta,q}
. For basic notation regarding interpolation theory and fundamental results, we refer the reader, e.g. to
[
12
,
40
,
53
]
.

Below we consider mainly two special classes of Banach function space
\Phi
, which correspond to the
L^{1}
-m.r. and
L^{\infty}
-m.r., that is, weighted Lebesgue spaces
L^{1}_{w}
and
L^{\infty}_{w}
, respectively. For instance, for
\Phi=L^{\infty}_{w}
the corresponding space
K_{\Phi}(X,Y)
can be considered as a generalized Marcinkiewicz space. More precisely, note that for the couple
(L^{1},L^{\infty})
, the space
K_{\Phi}(L^{1},L^{\infty})
is the Marcinkiewicz functional space
M_{w}
given by the norm

\|f\|_{K_{\Phi}(L^{1},L^{\infty})}\simeq\sup_{t>0}w(t){f^{**}(t)},

that is,
K(t,f)=tf^{**}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}f^{*}(s)\mathrm{d}s
. For
(L^{p},W^{k}_{p})
we have

K(t,f)\simeq\omega_{k}(f,t^{1/k})_{L^{p}},

where
\omega_{k}(f,s)_{L^{p}}:=\sup_{|h|\leq s}\|\delta^{k}_{h}f\|_{L^{p}}
.

The following result is complementary to our main result Theorems
3.1
and
3.3
, but may be of independent interest.
Roughly, it says that interpolation parameters
L^{1}_{w}
and
L^{\infty}_{w}
for general weights
w
are in fact equivalent to some parameters which are defined in terms of appropriately chosen concave functions. We start with the case of
L^{\infty}_{w}
, which is more transparent than that one of
L^{1}_{w}
.

Recall that a function
\phi:\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
is called
quasi-concave
, if
\phi
is non-decreasing and

\phi(t)\leq\max(1,t/s)\phi(s)\quad\textrm{ for all }s,t>0.

Recall that for any quasi-concave function
\phi
its least concave majorant
\tilde{\phi}
satisfies
\phi\leq\tilde{\phi}\leq 2\phi
; see e.g.
[
12
, Corollary 3.1.4]
.

Proposition 2.15
.

Let
(X,Y)
be a pair of Banach spaces such that
Y
is continuously embedded in
X
. Let
w
be a weight function on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Then, the following assertions hold.

(i)

There exists a quasi-concave function
\phi
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that
K_{L^{\infty}(w)}(X,Y)=K_{L^{\infty}(t/\phi(t))}(X,Y)
with equal norms, i.e. for every
x\in K_{L^{\infty}_{w}}(X,Y)

\|x\|_{K_{L^{\infty}_{w}}(X,Y)}=\sup_{t>0}\frac{K(t,x,X,Y)}{\phi(t)}.

In particular,
\|\cdot\|_{K_{L^{\infty}_{w}}(X,Y)}
is equivalent to the norm

\sup_{t>0}\frac{K(t,\cdot)}{\hat{\phi}(t)}
, where
\hat{\phi}
denotes the least concave majorant of
\phi
.

(ii)

There exists a concave function
\phi
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that

K_{L^{1}_{w}}(X,Y)=K_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathrm{d}(-\phi^{\prime}))}(X,Y)

with equivalent norms, i.e. for every
x\in K_{L^{1}_{w}}(X,Y)

\|x\|_{K_{L^{1}_{w}}(X,Y)}\simeq\int_{0}^{\infty}K(t,x,X,Y)\mathrm{d}(-\phi^{\prime}).

Here, the integral is the Riemann-Stieltjes integral.

Proof.

(i) We assume that
\min(1,(\cdot)^{-1})\in L^{\infty}_{w}
, since in the other case
K_{L^{\infty}_{w}}(X,Y)=\{0\}
and the proof is trivial.

Let
x\in K_{L^{\infty}_{w}}(X,Y)
and
x\neq 0
, i.e.
for all
s>0
we have that

0<K(s,x)\leq\frac{s}{w(s)}\|x\|_{K_{L^{\infty}_{w}}}

Since
K(\cdot,x)
is concave we further get

K(t,x)=\inf_{s>0}\max(1,t/s)K(s,x)\leq\inf_{s>0}\max(1,t/s)\frac{s}{w(s)}\|x\|_{K_{L^{\infty}_{w}}}.

Set

\phi(t):=\inf_{s>0}\max(1,t/s)\frac{s}{w(s)},\quad\,t>0.

Note that
\phi
is quasi-concave and for
\phi_{*}(t):=\frac{t}{\phi(t)}
for all
t>0
:

\|x\|_{K_{L^{\infty}_{\phi_{*}}}}:=\sup_{t>0}\frac{1}{t}K(t,x)\phi_{*}(t)\leq\|x\|_{K_{L^{\infty}_{w}}}.

Conversely, since
\phi(t)\leq t/w(t)
, for any
x\in X
and
t>0
we get

\frac{1}{t}K(t,x)w(t)\leq\frac{1}{t}K(t,x)\phi_{*}(t).

It shows that for
x\in K_{L^{\infty}_{\phi_{*}}}(X,Y)
,
\|x\|_{K_{L^{\infty}_{w}}}\leq\|x\|_{K_{L^{\infty}_{\phi_{*}}}}
.
Therefore, the proof of (i) is complete.

For (ii), we can assume that
\min(1,(\cdot)^{-1})\in L^{1}_{w}
, since in the other case
K_{L^{1}_{w}}(X,Y)=\{0\}
and the proof is trivial.
Let
\mathrm{d}\mu:=\frac{w(t)}{t}\mathrm{d}t
.
Recall that any concave function
\phi
has the Riemann-Stieltjes integral representation

\phi(t)=-\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\min(s,t)\mathrm{d}\phi^{\prime}(s),\qquad t>0.

In particular, for any
x\in X
there exists a Borel measure
\nu_{x}
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that

K(t,x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\min(s,t)\mathrm{d}\nu_{x}(s),\qquad t>0.

Therefore, if
x\in K_{L^{1}_{w}}(X,Y)
, then by Tonelli’s theorem we get that

\displaystyle\|x\|_{L^{1}_{w}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\min(s,t)\mathrm{d}\nu_{x}(s)\mathrm{d}\mu(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\min(s,t)\mathrm{d}\mu(t)\mathrm{d}\nu_{x}(s).

Note that the function
\psi(s):=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\min(s,t)\mathrm{d}\mu(t)
,
s>0
, is quasi-concave and finite for all
s>0
. Let
\phi
be the least concave majorant of
\psi
. In particular, if
\nu_{\phi}
stands for the measure on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
generated by
-\phi^{\prime}
, then we get

\displaystyle\|x\|_{L^{1}_{w}}

\displaystyle\simeq\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\min(s,t)\mathrm{d}\nu_{\phi}(t)\mathrm{d}\nu_{x}(s)=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}K(t,x)\mathrm{d}\nu_{\phi}(t).

It completes the proof.
∎

Remark 2.16
.

Note that the concave function
\phi
in (ii) of Proposition
2.15
is the least concave majorant of the function
\phi_{\mu}(\cdot,1)
, where
\phi_{\mu}(\cdot,\cdot)
is a
fundamental function of the functor

K_{\Phi}
with
\Phi:=L^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathrm{d}\mu)
;
see, e.g.
[
12
, p. 145]
for the corresponding definition.

Indeed, recall that the function
\phi_{\mu}
is defined by the condition
K_{\Phi}(t\mathbb{R},s\mathbb{R})=\phi_{\mu}(t,s)\mathbb{R}
for all
s,t>0
, that is, for all
r\in\mathbb{R}
we have that
\|r\|_{K_{\Phi}(t\mathbb{R},s\mathbb{R})}=\phi_{\mu}(t,s)|r|
, where
|r|
stands for the modulus of
r
.
Since for all
r\in\mathbb{R}
and
h>0
,
K(h,r,t\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R})=\min(t,h)|r|
, we obtain that

\|r\|_{K_{\Phi}(t\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R})}=|r|\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\frac{1}{h}\min(t,h)\mathrm{d}\mu(h).

Therefore, it yields
\phi_{\mu}(t,1)=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}{\frac{1}{s}}\min(t,s)\mathrm{d}\mu(s)
,
t>0
, as we claimed.

2.5.
The Hardy operator

Let
P
denote the
Hardy operator
and
Q
be its (formal) adjoint, that is, the integral operators given by

Pf(t):=\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}f(s)\,\mathrm{d}s,\quad\quad Qf(t):=\int_{t}^{\infty}f(s)\,\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s}

for all
f\in{\mathcal{M}}
such that the respective integrals exist for all
t\in(0,\infty)
. The maximal domain of
P
,
D_{max}(P)
, is hence the space
L^{1}_{loc}:=L^{1}_{loc}([0,\infty))
, while the maximal domain of
Q
,
D_{max}(Q)
, is the set of all measurable functions
f\in{\mathcal{M}}
such that
f\chi_{(\tau,\infty)}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+},t^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}t)
for every
\tau>0
.

Note that if
\Phi
is a Banach function space such that the Hardy operator
P
is bounded on
\Phi
, then
\Phi\subseteq L_{loc}^{1}
, and
\chi_{(0,1)}\in\Phi
if and only if
\min(1,\frac{1}{(\cdot)})\in\Phi
. According to
[
14
, Lemma 2.2]
, in this case, if
\chi_{(0,1)}\notin\Phi
, then
K_{\Phi}(X,Y)=\{0\}
.

The following characterization of the boundedness of the Hardy operator
P
and its adjoint
Q
on weighted
L^{1}
and
L^{\infty}
spaces is essentially stated in
[
42
]
; see also references therein.

Since, in
[
42
, Theorems 1 and 2]
, the weight functions are finite almost everywhere, the characterization conditions provided there, according to the convention established after
[
42
, Theorem 1]
, can be reformulated as follows: for any weight function
w\colon(0,\infty)\to[0,\infty]
, the operator
P
(resp.
Q
) is
bounded
on
L^{1}_{w}
if and only if

(12)

\sup_{t>0}\left[\textrm{ess\,sup}_{0<r\leq t}\frac{1}{w(r)}\int_{t}^{\infty}\frac{w(s)}{s}\mathrm{d}s\right]<\infty

(resp.

(13)

\sup_{t>0}\left[\textrm{ess\,sup}_{r\geq t}\frac{1}{rw(r)}\int_{0}^{t}w(s)\mathrm{d}s\right]<\infty).

(Similar statement in the case of
L^{\infty}
estimates can be extracted from
[
42
, Theorem 3]
).
Since we are working with weight functions with values in
(0,\infty)
and we are rather interested in the mapping properties of
P
and
Q
,
i.e.
PL^{1}_{w},QL^{1}_{w}\subset L^{1}_{w}
, the expression

\textrm{ess\,sup}_{0<r\leq t}\frac{1}{w(r)}
(resp.
\textrm{ess\,sup}_{r\geq t}\frac{1}{rw(r)}
) can be replaced with
\frac{1}{w(t)}
(resp.
\frac{1}{tw(t)}
). It can be read from the proof provided in
[
42
]
. For the convenience of the reader we provide a direct proof here.

Lemma 2.17

(
[
42
, Theorems 1 and 2]
)
.

Let
w
be a weight function on
(0,\infty)
. Then, the following statements are true:

(i)

The operator
P

(
resp.
Q
)
is bounded on
L^{1}_{w}
if and only if

(14)

[w]_{P,L^{1}}:=\mathrm{ess\,sup}_{t>0}\frac{1}{w(t)}\int_{t}^{\infty}\frac{w(s)}{s}\mathrm{d}s<\infty

\Big(
resp.

(15)

[w]_{Q,L^{1}}:=\mathrm{ess\,sup}_{t>0}\frac{1}{tw(t)}\int_{0}^{t}w(s)\mathrm{d}s<\infty\Big).

(ii)

The operator
P

(
resp.
Q
)
is bounded on
L^{\infty}_{w}
if and only if

(16)

[w]_{P,L^{\infty}}:=\mathrm{ess\,sup}_{t>0}\frac{w(t)}{t}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{w(s)}\mathrm{d}s<\infty

\Big(
resp.

(17)

[w]_{Q,L^{\infty}}:=\mathrm{ess\,sup}_{t>0}w(t)\int_{t}^{\infty}\frac{1}{sw(s)}\mathrm{d}s<\infty\Big).

Proof.

(i) For a weight function
w
let
\Phi:=L^{1}_{w}
.
Note that the Köthe dual
\Phi^{\prime}
of
\Phi
(
\Phi
is considered as a Banach function space over
(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathrm{d}t)
) is
L^{\infty}(w^{-1})
.

First, note that the condition
[w]_{P,L^{1}}<\infty
, implies that
\Phi\subset L^{1}_{loc}=D_{max}(P)
. Indeed, let
f\in\Phi
, then for each
\tau>0
, since by our assumption
w(s)>0
for all
s>\tau
, we can write

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\tau}|f(t)|\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq[w]_{P,L^{1}}\int_{0}^{\tau}\left(\int_{t}^{\infty}\frac{w(s)}{s}\mathrm{d}s\right)^{-1}|f(t)|w(t)\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq[w]_{P,L^{1}}\left(\int_{\tau}^{\infty}\frac{w(s)}{s}\mathrm{d}s\right)^{-1}\int_{0}^{\tau}|f(t)|w(t)\mathrm{d}t<\infty.

Now, for all
g\in\Phi^{\prime}
with
\|g\|_{\Phi^{\prime}}=1
, i.e.
|g(s)|\leq w(s)
for a.e.
s>0
, and for every
f\in\Phi
, since
(0,\infty)^{2}\ni(t,s)\mapsto\frac{|f(s)g(t)|}{t}\chi_{(0,t)(s)}
is measurable, by Tonelli’s theorem, we get

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}|Pf(t)g(t)|\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{|f(s)|}{t}\chi_{(0,t)}(s)\mathrm{d}s|g(t)|\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{s}^{\infty}\frac{|g(t)|}{t}\mathrm{d}t|f(s)|\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{s}^{\infty}\frac{w(t)}{t}\mathrm{d}t|f(s)|\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq[w]_{P,L^{1}}\int_{0}^{\infty}|f(s)|w(s)\mathrm{d}s.

By the Lorentz-Luxemburg theorem,
(\Phi^{\prime})^{\prime}=\Phi
, therefore,
\|Pf\|_{\Phi}\leq[w]_{P,L^{1}}\|f\|_{\Phi}
for all
f\in\Phi
.

To prove the statement for
Q
, first note that
[w]_{Q,L^{1}}
yields
\Phi\subset D_{max}(Q)
. Indeed, for
\tau>0
, since
0<w(s)
for all
s\in(0,\tau)
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\int_{\tau}^{\infty}|f(t)|\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}

\displaystyle\leq[w]_{Q,L^{1}}\int_{\tau}^{\infty}\left(\int_{0}^{t}w(s)\mathrm{d}s\right)^{-1}|f(t)|w(t)\mathrm{d}t<\infty.

Similarly as above, for any
g\in\Phi^{\prime}
with
\|g\|_{\Phi}\leq 1
and any
f\in\Phi
, by Tonelli’s theorem, we get

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}|Qf(t)g(t)|\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{|f(s)|}{s}\chi_{(t,\infty)}(s)\mathrm{d}s|g(t)|\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{s}\int_{s}^{\infty}|g(t)|\mathrm{d}t|f(s)|\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{s}\int_{s}^{\infty}w(t)\mathrm{d}t|f(s)|\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq[w]_{Q,L^{1}}\int_{0}^{\infty}|f(s)|w(s)\mathrm{d}s.

Again, the Lorentz-Luxemburg theorem gives the desired boundedness of
Q
.

For the converse implication, recall that by
[
42
, Theorem 1 and 2]
, the boundedness of
P
and
Q
on
L^{1}_{w}
implies respectively the conditions (
12
) and (
13
) (even for any measurable function
w
on
(0,\infty)
with values in
[0,\infty]
). Since those conditions imply obviously the corresponding ones stated in (i), and since in the sequel we do not use this converse implication in our proofs, its direct proof is omitted here. The proof of (i) is complete.

For the proof of (ii) note that the boundedness of
P
(respectively,
Q
) on
L^{1}_{w}
is equivalent to the boundedness of
Q
(respectively,
P
) on
L^{\infty}(w^{-1})
. Therefore, (ii) follows form (i). It completes the proof.
∎

The following consequence of Lemma
2.17
provides a simple (in its form) condition that characterizes weight functions for which the sum operator
P+Q
(called the Calderón operator) is bounded on weighted
L^{1}
(or
L^{\infty}
) spaces. We formulate in terms of these conditions our main results stated in Section
3
.

Lemma 2.18
.

Let
w
be a weight function on
(0,\infty)
.
Then the following assertions hold.

(i)

The operators
P
and
Q
are bounded on
L^{1}_{w}
if and only if

(18)

[w]_{P+Q,L^{1}}:=\mathrm{ess\,sup}_{t>0}\frac{1}{w(t)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{w(s)}{t+s}\mathrm{d}s<\infty.

(ii)

The operators
P
and
Q
are bounded on
L^{\infty}_{w}
if and only if
P
and
Q
are bounded on
L^{1}_{w^{-1}}
if and only if

(19)

[w]_{P+Q,L^{\infty}}:=\mathrm{ess\,sup}_{t>0}{w(t)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{t+s}\frac{1}{w(s)}\mathrm{d}s<\infty.

Proof.

Note that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{w(t)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{t+s}w(s)\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{w(t)}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{t+s}w(s)\mathrm{d}s+\frac{1}{w(t)}\int_{t}^{\infty}\frac{1}{t+s}w(s)\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{tw(t)}\int_{0}^{t}w(s)\mathrm{d}s+\frac{1}{w(t)}\int_{t}^{\infty}\frac{w(s)}{s}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq[w]_{P,L^{1}}+[w]_{Q,L^{1}}

On the other hand, since for every
t>0
,
\inf_{s\geq t}\frac{s}{s+t}=\inf_{0<s\leq t}\frac{t}{t+s}=\frac{1}{2}
, we easily get

\displaystyle\frac{1}{w(t)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{t+s}w(s)\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{w(t)}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{t+s}w(s)\mathrm{d}s+\frac{1}{w(t)}\int_{t}^{\infty}\frac{1}{t+s}w(s)\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{tw(t)}\int_{0}^{t}w(s)\mathrm{d}s+\frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{w(t)}\int_{t}^{\infty}\frac{w(s)}{s}\mathrm{d}s

Therefore,
2[w]_{P+Q,L^{1}}\geq[w]_{P,L^{1}}+[w]_{Q,L^{1}}
Lemma
2.17
(i) completes the proof of (i).
The proof of (ii) follows exactly the same argument.
∎

Remark 2.19
.

(a) Recall that the function
\mathcal{S}w(t)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{w(s)}{t+s}\mathrm{d}s
,
t>0
, is called the
Stieltjes transform
of the function
w
. Therefore, the statement (i) of the above result can be rephrased as follows: the Calderón operator
P+Q
is bounded on
L^{1}_{w}
if and only if Stieltjes’ transform of
w
is up to a constant less than
w
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
. It is readily seen that for non-negative
f
,
{\mathcal{S}}f\leq Pf+Qf\leq 2{\mathcal{S}}f
a.e. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

The following corollary shows that for the power weights
w_{\mu}(t):=t^{-\mu}

(t>0)
with
\mu\in(0,1)
, the quantity
[w]_{P+Q,L^{1}}
can be computed exactly.

Corollary 2.20
.

Let
w_{\mu}(t):=t^{-\mu}

(t>0)
with
\mu\in(0,1)
. Then,
[w]_{P+Q,L^{1}}=\frac{\pi}{\sin(\pi\mu)}
and, consequently,
P+Q
is bounded on
L^{1}_{w_{\mu}}
and on
L^{\infty}(w_{\mu}^{-1})
.

Proof.

Let
B
stand for the beta function. Then, the statement follows from the following

\displaystyle t^{\mu}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{s^{\mu}(t+s)}ds

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{s^{-\mu}}{1+s}ds=B(1-\mu,\mu)=\frac{\pi}{\sin(\pi\mu)}

and Lemma
2.18
.
∎

We conclude this subsection with an extension of Proposition
2.15
(ii). Roughly, we show that under an additional assumption on the parameter
\Phi=L^{1}_{w}
the corresponding interpolation functor
K_{\Phi}
admits a further equivalent description, which simplifies that given in Proposition
2.15
(ii). We start with necessary preparatory results on the
J
-method and refer the reader to
[
9
]
or
[
12
]
for the background.

Recall that if
\rho
is a quasi-concave function and
\vec{X}=(X_{0},X_{1})
is a Banach couple, then

J_{\rho,1}(\vec{X})
consists of all
x\in X_{0}+X_{1}
which can be represented by

\displaystyle x=\int_{0}^{\infty}u(t)\frac{dt}{t}\quad\text{convergence in \, $X_{0}+X_{1}$}\,,

where
u\colon(0,\infty)\to X_{0}+X_{1}
is strongly measurable with values in
X_{0}\cap X_{1}
and

\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{J(t,u(t):\vec{X})}{\rho(t)}\frac{dt}{t}<\infty\,.

Let
J_{\rho,1}(\vec{X})
be equipped with the norm

\|x\|_{\rho,1}=\inf\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{J(t,u(t);\vec{X})}{\rho(t)}\frac{dt}{t}\,,

where the infimum is taken over all
u
such that
x=\int_{0}^{\infty}u(t)\frac{dt}{t}
with the mentioned properties. Then,
J_{\rho,1}
is an exact interpolation functor.
Here,
J\colon(0,\infty)\times(X_{0}\cap X_{1})\to[0,\infty)
is the so called the
J
-functional defined by

J(t,x;\vec{X}):=\max(\|x\|_{X_{0}},\|x\|_{X_{1}}),\quad\,t>0,\,\,x\in X_{0}\cap X_{1}\,.

In what follows we will use the well-known and easily verified maximal property which states that for any exact
interpolation functor
F
with characteristic function
\varphi
the following continuous inclusion holds
for any Banach couple
\vec{X}
with norm less than or equal to
1

J_{\rho,1}(\vec{X})\hookrightarrow F(\vec{X})\,,

where
\rho(t):=\varphi_{*}(1,t)
and
\varphi_{*}(s,t):=1/\varphi(1/s,1/t)
for all
s,t>0
.

Recall also that the
lower
and
upper dilation indices
of a quasi-concave function
\phi\colon\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}

are given by

\alpha_{\phi}:=\lim_{t\to 0\raisebox{0.95pt}{$+$}}\frac{\ln s_{\phi}(t)}{\ln t},\quad\,\beta_{\phi}:=\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{\ln s_{\phi}(t)}{\ln t},

respectively, where
s_{\phi}(t):=\sup_{r>0}\frac{\phi(rt)}{\phi(r)}
for all
t>0
. Clearly,
0\leq\alpha_{\phi}\leq\beta_{\phi}\leq 1
. A quasi-concave function
\varphi
is said to be
quasi-power
if
0<\alpha_{\phi}\leq\beta_{\phi}<1
.

To proceed, we require the following auxiliary result, which characterizes the nontriviality of the dilation indices of quasi-concave functions
through integral conditions. While the fact that the nontriviality of the indices implies integral estimates is well known, we include a proof
for the sake of completeness.

Lemma 2.21
.

For any quasi-concave function
\phi\colon\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow~\mathbb{R}_{+}
the following statements hold:

(i)

The estimate
\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\phi(s)}{s}\,\mathrm{d}s\lesssim\phi(t)
for all
t>0
is equivalent to
\alpha_{\phi}>0
.

(ii)

The estimate
\int_{t}^{\infty}\frac{\phi(s)}{s^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}s\lesssim\frac{\phi(t)}{t}
for all
t>0
is equivalent to

\beta_{\phi}<1
.

Proof.

(i) Suppose, for the sake of contradiction, that

\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\phi(s)}{s}\,\mathrm{d}s\lesssim\phi(t),\quad t>0\,,

and
\alpha_{\phi}=0
. Since

\alpha_{\phi}=\sup_{0<t<1}\frac{\ln s_{\phi}(t)}{\ln t}\,,

it follows that for all
t\in(0,1)
, one has
s_{\phi}(t)=1
. Consequently, for each positive integer
n
,
there exists a
r_{n}>0
such that

\frac{\phi(r_{n}/n)}{\phi(r_{n})}\geq\frac{1}{2}\,.

This leads to the estimate

\sup_{t>0}\frac{1}{\phi(t)}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\phi(s)}{s}\,\mathrm{d}s\geq\frac{\phi(r_{n}/n)}{\phi(r_{n})}\int_{r_{n}/n}^{r_{n}}\frac{\phi(s)}{s\,\phi(r_{n}/n)}\,\mathrm{d}s\geq\frac{1}{2}\log n

for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, which is a contradiction. Therefore,
\alpha_{\phi}>0
.

For the reverse implication, assume that

\alpha_{\phi}>0
. By the definition, for any
\varepsilon\in(0,\alpha_{\phi})
there exists a constant
K=K(\varepsilon)>0
such that

s_{\phi}(t)\leq Kt^{\alpha_{\phi}-\varepsilon},\quad\,t\in(0,1]\,.

This implies that

C_{\phi}:=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{s_{\phi}(r)}{r}\,\mathrm{d}r\leq\frac{K}{\alpha_{\phi}-\varepsilon}<\infty\,.

Hence,

\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\phi(s)}{s}\,\mathrm{d}s=\bigg(\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\phi(tr)}{r\,\phi(t)}\,\mathrm{d}r\bigg)\phi(t)\leq C_{\phi}\phi(t),\quad t>0.

This completes the proof of (i).

(ii) Let
\psi(t):=t\phi(1/t)
for all
t>0
. A change of variable shows that the estimate

\int_{t}^{\infty}\frac{\phi(s)}{s^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}s\lesssim\frac{\phi(t)}{t}
for all
t>0
is equivalent to the estimate

\int_{0}^{1/t}\frac{\psi(s)}{s}\,\mathrm{d}s\lesssim\psi(1/t)
for all
t>0
, and consequently to

\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\psi(s)}{s}\,\mathrm{d}s\lesssim\psi(t),\quad\,t>0\,.

Clearly,
\psi
is a quasi-concave function. Thus, by (i), this estimate is equivalent to
\alpha_{\psi}>0
.
Since
s_{\psi}(t)=ts_{\phi}(1/t)
for all
t>0
, it follows that
\alpha_{\psi}=1-\beta_{\phi}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Proposition 2.22
.

Let
\Phi:=L^{1}_{w}
be a parameter of the
K
-method such that operators
P
and
Q
are bounded on
\Phi
. Let
\Psi:=L^{1}_{v}
, where
v(t):=\varphi(1,1/t)
for all
t>0
and

\varphi
is the characteristic function of the functor
K_{\Phi}
.

Then, the following statements are true
:

(i)

For any Banach couple
\vec{X}=(X_{0},X_{1})
,

K_{\Phi}(\vec{X})\simeq K_{\Psi}(\vec{X})\,,

up to equivalence of norms depending only on
[w]_{P,L_{1}}
and
[w]_{Q,L_{1}}

(ii)

The operators
P
and
Q
are bounded on
L^{1}_{v}
.

(iii)

The function
\phi=\varphi(\cdot,1)
is quasi-power.

Proof.

(i) Note that
\varphi(1,1/t)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\min\big(1,\frac{s}{t}\big)\frac{w(s)}{s}\,\mathrm{d}s
for all
t>0
. Thus, by Lemma
2.18
, the boundedness of
P
and
Q
on
L^{1}_{w}
yields that for almost all
t>0
one has

v(t)=\varphi(1,1/t)=\frac{1}{t}\,\int_{0}^{t}w(s)\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{t}^{\infty}\frac{w(s)}{s}\,\mathrm{d}s\leq Cw(t)

with
C:=[w]_{P,L^{1}}+[w]_{Q,L^{1}}
. Consequently,
K_{\Phi}(\vec{X})\hookrightarrow K_{\Psi}(\vec{X})

with the norm of the inclusion map less than or equal to
C
.

For the inverse inclusion, let
\rho(t):=1/\varphi(1,1/t)
for all
t>0
.
Then, based on the aforementioned maximal property, we have
J_{\rho,1}(\vec{X})\hookrightarrow K_{\Phi}(\vec{X})\,.
Thus, it is enough to show that

K_{\Psi}(\vec{X})\hookrightarrow J_{\rho,1}(\vec{X})

with the norm of the inclusion map independent of
\vec{X}
. To see this fix
x\in K_{\Psi}(\vec{X})
. Note that from the quasi-concavity of

\varphi(1,\cdot)
, we have
\min(1,1/t)\varphi(1,1)\leq\varphi(1,1/t)
for all
t>0
, and hence

L^{1}(v(t)/t)\hookrightarrow L^{1}\big(\min(t^{-1},t^{-2}))\,.

This implies
\min(1,1/t)K(t,x;\vec{X})\to 0
as
t\to 0
or as
t\to\infty
. Thus, applying the fundamental lemma of interpolation theory,
we conclude the existence of a representation
x=\int_{0}^{\infty}u(t)\frac{dt}{t}
(convergence in
X_{0}+X_{1}
) with

u\colon(0,\infty)\to X_{0}+X_{1}
strongly measurable with values in
X_{0}\cap X_{1}
, such that

J(t,u(t);\vec{X})\leq\gamma K(t,x;\vec{X}),\quad\,t>0\,,

where
\gamma
is a universal constant; see
[
9
, Lemma 3.3.2]
with
\gamma<4
.
In consequence,
x\in J_{\rho,1}(\vec{X})
with
|x|_{\rho,1}\leq\gamma|x|_{K_{\Psi}(\vec{X})}
, and this completes the proof of(i).

(ii) Taking

\vec{X}:=(t\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R})
in (i), we conclude that

\int_{0}^{\infty}s^{-1}\min(t,s)\varphi(1,1/s)\,ds\leq C\varphi(t,1),\quad\,t>0\,.

Hence, by Lemma
2.18
(i),

(20)

\int_{0}^{t}v(s)\mathrm{d}s\leq Ct\,v(t),\quad\,\int_{t}^{\infty}\frac{v(s)}{s}\,\mathrm{d}s\leq C\,v(t),\quad\,t>0

and this proves the statement (ii).

(iii) Clearly, the inequalities (
20
) are equivalent to

\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\phi(s)}{s}\mathrm{d}s\leq C\phi(t),\quad\,\int_{t}^{\infty}\frac{\phi(s)}{s^{2}}\mathrm{d}s\leq C\frac{\phi(t)}{t},\quad\,t>0\,.

Therefore, Lemma
2.21
completes the proof.
∎

2.6.
The representation of interpolation spaces
K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})

The next preparatory result, Proposition
2.23
, is well-known for the parameters
\Phi
corresponding to the classical interpolation functors
(\,\cdot\,)_{\theta,q}
with
\theta\in(0,1)
and
q\in[1,\infty]
; see e.g.
[
30
, Chapter 6.2]
. For a more general class of
\Phi
it can be extracted as a special case of
[
15
, Theorem 3.1]
; see also
[
15
, Remarks 3.2 and 3.3]
. For the reader’s convenience, we give a direct, self-contained, alternative proof based on Lemma
2.3
.

Proposition 2.23
.

Let
-{\mathcal{A}}
be a generator of a bounded analytic semigroup
{\mathcal{T}}
on a Banach space
{\mathcal{X}}
. Let
\Phi
be a Banach function space over
\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that
\min(1,\frac{1}{(\cdot)})\in\Phi
and the Hardy operator
P
is bounded on
\Phi
.

Then,

K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})=\left\{x\in{\mathcal{X}}:\left[(0,\infty)\ni t\mapsto\|{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{T}}(t)x\|_{{\mathcal{X}}}\right]\in\Phi\right\}

and

(21)

\|x\|_{K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})}\simeq\|x\|_{\mathcal{X}}+\|{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{T}}(\cdot)x\|_{\Phi({\mathcal{X}})},\qquad x\in K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}}).

In particular, if, in addition,
{\mathcal{A}}
is invertible, i.e.
0\in\rho({\mathcal{A}})
, then

\|x\|_{K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})}\simeq\|{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{T}}(\cdot)x\|_{\Phi({\mathcal{X}})},\qquad x\in K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}}).

Moreover, the part of
{\mathcal{A}}
in
K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})
is again the generator of a bounded analytic semigroup
T
on
K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})
, which is the restriction of
{\mathcal{T}}
to
K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})
.

Proof.

First note that for every
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
and
t>0

(22)

\|{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{T}}(t)x\|_{{\mathcal{X}}}\lesssim\frac{1}{t}K(t,x)\leq\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}\|{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{T}}(s)x\|_{{\mathcal{X}}}\mathrm{d}s+\|{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{T}}(t)x\|_{{\mathcal{X}}}+\|{\mathcal{T}}(t)x\|_{{\mathcal{X}}}.

In fact, for the first inequality note that for each
x=y+z\in{\mathcal{X}}
with
y\in{\mathcal{X}}
and
z\in D_{\mathcal{A}}
we have that

t\|{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{T}}(t)x\|_{{\mathcal{X}}}\leq\sup_{s>0}\|s{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{T}}(s)\|_{{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{X}})}\|y\|_{\mathcal{X}}+\sup_{s>0}\|{\mathcal{T}}(s)\|_{{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{X}})}\,t\|{\mathcal{A}}z\|_{\mathcal{X}}.

For the second one, by Lemma
2.3
, for every
t>0
and
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
,
x-{\mathcal{T}}(t)x={\mathcal{A}}\int_{0}^{t}{\mathcal{T}}(s)x\mathrm{d}s\in{\mathcal{X}}
and
{\mathcal{T}}(t)x\in D_{\mathcal{A}}
.

Moreover, it is easily seen that for all
t>0
and
x\in{\mathcal{X}}

\min(1,{t^{-1}})\|x\|_{\mathcal{X}}\leq t^{-1}K(t,x)\leq t^{-1}\|x\|_{{\mathcal{X}}}.

Combining this estimate with (
22
), the uniform boundedness of
({\mathcal{T}}(t))_{t\geq 0}
on
{\mathcal{X}}
and
P
on
\Phi
, we get (
21
).

Furthermore, if
{\mathcal{A}}
is invertible, then
\|{\mathcal{T}}(t)x\|_{\mathcal{X}}\leq\|{\mathcal{A}}^{-1}\|_{{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{X}})}\|{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{T}}(t)x\|_{{\mathcal{X}}}
for all
t>0
. By (
22
), it proves the second claim. The last one is a direct consequence of (
21
) and the well-known characterization of generators of analytic semigroups; see e.g.
[
4
, Theorem 3.7.11]
.
∎

Note that the expression

(23)

[x]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}:=\|{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{T}}(\cdot)x\|_{\Phi({\mathcal{X}})},\qquad x\in K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}}),

is a seminorm on
K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})
, and in the case when
{\mathcal{A}}
is injective, it gives a norm, which is equivalent to
\|\cdot\|_{K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})}
if
0\in\rho({\mathcal{A}})
. We refer to
[\cdot]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}
as the
homogenous part
of the norm
\|\cdot\|_{K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})}
.

2.7.
Equivalent representation of homogenous part of interpolation norms

Note that
[
15
, Theorems 3.1 and 3.4]
provides further equivalent representations of the interpolation spaces
K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})
and its norms.
A close analysis of the proof of those results in fact shows that the homogeneous part (
23
) admits equivalent expressions, which we discuss in this subsection.

More precisely, if
\psi\neq 0
is a holomorphic function on a sector
S_{\omega}:=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:z\neq 0,|\arg z|<\omega\}
, with
\omega>\omega_{\mathcal{A}}
(the sectoriality angle of
{\mathcal{A}}
) such that one of the following conditions holds:

(A)

\psi
,
(\cdot)^{-1}\psi\in{\mathcal{E}}(S_{\omega})
,
\lim_{z\rightarrow 0}z^{-1}\psi(z)\neq 0
,
\sup_{z\in S_{\omega},s\geq 1}\left|\frac{\psi(sz)}{s\psi(z)}\right|<\infty
, and

P
is bounded on
\Phi
(
[
15
, Theorem 3.1]
),

(B)

\psi
,
(\cdot)^{-1}\psi\in{\mathcal{E}}(S_{\omega})
, and
P
and
Q
are bounded on
\Phi
(
[
15
, Theorem 3.4]
),

then

K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})=\{x\in{\mathcal{X}}:\left[(0,\infty)\ni t\mapsto t^{-1}\|\psi(t{\mathcal{A}})x\|_{\mathcal{X}}\right]\in\Phi\}

(24)

[x]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}:=\|{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{T}}(\cdot)x\|_{\Phi({\mathcal{X}})}\simeq\|(\cdot)^{-1}\psi(\cdot{\mathcal{A}})x\|_{\Phi({\mathcal{X}})}\,,\qquad x\in{\mathcal{X}}.

Here,
{\mathcal{E}}(S_{\omega})
denotes the subalgebra of the space
H^{\infty}(S_{\omega})
of bounded holomorphic functions on
S_{\omega}
, which is given by
{\mathcal{E}}(S_{\omega}):=H^{\infty}_{0}(S_{\omega})\oplus\langle(1+z)^{-1}\rangle\oplus\langle 1\rangle
, where

H^{\infty}_{0}(S_{\omega}):=\{f\in H^{\infty}(S_{\omega}):\exists C,\alpha>0\textrm{ s.t. }|f(z)|\leq C\min(|z|^{\alpha},|z|^{-\alpha})(z\in S_{\omega})\}.

For instance, each of the following functions satisfies the above condition (A):

(25)

\psi_{1}(z)=e^{-z}-1,\qquad\psi_{2}(z)=z(1+z)^{-1},\qquad\psi_{3}(z)=z(1+z)^{-2}.

Below, we provide an extension of this statement for a special class of
\Phi
; see Proposition
2.24
below. We start with some preliminaries. For any Banach function space
\Phi
over
(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathrm{d}t)
(not necessarily such that
P
is bounded on it) and for any
\psi\in{\mathcal{E}}(S_{\omega})
we put

(26)

{\mathcal{X}}_{\Phi,\psi}:={\mathcal{X}}_{\Phi,\psi,{\mathcal{A}}}:=\{x\in{\mathcal{X}}:[x]_{\Phi,\psi,{\mathcal{A}}}<\infty\},

where

[x]_{\Phi,\psi}:=[x]_{\Phi,\psi,{\mathcal{A}}}:=\|(\cdot)^{-1}\psi(\cdot{\mathcal{A}})x\|_{\Phi({\mathcal{X}})}

Note that
[\cdot]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}
in (
23
) corresponds to
[\cdot]_{\Phi,\psi,{\mathcal{A}}}
with
\psi(z):=ze^{-z}
.

In the case of injective sectorial operators
{\mathcal{A}}
on Hilbert spaces
{\mathcal{X}}
and
\Phi=L^{p}(\mathbb{R}_{+},t^{\mu}\mathrm{d}t)
with
p\in[1,\infty]
and
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
, the corresponding spaces
{\mathcal{X}}_{\Phi,\psi,{\mathcal{A}}}
for
\psi\in H^{\infty}_{0}(S_{\omega})
with some additional decaying properties at
0
and
\infty
, have been developed and studied by McIntosh and his co-workers; see e.g.
[
1
]
. A systematized account of related results, rephrased already in a general setting of Banach spaces
{\mathcal{X}}
, was provided by Haase in
[
30
, Section 6.4]
, where the
homogeneous spaces
may be, in general, larger than
{\mathcal{X}}_{\Phi,\psi,{\mathcal{A}}}
defined above.

In particular, when
{\mathcal{A}}
is injective, an analysis of the proof of
[
30
, Theorem 6.4.2, p.150]
shows that for
\Phi=L^{p}(t^{\mu})
(
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
,
p\in[1,\infty]
), for any
\psi
,
\gamma\in{\mathcal{E}}(S_{\omega})
with
z^{-\theta}\psi
,
z^{-\theta}\gamma\in H^{\infty}_{0}(S_{\omega})
, where
\theta=1-\frac{\mu+1}{p}
if
p\in[1,\infty)
and
\theta:=1-\mu
if
p=\infty
, the semi-norms
[\cdot]_{\Phi,\psi}
and
[\cdot]_{\Phi,\phi}
are equivalent
on
{\mathcal{X}}
. It is not clear whether the general case (that is, when
{\mathcal{A}}
is not injective) can be reduced to the injective one by some direct argument. For this reason, below we provide a modification of the argument applied in the proof of
[
30
, Theorem 6.4.2]
.

The main point of the extension of
[
30
, Theorem 6.4.2]
, see Proposition
2.24
(i), is to show that in the case of arbitrary sectorial operators
{\mathcal{A}}
, and
p
,
\mu
,
\theta
and
\Phi
as above, for some
\psi,\gamma\in{\mathcal{E}}(S_{\omega})\setminus H^{\infty}_{0}(S_{\omega})
with
z^{-\theta}\psi
,
z^{-\theta}\gamma\in H^{\infty}_{0}(S_{\omega})
the equivalence of semi-norms
[\cdot]_{\Phi,\psi}
and
[\cdot]_{\Phi,\gamma}
still holds
