on
{\mathcal{X}}
.

Moreover, the statement (iii) of Proposition
2.24
shows that we should not expect such equivalence of the corresponding semi-norms if we only know
z^{-\theta}\psi
,
z^{-\theta}\gamma\in{\mathcal{E}}(S_{\omega})
instead of
z^{-\theta}\psi
,
z^{-\theta}\gamma\in H^{\infty}_{0}(S_{\omega})
. Specifically, it shows that the one of the main ingredients of the proof,
[
30
, Theorem 5.2.2b)]
, is sharp, and also gives an limitation on the assumptions of
[
30
, Theorem 6.4.2]
.

Proposition 2.24
.

Let
{\mathcal{A}}
be a sectorial operator on a Banach space
{\mathcal{X}}
.

(i)

Let
\Phi=L^{p}(\mathbb{R}_{+},t^{\mu}\mathrm{d}t)
for
p\in[1,\infty]
and
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
.
Let
\psi
,
\gamma\in{\mathcal{E}}(S_{\omega})\setminus\{0\}

(
with
\omega>\omega_{\mathcal{A}})
such that
(\cdot)^{-\theta}\psi
,
(\cdot)^{-\theta}\gamma\in H^{\infty}_{0}(S_{\omega})
with
\theta:=1-\frac{\mu+1}{p}
if
p\in[1,\infty)
or
\theta:=1-\mu
if
p=\infty
.
If for each
\xi\in\{\psi,\gamma\}
we have
\xi\in H_{0}^{\infty}(S_{\omega})
or

(27)

{\mathcal{X}}_{\Phi,\xi}\,\subset\overline{D({\mathcal{A}})}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\xi({\mathcal{A}})\overline{D({\mathcal{A}})}\,\subset{\mathcal{R}}({\mathcal{A}}),

then, the semi-norms
[\cdot]_{\Phi,\psi}
and
[\cdot]_{\Phi,\gamma}
are equivalent on
{\mathcal{X}}
.

(ii)

If, in addition,
-{\mathcal{A}}
generates a bounded holomorphic semigroup
{\mathcal{T}}
on
{\mathcal{X}}
, then for any
\theta\in(0,1)
, if
\Phi=L^{p}(\mathbb{R}_{+},t^{p(1-\theta)-1}\mathrm{d}t)
with
p\in[1,\infty)
or
\Phi=L^{\infty}(t^{1-\theta})
, then all semi-norms
[\cdot]_{\Phi,\psi_{j}}
,
j=1,2,3
, for
\psi_{j}
as in (
25
), are equivalent to the semi-norm (
23
) and
K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})={\mathcal{X}}_{\Phi,\psi_{j}}
(as the sets).
In the case
\Phi=L^{\infty}(t)
(i.e.
\theta=0
),
{\mathcal{X}}_{\Phi,\psi_{2}}={\mathcal{X}}_{\Phi,\psi}={\mathcal{X}}
.

(iii)

If
-{\mathcal{A}}
generates a bounded holomorphic semigroup
{\mathcal{T}}
on
{\mathcal{X}}
and
{\mathcal{A}}y\neq 0
for some
y\in D({\mathcal{A}})
, then the conclusion of (ii) false for
\theta=0
and
p\in[1,\infty)
.

More precisely, for any
p\in[1,\infty)
, if
\Phi=L^{p}(\mathbb{R}_{+},t^{p-1}\mathrm{d}t)
, then the semi-norm
[\cdot]_{\Phi,\psi_{2}}
is not equivalent to any of the following, mutually equivalent semi-norms:
[\cdot]_{\Phi,\psi_{j}}

(j=1,3)
and
[\cdot]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}

(
see (
25
) and (
23
)
)
.

Proof.

(i) We provide only main supplementary observation which should be made in the proof of
[
30
, Theorem 6.4.2]
. Following
[
30
, Lemma 6.2.5]
, set

\alpha(x):=z^{n}(1+z)^{-2n},\quad\beta(z):=c^{-1}\alpha(z)\overline{\gamma(\bar{z})},\quad\phi(z):=\alpha(z)\beta(z)\gamma(z),\quad\,z\in S_{\omega},

where
|\theta|+1<n\in\mathbb{N}
and
c
stands for
\int_{0}^{\infty}|\alpha(t)\gamma(t)|^{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}
, which is finite because
\alpha\gamma\in H^{\infty}_{0}(S_{\omega})
.
Obviously,
\beta,\phi\in H^{\infty}_{0}(S_{\omega})
.
To allow
\psi
or
\gamma
be in
{\mathcal{E}}(S_{\omega})\setminus H^{\infty}_{0}(S_{\omega})
but satisfy (
27
), the point is to use the full power of the McIntosh’s approximation result (see, e.g.
[
30
, Theorem 5.2.6 or Lemma 5.2.3e)]
), not only its
intermediate
form
[
30
, Proposition 5.2.4c)]
that was applied in the proof of
[
30
, Theorem 6.4.2]
and which is sufficient in the case
\psi,\gamma\in H^{\infty}_{0}(S_{\omega})
.
More precisely, arguing as in the proof of
[
30
, Theorem 6.4.2]
, we derive to the estimate: for all
0<a<b<\infty

\displaystyle\|\phi_{a,b}({\mathcal{A}})t^{-\theta}

\displaystyle\psi(t{\mathcal{A}})x\|_{\mathcal{X}}

(28)

\displaystyle\leq c_{\beta}c_{\psi,\alpha}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\|\widetilde{\psi}(t{\mathcal{A}})\widetilde{\alpha}(s{\mathcal{A}})\|_{{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{X}})}\|s^{-\theta}\gamma(s{\mathcal{A}})x\|^{p}_{\mathcal{X}}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s}\right)^{1/p}

(with modification when
p=\infty
),
where

\phi_{a,b}({\mathcal{A}}):=\int_{a}^{b}\phi(s{\mathcal{A}})\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s},\quad\widetilde{\psi}(z):=z^{-\theta}\psi(z),\quad\widetilde{\alpha}(z):=z^{\theta}\alpha(z)\quad(z\in S_{\omega})

and

c_{\beta}:=\sup_{s>0}\|\beta(s{\mathcal{A}})\|_{{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{X}})},\quad c_{\psi,\alpha}:=\sup_{t>0}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\|\widetilde{\psi}(t{\mathcal{A}})\widetilde{\alpha}(s{\mathcal{A}})\|_{{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{X}})}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s}\right)^{1/p^{\prime}}.

Since
\beta\in H^{\infty}_{0}(S_{\omega})
,
c_{\beta}
is finite; see, e.g.
[
30
, Theorem 5.2.2a)]
.
Since
\widetilde{\psi}
and
\widetilde{\alpha}
are in
H^{\infty}_{0}(S_{\omega})
, by
[
30
, Theorem 5.2.2b)]
,
c_{\psi,\alpha}
is also finite. Taking the
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathrm{d}t/t)
norm of the right-hand side of (
28
), by Fubini’s theorem and, again,
[
30
, Theorem 5.2.2b)]
, yields the quantity less than
c_{\beta}c_{\psi,\alpha}c_{\alpha,\psi}[x]_{\Phi,\gamma,{\mathcal{A}}}
with
c_{\alpha,\psi}<\infty
.
Therefore, with the help of Lebesgue’s domination theorem, it suffices to show that for all
x\in{\mathcal{X}}_{\Phi,\gamma}
and
t>0
we have:

\phi_{a,b}({\mathcal{A}})\psi(t{\mathcal{A}})x\rightarrow\psi(t{\mathcal{A}})x\qquad\textrm{as }a\rightarrow 0\raisebox{0.95pt}{$+$},\,\,b\rightarrow\infty.

If we know that
\psi\in H^{\infty}_{0}(S_{\omega})
, then the conclusion follows from
[
30
, Proposition 5.2.4c)]
directly. In the case of
\psi
satisfying (
27
), by McIntosh’s approximation result, see
[
30
, Theorem 5.2.6]
, it is sufficient that for all
x\in{\mathcal{X}}_{\Phi,\gamma}
,
\psi(t{\mathcal{A}})x\in\overline{D({\mathcal{A}})\cap{\mathcal{R}}({\mathcal{A}})}
.
Recall that for every
y\in\overline{D({\mathcal{A}})}
,
\tau(\tau+{\mathcal{A}})^{-1}y\rightarrow y
as
\tau\rightarrow\infty
.
Since
\psi\in{\mathcal{E}}(S_{\omega})
,
\overline{D({\mathcal{A}})}
is invariant with respect to
\psi(t{\mathcal{A}})
and by our assumption for every
x\in\overline{D({\mathcal{A}})}
we have
\psi(t{\mathcal{A}})x\in{\mathcal{R}}({\mathcal{A}})
, Hence

D({\mathcal{A}})\cap{\mathcal{R}}({\mathcal{A}})\ni\psi(t{\mathcal{A}})\tau(\tau+{\mathcal{A}})^{-1}x=\tau(\tau+{\mathcal{A}})^{-1}\psi(t{\mathcal{A}})x\rightarrow\psi(t{\mathcal{A}})x

for all
x\in\overline{D({\mathcal{A}})}
as
\tau\rightarrow\infty
. Thus, we get

[\cdot]_{\Phi,\psi}\leq c_{\beta}c_{\psi,\alpha}c_{\alpha,\psi}[\cdot]_{\Phi,\gamma}
and
{\mathcal{X}}_{\Phi,\gamma}\subset{\mathcal{X}}_{\Phi,\psi}
.
Interchanging
\gamma
with
\psi
in the above reasoning completes the proof of (i).

For (ii), note that all functions
\psi_{j}
(
j=1,2,3
) satisfy the conditions stated in (A) above. Moreover, the Hardy operator
P
is bounded on
\Phi
; see Lemma
2.17
(in fact,
Q
too). Therefore, (ii) follows from the result stated on the beginning of this section.
However, it is more instructive to give direct arguments for some equivalences based on the statement (i). First note that
\psi_{3},(\cdot)^{-\theta}\psi_{3}\in H^{\infty}_{0}(S_{\omega})
.
Further, the proof of
{\mathcal{X}}_{\Phi,\psi_{j}}\subset\overline{D({\mathcal{A}})}
for
j=1,2
, does not use the boundedness of
P
on
\Phi
and follows directly from the estimates: for all
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
and
t>0

\inf_{z\in D({\mathcal{A}})}\|x-z\|_{\mathcal{X}}\leq\|x-e^{-t{\mathcal{A}}}x\|_{\mathcal{X}}=\|\psi_{1}(t{\mathcal{A}})x\|_{\mathcal{X}},

\inf_{z\in D({\mathcal{A}})}\|x-z\|_{\mathcal{X}}\leq\|x-(1+t{\mathcal{A}})^{-1}x\|_{\mathcal{X}}=\|\psi_{2}(t{\mathcal{A}})x\|_{\mathcal{X}}.

Indeed, since
\Phi\subset L^{1}_{loc}
, the function
\mathbb{R}_{+}\ni t\mapsto\frac{1}{t}
is not in
\Phi
; see also Remark
2.25
below.
Moreover, for
\psi(z):=ze^{z}
, as in the proof of Proposition
2.23
, we can write for all
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
and
t>0
:

\inf_{z\in D({\mathcal{A}})}\|x-z\|_{\mathcal{X}}\leq\|x-e^{-t{\mathcal{A}}}x\|_{\mathcal{X}}=\Big\|{\mathcal{A}}\int_{0}^{t}{\mathcal{T}}(s)x\mathrm{d}s\Big\|_{\mathcal{X}}\leq\int_{0}^{t}\Big\|\frac{1}{s}\psi(s{\mathcal{A}})x\Big\|_{\mathcal{X}}\mathrm{d}s.

Therefore, the boundedness of
P
on
\Phi
ensures that
x\in\overline{D({\mathcal{A}})}
if
x\in{\mathcal{X}}_{\Phi,\psi}
.
Furthermore, for all
j=1,2
we have that
\psi_{j}({\mathcal{A}}){\mathcal{X}}\subset{\mathcal{R}}({\mathcal{A}})
. Indeed, for
j=1
, by Lemma
2.3
,
\psi_{1}(t{\mathcal{A}})=-{\mathcal{A}}\int_{0}^{t}{\mathcal{T}}(s)\mathrm{d}s
,
t>0
, and the case
j=2
is obvious. Note also that
\psi({\mathcal{A}}){\mathcal{X}}={\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{T}}(1){\mathcal{X}}={\mathcal{T}}(1/2){\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{T}}(1/2){\mathcal{X}}\subset D({\mathcal{A}})\cap{\mathcal{R}}({\mathcal{A}})
and
K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})={\mathcal{X}}_{\Phi,\psi}
(as the sets) by Proposition
2.23
.
Thus, all functions
\psi_{j}
,
j=1,2,3
and
\psi
satisfy the assumption of (i), which implies the first statement of (ii). For the second one, if
\Phi=L^{\infty}(t)
, then for all
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
we have that

[x]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}\leq\sup_{t>0}\|t{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{T}}(t)\|_{{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{X}})}\|x\|_{\mathcal{X}}
and

[x]_{\Phi,\psi_{2}}\leq\sup_{t>0}\|I-t(t+{\mathcal{A}})^{-1}\|_{{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{X}})}\|x\|_{\mathcal{X}}
.
It easily gives the desired claim.

For (iii), since
\psi
and
\psi_{j}
,
j=1,3
, are in
H^{\infty}_{0}(\Sigma_{\omega})
and
\theta=0
, by (i), their corresponding semi-norms are mutually equivalent.
Suppose that there exists
y\in D({\mathcal{A}})
such that
{\mathcal{A}}y\neq 0\in{\mathcal{X}}
. Let
\tilde{y}:=\lambda(\lambda+{\mathcal{A}})^{-1}y
for some
\lambda>0
. Of course,
x:={\mathcal{A}}\tilde{y}\in D({\mathcal{A}})\cap{\mathcal{R}}({\mathcal{A}})
and, since
{\mathcal{A}}y\neq 0
,
x=\lambda(\lambda+{\mathcal{A}})^{-1}{\mathcal{A}}y\neq 0
too. For some
n\in\mathbb{N}
with
n>p
, where
p\in[1,\infty)
is fixed, and all
\lambda>0
set

x_{\lambda}:={\mathcal{A}}^{n}(\lambda+{\mathcal{A}})^{-n}(1+\lambda{\mathcal{A}})^{-n}x

Since
x\in{\mathcal{R}}({\mathcal{A}})
, we have that
x_{\lambda}\rightarrow x
as
\lambda\rightarrow 0^{+}
. Hence, for some
\lambda_{0}>0
small enough
x_{\lambda_{0}}=:x_{0}\neq 0
.

It is readily seen that
x_{0}\in{\mathcal{X}}_{\Phi,\psi}
, where
\Phi=L^{p}(\mathbb{R}_{+},t^{p-1}\mathrm{d}t)
,
p\in[1,\infty)
. Indeed, denoting
y_{0}:=(\lambda_{0}+{\mathcal{A}})^{-n}(1+\lambda_{0}{\mathcal{A}})^{-n}x_{0}
, i.e.
x_{0}={\mathcal{A}}^{n}y_{0}
, we get

\displaystyle[x_{0}]_{\Phi,\psi}^{p}

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{\infty}\|{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{T}}(t)x_{0}\|_{\mathcal{X}}^{p}\,t^{p-1}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\|{\mathcal{T}}(t){\mathcal{A}}x_{0}\|^{p}_{\mathcal{X}}\,t^{p-1}\mathrm{d}t+\int_{1}^{\infty}\|{\mathcal{A}}^{n+1}{\mathcal{T}}(t)y_{0}\|_{\mathcal{X}}\,t^{p-1}\mathrm{d}t.

Since
\sup_{t>0}\|t^{n}{\mathcal{A}}^{n}{\mathcal{T}}\c{(}t)\|_{{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{X}})}<\infty
and
n>p
, we have that
x_{0}\in{\mathcal{X}}_{\Phi,\psi}
. We show that
x\notin{\mathcal{X}}_{\Phi,\psi_{2}}
.
In fact, if
x_{0}\in{\mathcal{X}}_{\Phi,\psi_{2}}
, then

[x_{0}]_{\Phi,\psi_{2}}^{p}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\|{\mathcal{A}}(1+t{\mathcal{A}})^{-1}x_{0}\|_{\mathcal{X}}^{p}\,t^{p-1}\mathrm{d}t=\int_{0}^{\infty}\|{\mathcal{A}}(t+{\mathcal{A}})^{-1}x_{0}\|_{\mathcal{X}}^{p}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}<\infty.

In particular,

\liminf_{t\rightarrow 0\raisebox{0.95pt}{$+$}}\|{\mathcal{A}}(t+{\mathcal{A}})^{-1}x_{0}\|_{\mathcal{X}}=0

However, since for all
\tilde{x}\in\overline{{\mathcal{R}}({\mathcal{A}})}
we have
{\mathcal{A}}(t+{\mathcal{A}})^{-1}\tilde{x}\rightarrow\tilde{x}
as
t\rightarrow 0\raisebox{0.95pt}{$+$}
, and since
x_{0}\in{\mathcal{R}}({\mathcal{A}})
, we get
x_{0}=0
, which contradicts our assumption. It completes the proof of (iii). ∎

Remark 2.25
.

(a) We first remark on the condition (
27
) of Proposition
2.24
(i). In general, for
\Phi\in L^{1}_{loc}
, if
\psi\in{\mathcal{E}}(S_{\omega})
is such that
\phi:=\psi+1
is holomorphic at
0
and decays at
\infty
as
|z|^{-\epsilon}
(
z\in S_{\omega}
) for some
\epsilon>0
, then
{\mathcal{X}}_{\Phi,\psi}\subset\overline{D({\mathcal{A}})}
.
Indeed, then
\psi(tA)=I-\phi(t{\mathcal{A}})
and
\phi(t{\mathcal{A}}){\mathcal{X}}\subset\overline{D({\mathcal{A}})}
, since
\phi(t{\mathcal{A}})
is represented by the absolutely convergent Cauchy integral
\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\Gamma}\phi(z)R(z,{\mathcal{A}})\mathrm{d}z
with
\Gamma:=\partial(S_{\omega}\cup B)
, where
B
is a ball with the center at
0
.

(b) Note also that if
{\mathcal{A}}
is densely defined and
z^{-1}\psi
,
z^{-1}\gamma\in{\mathcal{E}}(S_{\omega})
, then (
27
) holds for any
\psi,\gamma\in{\mathcal{E}}(S_{\omega})
. Indeed, recall that
\psi({\mathcal{A}})={\mathcal{A}}(z^{-1}\psi)({\mathcal{A}})
and similarly for
\gamma
; see e.g.
[
30
, Theorem 1.3.2]
. Therefore, in such case, Proposition
2.24
(i) does not require the boundedness of Hardy’s operator
P
on
\Phi
as the assumptions of
[
15
, Theorems 3.1 and 3.4]
; see (A) and (B) at the beginning of this subsection.

We conclude this subsection with a noteworthy implication of Proposition
2.24
to the Kalton-Portal characterization of the
L^{1}
-m.r. and
L^{\infty}
-m.r. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}

[
37
, Theorems 3.6 and 3.7]
; see also Theorems
2.8
and
2.14
.
The presented below (equivalent) characterization conditions are expressed in
a priori
terms of a generator, which may be more useful in applications than those involving semigroup operators.

Proposition 2.26
.

Let
-A
be the generator of a bounded analytic semigroup on a Banach space
X
.
Let
0\neq\gamma\in H^{\infty}_{0}(S_{\omega})
for some
\omega\in(\omega_{A},\frac{\pi}{2})
, where
\omega_{A}
denotes the sectoriality angle of
A
.

Then, the following assertions are true:

(i)

The operator
A
has
L^{1}
-m.r. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
if and only if there exists a constant
C
such that for every
x\in X

(29)

\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big\|\frac{1}{t}\gamma(tA)x\Big\|_{X}\mathrm{d}t\leq C\|x\|_{X}.

In particular, as
\gamma
one can take
\gamma(z):=z(1+z)^{-1-\epsilon}
for any
\epsilon>0
.

Moreover, if there exists a constant
C
such that for every
x\in X

\int_{0}^{\infty}\|A(1+tA)^{-1}x\|_{X}\mathrm{d}t\leq C\|x\|_{X},

then
A=0\in{\mathcal{L}}(X)
.

(ii)

The operator
A
has
L^{\infty}
-m.r. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
if and only if there exists a constant
C
such that for every
x\in X

\|x\|_{X}\leq C\sup_{t>0}\|\gamma(tA)x\|_{X}+\limsup_{t\rightarrow\infty}\|{\mathcal{T}}(t)x\|_{X}.

Proof.

Let
\psi(z):=ze^{-z}
and
\eta(z):=z(1+z)^{-1-\epsilon}
for
z\in S_{\omega}
with
\omega>\omega_{A}
. Obviously,
\psi,\eta,\gamma\in H^{\infty}_{0}(S_{\omega})
. Therefore, taking
\Phi:=L^{1}
(resp.
\Phi:=L^{\infty}(t)
), which corresponds to
\theta=0
, by Proposition
2.24
(i) and
[
37
, Theorems 3.6 and 3.7]
, we get the desired claim of (i) (resp. of (ii)).

The proof of the additional statement of (i) follows the idea of the proof of Proposition
2.24
(iii) above. Indeed, following (
26
) and (
25
), note that
X_{\Phi,\psi_{2},A}=X
with
\Phi=L^{1}
. For all
x\in X
we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\|A(1+tA)^{-1}x\|_{X}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\|A(t+A)^{-1}x\|_{X}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\|x-t(t+A)^{-1}x\|_{X}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}<\infty.

In particular, for all
x\in X

\liminf_{t\rightarrow 0\raisebox{0.95pt}{$+$}}\|A(t+A)^{-1}x\|_{\mathcal{X}}=\liminf_{t\rightarrow\infty}\|x-t(t+A)^{-1}x\|_{\mathcal{X}}=0.

It shows that
\overline{D(A)}=X
and, in particular, for any
x\in{\mathcal{R}}(A)\subset X
,
\liminf_{t\rightarrow 0\raisebox{0.95pt}{$+$}}\|A(t+A)^{-1}x\|_{X}=0
. But, for
x=Ay
with
y\in D(A)
, we have

A(t+A)^{-1}x=x+ty+t^{2}(t+A)^{-1}y\rightarrow x\textrm{ as }t\rightarrow 0\raisebox{0.95pt}{$+$}.

Therefore
{\mathcal{R}}(A)=\{0\}
, i.e.
A=0
. The proof is complete.
∎

We conclude with a result, see Theorem
2.28
below, which should be carefully confronted with Theorem
3.1
. We start with an auxiliary lemma.

Lemma 2.27
.

Let
-{\mathcal{A}}
be the generator of a bound analytic semigroup on a Banach space
{\mathcal{X}}
.
Let
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
and
\omega\in(\omega_{\mathcal{A}},\frac{\pi}{2})
.
Let
\psi\in{\mathcal{E}}(S_{\omega})
with
(\cdot)^{-\theta}\psi\in H^{\infty}_{0}(S_{\omega})
satisfy
{\mathcal{X}}_{\Phi,\psi}\subset\overline{D({\mathcal{A}})}
and
\psi({\mathcal{A}})\overline{D({\mathcal{A}})}\subset{\mathcal{R}}({\mathcal{A}})
or
\psi\in H^{\infty}_{0}(S_{\omega})
.
Let
\Phi:=L^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+},t^{-\theta}\mathrm{d}t)
.
Finally, let
X
stand for
{\mathcal{X}}_{\Phi,\psi}
equipped with
\|x\|_{X}:=\|x\|_{{\mathcal{X}}}+[x]_{\Phi,\psi}
.

Then, the following statements are true.

(i)

The space
(X,\|\cdot\|_{X})
is a Banach space and the part of
-{\mathcal{A}}
in
X
is the generator of an analytic semigroup.

(ii)

For all
x\in{\mathcal{X}}_{\Phi,\psi}

\int^{\infty}_{0}[{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{T}}(t)x]_{\Phi,\psi}\mathrm{d}t\lesssim[x]_{\Phi,\psi}.

Proof.

The statement (i) follows by standard arguments. We omit its proof.

For (ii), let
\gamma=\phi\psi
for some
\phi\in H^{\infty}_{0}(S_{\omega})
and let
\eta(z):=ze^{-z}

(z\in\mathbb{C})
.
Of course,
\gamma\in H^{\infty}_{0}(S_{\omega})
and
(\cdot)^{-\theta}\gamma\in H^{\infty}_{0}(S_{\omega})
.
By Proposition
2.24
, the semi-norms
[\cdot]_{\Phi,\psi}
and
[\cdot]_{\Phi,\gamma}
are equivalent on
{\mathcal{X}}
.

Moreover, since
\gamma(t{\mathcal{A}})=\phi(t{\mathcal{A}})\psi(t{\mathcal{A}})
and
\phi,\eta\in H^{\infty}_{0}(S_{\omega})
, by
[
30
, Theorem 5.2.2b)]
, we get

\kappa:=\sup_{s>0}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\|\phi(s{\mathcal{A}})\eta(t{\mathcal{A}})\|_{{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{X}})}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}<\infty.

Therefore, writing
\frac{1}{t}\eta(t{\mathcal{A}})={\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{T}}(t)
, we have that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}[{t}^{-1}\eta(t{\mathcal{A}})x]_{\Phi,\gamma}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\|{s}^{-1}\gamma(s{\mathcal{A}}){t}^{-1}\eta(t{\mathcal{A}})x\|_{\mathcal{X}}s^{-\theta}\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\|\phi(s{\mathcal{A}}){t}^{-1}\eta(t{\mathcal{A}})\|_{{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{X}})}\|{s}^{-1}\psi(s{\mathcal{A}})x\|_{\mathcal{X}}s^{-\theta}\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\kappa\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\|{s}^{-1}\psi(s{\mathcal{A}})x\|_{\mathcal{X}}s^{-\theta}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\simeq\,\kappa\,[x]_{\Phi,\psi}.

By Proposition
2.24
(i),
[\cdot]_{\Phi,\psi}\lesssim[\cdot]_{\Phi,\gamma}
on
X_{\Phi,\psi}=X_{\Phi,\gamma}
. It finishes the proof of (ii).

∎

Theorem 2.28
.

Let
-{\mathcal{A}}
be the generator of a bounded, analytic semigroup on a Banach space
{\mathcal{X}}
.
Let
0\neq\psi\in H^{\infty}_{0}(S_{\omega})
, where
\omega\in(\omega_{\mathcal{A}},\frac{\pi}{2})
.

Let

X:={\mathcal{X}}_{1,{\mathcal{A}}}:=\Big\{x\in{\mathcal{X}}:\int_{0}^{\infty}\|{t^{-1}}\psi(t{\mathcal{A}})x\|_{\mathcal{X}}\mathrm{d}t<\infty\Big\}

be equipped with the norm

\|x\|_{X}:=\|x\|_{{\mathcal{X}}}+\int_{0}^{\infty}\|{t^{-1}}\psi(t{\mathcal{A}})x\|_{\mathcal{X}}\mathrm{d}t.

Then, the part of
{\mathcal{A}}
in
X
has
L^{1}
-m.r. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

Moreover,
D({\mathcal{A}})\cap{\mathcal{R}}({\mathcal{A}})\subset X
. In particular, if
D({\mathcal{A}})
and
{\mathcal{R}}({\mathcal{A}})
are dense in
{\mathcal{X}}
, then
{\mathcal{X}}_{1,{\mathcal{A}}}
is.

Proof.

(i) By Lemma
2.27
(i) we already know that
X
is a Banach space and the part of
-{\mathcal{A}}
in
X
is the generator of a bounded, analytic semigroup
T
on
X
. Therefore, it suffices to show that
A
satisfies
(
29
) of Proposition
2.26
.

Since
\eta(z):=ze^{-z}

(z\in\mathbb{C})
is in
H^{\infty}_{0}(S_{\omega})
, by
[
30
, Theorem 6.4.2]
, the semi-norms
[\cdot]_{\Phi,\eta}
and
[\cdot]_{\Phi,\psi}
are equivalent. Here
\Phi:=L^{1}
(i.e.
\theta=0
). Therefore, by Lemma
2.27
(ii), for all
x\in X

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\|AT(t)x\|_{X}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\|AT(t)x\|_{\mathcal{X}}\mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{\infty}[AT(t)x]_{\Phi,\psi}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\lesssim[x]_{\Phi,\eta}+[x]_{\Phi,\psi}\lesssim\|x\|_{X}.

For the last statement note that for
x\in D({\mathcal{A}})
such that
x={\mathcal{A}}y
for some
y\in D({\mathcal{A}})
, we have for all
t>0
that

\|{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{T}}(t)x\|_{\mathcal{X}}\leq\min\left(\|{\mathcal{T}}(t){\mathcal{A}}x\|_{\mathcal{X}},\|{\mathcal{A}}^{2}{\mathcal{T}}(t)y\|_{\mathcal{X}}\right)\lesssim\min(1,t^{-2}).

Therefore,

D({\mathcal{A}})\cap{\mathcal{R}}({\mathcal{A}})\subset{\mathcal{X}}_{\Phi,\eta}={\mathcal{X}}_{\Phi,\psi}=X
. Finally, recall that the density of
D(A)
and
{\mathcal{R}}(A)
implies the density of
D({\mathcal{A}})\cap{\mathcal{R}}({\mathcal{A}})
; see, e.g.
[
47
, Theorem 3.1.2(iv), p. 91]
. It completes the proof.
∎

Remark 2.29
.

(a) One can show that
{\mathcal{X}}_{1,{\mathcal{A}}}\subset J_{0,1}({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})
. Since
J_{0,1}({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})
coincides with the closure of
D({\mathcal{A}})
in
{\mathcal{X}}
(cf.
[
9
, Theorem 3.4.2]
), in general, this inclusion is proper.
Furthermore, the following example shows that, in general,
({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})_{\theta,1}\nsubseteq{\mathcal{X}}_{1,{\mathcal{A}}}
for all
\theta\in(0,1)
.

Let
{\mathcal{X}}:=L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
and
{\mathcal{A}}:=-\Delta
be the negative of the distributional realization of the Laplace operator on
{\mathcal{X}}
. In this case, the space
{\mathcal{X}}_{1,{\mathcal{A}}}
is contained in the subspace
\{h\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}):{\mathcal{F}}{h}(0)=0\}
of
L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
, where
{\mathcal{F}}h
denotes the Fourier transform of
h
.
Indeed, suppose that
h\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
is such that
{\mathcal{F}}{h}(0)\neq 0
. Since
{\mathcal{F}}{h}
is continuous at zero, there exists
\delta>0
such that
|{\mathcal{F}}{h}(t)|\geq\frac{1}{2}|{\mathcal{F}}{h}(0)|
for all
t\in(0,\delta)
. Hence, the integral
\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{|{\mathcal{F}}{h}(t)|}{t}\,\mathrm{d}t
diverges. We will show that this implies
\int_{0}^{\infty}\|{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{T}}(t)h\|_{{\mathcal{X}}}\mathrm{d}t=\infty
, where
{\mathcal{T}}
stands for a semigroup generated by
-{\mathcal{A}}
. Note that

{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{T}}(t)h)(\xi)=|\xi|^{2}e^{-t|\xi|^{2}}{\mathcal{F}}h(\xi),\qquad\xi\in\mathbb{R}.

Therefore, we get

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\|{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{T}}(t)h\|_{{\mathcal{X}}}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\geq\int_{0}^{\infty}\sup_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}|\xi|^{2}e^{-t|\xi|^{2}}|{\mathcal{F}}{h}(\xi)|\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\geq\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{t}\left|{\mathcal{F}}{h}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}\right)\right|\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\approx\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{|{\mathcal{F}}{h}(t)|}{t}\mathrm{d}t.

It proves our claim.

(b) As regarding the additional statement of Theorem
2.28
, recall that for densely defined, injective sectorial operators
{\mathcal{A}}
on a reflexive Banach space
{\mathcal{X}}
,
D(A)\cap{\mathcal{R}}({\mathcal{A}})
is dense in
{\mathcal{X}}
; see, e.g.
[
47
, Theorem 3.1.2(iii), p.91]
.

2.8.
The density of the domain in interpolation spaces

Our next result clarifies the density of the domain of the part
A
of
{\mathcal{A}}
in
K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})
for some parameters
\Phi
.

Lemma 2.30
.

Let
{\mathcal{A}}
,
{\mathcal{T}}
and
\Phi
be as specified in the previous lemma.

Then, the part
A
of
{\mathcal{A}}
in
K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})
is again the generator of a bounded analytic semigroup
T
on
K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})
, which is the restriction of
{\mathcal{T}}
to
K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})
. Moreover, the following assertions hold.

(i)

If, in addition,
\Phi
has absolutely continuous norm, then the domain of
A
is dense in
K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})
, i.e. the semigroup
T
generated by
A
is strongly continuous at
0
.

In particular, it applies to
\Phi=L^{q}(\mathbb{R}_{+},t^{q(1-\theta)-1}\mathrm{d}t)
with
\theta\in(0,1)
and
q\in[1,\infty)
, which corresponds to the real interpolation space
K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})=({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})_{\theta,q}
.

(ii)

If, in addition,
{\mathcal{A}}
is unbounded on
{\mathcal{X}}
and
\Phi=L^{\infty}(t^{-\theta})
with
\theta\in(0,1)
, then its part
A
in
K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})=({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})_{\theta,\infty}

is not densely defined.

Proof.

Let
X:=K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})
.
Since
\rho({\mathcal{A}})\subset\rho(A)
with
R(\lambda,A)=R(\lambda,{\mathcal{A}})_{|X}

(\lambda\in\rho({\mathcal{A}}))
, by the representation of the space
K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})
stated in Proposition
2.23
,
-A
is the generator of the semigroup
T(t):={\mathcal{T}}(t)_{|X}
(
t\geq 0)
on
X
.

Further note that our assumption on the boundedness of
P
on
\Phi
implies that
\Phi\subset L^{1}_{loc}
. It gives that
K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})=K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{Y}},D_{\mathcal{A}})
, where
{\mathcal{Y}}:=\overline{D({\mathcal{A}})}^{{\mathcal{X}}}
. Indeed, if there exists
x\in{\mathcal{X}}\setminus{\mathcal{Y}}
, then
\frac{1}{t}K(t,x,{\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})\geq\frac{1}{t}\textrm{dist}(x,{\mathcal{Y}})
for
t\in(0,1)
. Moreover, for each
x\in{\mathcal{Y}}
,
K(t,x,{\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})=K(t,x,{\mathcal{Y}},D_{\mathcal{A}})
,
t\geq 0
. Consequently,
X\subset{\mathcal{Y}}
.

We show that for every
x\in X
, the vectors
x_{n}:=n(n+{\mathcal{A}})^{-1}x\in D({\mathcal{A}})
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, are in the domain of
A
and approximate
x
in the norm of
X
.
For the first statement, just note that
{\mathcal{A}}x_{n}=nx-n^{2}(n+{\mathcal{A}})^{-1}x\in X
. For the second one, note that for all
t>0
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
:

\|{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{T}}(t)(x_{n}-x)\|_{\mathcal{X}}\leq\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\|n(n+{\mathcal{A}})^{-1}-I\|_{{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{X}})}\|{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{T}}(t)x\|_{\mathcal{X}}.

Hence, since
x\in X\subset{\mathcal{Y}}
,
x_{n}-x=-{\mathcal{A}}(n+{\mathcal{A}})^{-1}x
converges to
0
in
{\mathcal{X}}
as
n\rightarrow\infty
, by (
2.27
) and the absolute continuity of the norm of
\Phi
,
x_{n}
approximate
x
in the norm of
X
. It completes the proof of (i).

For (ii), recall that for any Banach couple
({\mathcal{Y}},\mathcal{Z})
such that
\mathcal{Z}
is continuously embedded in
{\mathcal{Y}}
, but not closed in
{\mathcal{Y}}
, the real interpolation spaces

({\mathcal{Y}},\mathcal{Z})_{\theta,\infty}
are not
regular
, that is,
\mathcal{Z}\subset({\mathcal{Y}},\mathcal{Z})_{\theta,\infty}
is not a dense subset of
({\mathcal{Y}},\mathcal{Z})_{\theta,\infty}
(
\theta\in(0,1)
), cf. for instance
[
9
, Theorem 3.4.2(c)]
.

Specifying it to our context, the set
D({\mathcal{A}})
(hence also
D(A)
) is not dense in
({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})_{\theta,\infty}
.
∎

3.
Extension of Da Prato - Grisvard theorem

The main results of this section, Theorems
3.1
and
3.3
, identify those Banach function spaces
\Phi
over
\mathbb{R}_{+}
(in other words, the parameters of the
K
-method) such that, for any generator
-{\mathcal{A}}
of a bounded analytic semigroup on a Banach space
{\mathcal{X}}
, the part
A
of
{\mathcal{A}}
in the corresponding interpolation space
X=K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})
satisfies the
L^{1}
- or
L^{\infty}
-m.r. type of estimates, including weighted ones. Even for
\Phi
corresponding to the classical real interpolation spaces
({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})_{\theta,r}
(
\theta\in(0,1)
,
r=1,\infty
), our results extend, in several ways, Da Prato-Grisvard type results from the literature. In particular, our assumptions on the force term
f
in
(CP)_{A,f,x}
to derive the homogeneous estimates (
32
) or (
38
) or (
45
) should be compared with those in
[
51
, Proposition 2.14]
; see also Section
5
for further connections to related results in the literature. Moreover, in
3.1
(iii) we provide a natural example of a non-invertible generator of a bounded, analytic semigroup on a real interpolation space, which does not have
L^{1}
-m.r. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

3.1.
An extension of the Da Prato-Grisvard theorem for
L^{1}
-m.r.

Recall that
[\cdot]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}
stands for the homogenous part of the norm of the interpolation space
K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})
; see (
23
).

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
-{\mathcal{A}}
be the generator of a bounded analytic semigroup
{\mathcal{T}}
on a Banach space
{\mathcal{X}}
. Let
w
be a weight function on
(0,\infty)
such
that
[w]_{P+Q,L^{1}}<\infty

(
see, (
18
)
)
.

Denote by
A
the part of
{\mathcal{A}}
in
X:=K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})
, where
\Phi:=L^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+},w\mathrm{d}t)
.

Then,
-A
is the generator of a bounded analytic
C_{0}
-semigroup
T
on
X
and the following assertions hold.

(i)

For any
f\in L^{1}_{loc}([0,\infty);X)
and
x\in X
the function

(30)

u(t):=T(t)x+\int_{0}^{t}T(t-s)f(s)\mathrm{d}s,\qquad t\geq 0,

is a unique strong solution to
(CP)_{A,f,x}
.

(ii)

The operator
A
on
X
has
L^{1}
-m.r.  on finite intervals. Moreover, for any
\tau\in(0,\infty)
there exists a constant
C_{\tau}>0
such that for any
f\in L^{1}_{loc}([0,\infty);X)
and
x\in X
the corresponding strong solution
u
to
(CP)_{A,f,x}
satisfies

(31)

\|u^{\prime}\|_{L^{1}(0,\tau;X)}+\|Au\|_{L^{1}(0,\tau;X)}+\|u\|_{{BUC}([0,\tau];X)}\leq C_{\tau}\left(\|f\|_{L^{1}(0,\tau;X)}+\|x\|_{X}\right).

Furthermore, the following homogenous estimates hold on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
:

there exists a constant
C>0
such that for every
f\in L^{1}_{loc}([0,\infty);X)
with
[f(\cdot)]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
and every
x\in X
, the strong solution
u
to
(CP)_{A,f,x}
satisfies

\displaystyle\|\,[u^{\prime}]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}+\|\,[Au]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}

\displaystyle+\|[u]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}\|_{{BUC}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+})}

(32)

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\|\,[f]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}+[x]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}\right),

where
[\cdot]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}
stands for the homogeneous part of the norm
\|\cdot\|_{X}
of
X
.

(iii)

In general,
A
does not have
L^{1}
-m.r. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
. More precisely,
the distributional realization of the negative Laplacian
-\Delta
on the classical Besov spaces
X=B^{2\theta}_{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})=(L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),W^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))_{\theta,1}
,
\theta\in(0,1)
, does not have
L^{1}
-m.r. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

(iv)

If, in addition,
0\in\rho({\mathcal{A}})
,
then
A
on
X
has
L^{1}
-m.r. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
and, furthermore, the solution operator
U
defined in (
1
) is bounded on
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+};X)
.

Consequently, for every
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+};X)
and
x\in X
the corresponding solution
u
satisfies (
32
) with the norm
\|\cdot\|_{X}
instead of the seminorm
[\cdot]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}
and, in addition,

\|u\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+};X)}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+};X)}+\|x\|_{X}.

(v)

The above statements
(ii)
and
(iv)

(
respectively,
(iii))
is true if
(
unweighted
)

L^{1}
-m.r. is replaced by

(
weighted
)

L^{1}_{v}
-m.r. for every non-increasing weight
v

(
respectively, for every
v

which satisfies the assumptions of Proposition
\ref{L1 extrapol var 2})
.

(vi)

If
v(t):=e^{-t}
,
t>0
, then
A
on
X
has
L^{1}_{v}
-m.r. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

Proof.

Combining Lemma
2.18
(i), Proposition
2.23
and Lemma
2.30
(i) we get that

-A
is the generator of a bounded analytic
C_{0}
-semigroup
T
on
X
and
T(t)
is the restriction of
{\mathcal{T}}(t)
to
X
(
t\geq 0
).

We begin with the proof of the statement (iv), which we then apply, by the a rescaling argument, in the proofs of (i) and (ii).

For (iv), by (
14
) and Lemma
2.18
(i), in particular, the Hardy operator
P
is bounded on
\Phi
, and Proposition
2.23
shows that, up to the equivalence of the norm, we have the following Komatsu type representation of
X:=K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})
:

X=\left\{x\in{\mathcal{X}}:\left[(0,\infty)\ni t\mapsto\|{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{T}}(t)x\|_{{\mathcal{X}}}\right]\in\Phi\right\}

and

\|x\|_{X}\simeq[x]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}:=\int_{0}^{\infty}\|{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{T}}(s)x\|_{\mathcal{X}}w(s)\mathrm{d}s,\qquad x\in X.

Moreover, there exists a constant
C_{\mathcal{A}}
such that for all
t,s>0
we have that

\|{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{T}}(t/2){\mathcal{T}}(s)\|_{{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{X}})}=\|{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{T}}((t+s)/{2}){\mathcal{T}}({s}/{2})\|_{{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{X}})}\leq\frac{C_{\mathcal{A}}}{t+s}.

It gives that for all
x\in X

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}[AT(t)x]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left\|{\mathcal{A}}^{2}{\mathcal{T}}(s+t)x\right\|_{\mathcal{X}}w(s)\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C_{\mathcal{A}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{t+s}\|{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{T}}({t}/{2})x\|_{\mathcal{X}}w(s)\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=C_{\mathcal{A}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left[\frac{1}{w(t/2)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{t+s}w(s)\mathrm{d}s\right]\|{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{T}}(t/2)x\|_{\mathcal{X}}w(t/2)\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq 2C_{\mathcal{A}}[w]_{P+Q,L^{1}}\,[x]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}.

For the further references we point out here that we do not make use of the invertibility of
{\mathcal{A}}
in the above estimate.
The fact that
0\in\rho({\mathcal{A}})
yields
\|\cdot\|_{X}\simeq[\cdot]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}
, and the Kalton-Portal characterization, see Theorem
2.8
(with
v\equiv 1
), implies that
A
has
L^{1}
-m.r. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
. The boundedness of the corresponding solution operator
U=U_{A}
follows from Dore’s result
[
21
, Theorem 5.2]
; see also Remark
2.1
(c).
It completes the proof of the point (iv).

For (ii), note that for all
\lambda>0
, the operator
{\mathcal{A}}+\lambda
is the generator of a bounded analytic semigroup and
0\in\rho({\mathcal{A}}+\lambda)
. Moreover,
since
\|x\|_{D_{\mathcal{A}}}\simeq\|x\|_{D_{{\mathcal{A}}+\lambda}}
for
x\in D({\mathcal{A}})=D({\mathcal{A}}+\lambda)
, we have that
X:=K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})=K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},D_{{\mathcal{A}}+\lambda})
with the equivalence of the norms. By (iv) we know that
({\mathcal{A}}+\lambda)_{|X}={\mathcal{A}}_{|X}+\lambda
has
L^{1}
-m.r. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
, which easily gives that
A
has
L^{1}
-m.r. on finite intervals; cf. the proof of Lemma
2.4
(ii). In particular, the condition (
2
) with
p=1
holds; cf. Remark
2.1
(b). Therefore, since
D(A)
is dense in
X
(see Lemma
2.30
(i)), by Lemma
2.2
(ii) and (iii), we get the statement (i). The estimates (
31
) of (ii) follow easily.

For the proof of the homogenous estimates (
32
), as has been stressed in the proof of (iv) above, for all
x\in X
we have

(33)

\int_{0}^{\infty}[AT(t)x]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}\mathrm{d}t\leq C[x]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}

for a constant
C
independent of
x
.
Fix
f\in L^{1}_{loc}([0,\infty);X)
with
[f]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}\in L^{1}
. Since
A
has
L^{1}
-m.r. on finite intervals,
Lemma
2.4
(ii) shows that for a.e.
t>0
the function
(0,t)\ni s\mapsto AT(t-s)f(s)\in X
is Bochner integrable.
For such
t
one can find a sequence of simple functions
g_{n}:(0,t)\rightarrow X
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, such that
g_{n}(s)\rightarrow AT(t-s)f(s)
as
n\rightarrow\infty
and

\|g_{n}(s)\|_{X}\leq 2\|AT(t-s)f(s)\|_{X}\qquad\textrm{for a.e. }s\in(0,t).

In particular, by the (scalar) Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem,

\int_{0}^{t}\|g_{n}(s)-AT(t-s)f(s)\mathrm{d}s\|_{X}\mathrm{d}s\rightarrow 0\qquad\textrm{ as }n\rightarrow\infty.

In addition, combining again Lebesgue’s dominated theorem with Hille’s thoerem,
\|\int_{0}^{t}g_{n}(s)\mathrm{d}s-AUf(t)\|_{X}\rightarrow 0
as
n\rightarrow\infty
.
Since for every simple function
g_{n}
,
\bigl[\int_{0}^{t}g_{n}(s)\mathrm{d}s\bigr]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}=\int_{0}^{t}[g(s)]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}\mathrm{d}s
, for a.e.
t>0
we get that

\displaystyle[AUf(t)]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}

\displaystyle\leq\bigl[A\int_{0}^{t}T(t-s)f(s)\mathrm{d}s-\int_{0}^{t}g_{n}(s)\mathrm{d}s\bigr]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}+\bigl[\int_{0}^{t}g_{n}(s)\mathrm{d}s\bigr]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}

\displaystyle=\bigl[A\int_{0}^{t}T(t-s)f(s)\mathrm{d}s-\int_{0}^{t}g_{n}(s)\mathrm{d}s\bigr]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}+\int_{0}^{t}[g_{n}(s)]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\bigl[A\int_{0}^{t}T(t-s)f(s)\mathrm{d}s-\int_{0}^{t}g_{n}(s)\mathrm{d}s\bigr]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{0}^{t}[g_{n}(s)-AT(t-s)f(s)\mathrm{d}s]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}[AT(t-s)f(s)]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}\mathrm{d}s

Therefore, letting
n\rightarrow\infty
, we obtain

(34)

[AUf(t)]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}\leq\int_{0}^{t}[AT(t-s)f(s)]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}\mathrm{d}s\qquad(\textrm{ a.e. }t>0).

Now, combining the (scalar) Fubini theorem with (
33
) and (
34
), we get

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}[AUf(t)]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\int_{0}^{t}[AT(t-s)f(s)]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\int_{s}^{\infty}[AT(t-s)f(s)]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\int_{0}^{\infty}[AT(t)f(s)]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}[f(s)]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}\mathrm{d}s.

Finally, to show that
[Uf]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}
is bounded, uniformly continuous on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
with the bound on its sup norm as in (
32
),
first note that for all
t>s>0
we have the integral representation formula:

Uf(t)-Uf(s)=\int_{s}^{t}(Uf)^{\prime}(r)\mathrm{d}r,

where the integral exists in
X
. Since
(Uf)^{\prime}=f-AUf
is locally Bochner integrable on
[0,\infty)
, the same argument that we used to get (
34
), shows that for all
t>s>0

\big|\,[Uf(t)]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}-[Uf(s)]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}\,\big|\leq[Uf(t)-Uf(s)]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}\leq\int_{s}^{t}[(Uf)^{\prime}(r)]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}\mathrm{d}r.

Therefore, since
[(Uf)^{\prime}]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}\leq[f]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}+[AUf]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}\in L^{1}
and
Uf(0)=0
, we get that
Uf
is bounded and uniformly continuous with respect to the semi-norm
[\cdot]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}
, with

\sup_{t>0}[Uf(t)]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}\leq C\|[f]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}.

It completes the proof of (
32
) for the initial value
x=0
.

For a non-zero initial value
x\in X
, if
u
is the corresponding strong solution to
(CP)_{A,f,x}
, then
Au(t)=AT(t)x+AUf(t)
for a.e.
t>0
. By (
33
) we get
[Au]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}\in L^{1}
. Since
u^{\prime}=f-Au
a.e. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
, the same argument to the above gives (
32
).
It completes the proof of (ii).

For (iii), consider a function
\phi(\xi):=e^{\frac{-|\xi|^{2}}{2}}

(\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Since
\phi
is a Schwartz function,
\phi\in B^{2\theta}_{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. To prove the lack of
L^{1}
-m.r. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
for the negative Laplacian on
B^{2\theta}_{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, by the Kalton-Portal characterization, it is sufficient to show that
\int_{0}^{\infty}\|\Delta e^{t\Delta}\phi\|_{B^{2\theta}_{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\mathrm{d}t=\infty
. First, note that for
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
t\in(0,\infty)
we have:

(e^{t\Delta}\phi)(\xi)=\frac{1}{(t+1)^{\frac{n}{2}}}e^{\frac{-|\xi|^{2}}{2(t+1)}}.

Since
e^{t\Delta}\phi
is a radial function, we have
\Delta e^{t\Delta}\phi(\xi)=\frac{1}{r^{n-1}}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\left((\cdot)^{n-1}(e^{t\Delta}\phi)^{\prime}\right)
with
r=|\xi|
. Therefore

(\Delta e^{t\Delta}\phi)(\xi)=\frac{1}{(t+1)^{\frac{n}{2}+1}}e^{\frac{-r^{2}}{2(t+1)}}\left(1-\frac{r^{2}}{t+1}\right),\qquad r=|\xi|.

Finally, we get

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\|\Delta e^{t\Delta}\phi\|_{B^{2\theta}_{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\geq\int_{0}^{\infty}\|\Delta e^{t\Delta}\phi\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\gtrsim\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{t^{\frac{n}{2}+1}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left|r^{n-1}e^{\frac{-r^{2}}{2t}}\left(1-\frac{r^{2}}{t}\right)\right|\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{t^{\frac{n-1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}}}{t^{\frac{n}{2}+1}}\int_{0}^{\infty}|e^{-r^{2}}(1-r^{2})|\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}t=\infty.

It completes the proof of (iii).

(v)
It follows from Corollary
2.9
(i) and Proposition
2.12
, respectively.

Finally, for (vi), by Theorem
2.8
, it is sufficient to show that there exists a constant
C
such that for any
x\in X
we have

(35)

\int_{0}^{\infty}\|AT(t)x\|_{\mathcal{X}}e^{-t}\mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{\infty}[AT(t)x]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}e^{-t}\mathrm{d}t\leq C(\|x\|_{\mathcal{X}}+[x]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}).

By Proposition
2.22
, up to the change of the weight function, we can assume that
w(t)=\phi(1,1/t)
,
t>0
with
\phi(1,\cdot)
is quasi-concave. In particular,

\sup_{t>0}e^{-t}/w(t)\leq 1/\phi(1,1)
. It gives that

\int_{0}^{\infty}\|AT(t)x\|_{\mathcal{X}}e^{-t}\mathrm{d}t\leq\frac{1}{\phi(1,1)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\|AT(t)x\|_{\mathcal{X}}w(t)\mathrm{d}t=\frac{1}{\phi(1,1)}[x]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}.

For the second integral in (
35
), we can apply (
33
) already proved in (iv). It completes the proof of (vi).
∎

Remark 3.2
.

(a) By Corollary
2.20
, the conclusions of Theorem
3.1
hold, in particular, for all power weights
w(t):=t^{-\theta}
with
\theta\in(0,1)
, that is, for the parts of
{\mathcal{A}}
in the classical interpolation spaces
({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})_{\theta,1}
with
\theta\in(0,1)
.

(b) Regarding the point (iii) of Theorem
3.1
. By Kalton and Lancien
[
36
, Theorem 3.3]
(see also Fackler
[
22
]
) and Weis’ characterization of the
L^{q}
-m.r. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
(for
q\in(1,\infty)
and the underlying space with the
U\!M\!D
-property) for any
r\in(1,\infty)\setminus\{2\}
on
L^{r}(0,1)=:{\mathcal{X}}
there exists a bounded, injective, sectorial operator
{\mathcal{A}}
with the sectorial angle
\omega_{\mathcal{A}}
equal to
0
and the dense range, which does not have
L^{q}
-m.r. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
for any
q\in(1,\infty)
. Of course,
{\mathcal{A}}
has
L^{q}
-m.r. on any finite interval. By Dore
[
21
, Theorem 7.1]
(or
[
32
, Theorem 17.2.26, p.598]
),
{\mathcal{A}}
does not have
L^{1}
-m.r. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
(and
L^{\infty}
-m.r. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
as well).

Since in this case
D_{\mathcal{A}}\simeq{\mathcal{X}}
, so
X:=({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})_{\theta,q}\simeq X
, the part
A
of
{\mathcal{A}}
in
X
is just
{\mathcal{A}}
. Therefore, this somehow artificial example shows that the assumption that
0\in\rho({\mathcal{A}})
cannot be dropped in Theorem
3.1
(iv) even in the case of
{\mathcal{X}}
being a reflexive Banach space and
{\mathcal{A}}
being a densely defined, injective generator of a bounded analytic semigroup, which has dense range.

In the context of the example provided in (iii) of Theorem
3.1
, since the range of the Laplacian on
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is a subset of the closed subspace
\{f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}):\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}f\mathrm{d}t=0\}\subset L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, its part in
B^{2\theta}_{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
\theta\in(0,1)
, does not have dense range.

Theorem
3.1
should be read in connection to Theorem
2.28
. Note that the function
\phi
which we make use of in the proof of (iii) is not in the range of the Laplacian on
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

(c) Comparing with a recent result on homogeneous estimates
[
51
, Proposition 2.14, Theorem 2.20]
(the case
q=1
), in Theorem
3.1
(ii) we relax the assumptions on the force term
f
in
(CP)_{A,f,x}
showing that
f
can be taken from
L^{1}_{loc}([0,\infty);X)
instead of
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+};X)
.
As the following example shows, our condition is strictly weaker (for
\theta>\frac{1}{2}
) than that in
[
51
, Proposition 2.14]
, see also a comment below
[
51
, Theorem 2.20]
therein.

More precisely, setting
{\mathcal{A}}:=-\Delta
,
{\mathcal{X}}:=L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
,
\Phi:=L^{1}_{w}
for
w(t):=t^{-\theta}(t>0)
,
X:=K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})=({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})_{\theta,1}=B^{2\theta}_{1,1}(\mathbb{R})
, there exists
f\in L^{1}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}_{+};X)
such that
[f]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
, but
f\notin L^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+};X)
. Indeed, let

f(t):=\chi_{(1,\infty)}(t)\frac{1}{t^{2}}e^{-\frac{\xi^{2}}{2t^{2}}},\qquad t>0.

The function
f
is not even in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+};L^{1}(\mathbb{R}))\supsetneq L^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+};B^{2\theta}_{1,1}(\mathbb{R}))
, because

\int_{1}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{t^{2}}e^{-\frac{\xi^{2}}{2t^{2}}}\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}t=\sqrt{2\pi}\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{t}\mathrm{d}t=\infty.

However, following calculations done in
[
32
, Example 17.4.2]
we get

(\Delta e^{s\Delta}f)(\xi)=\frac{1}{t}\frac{1}{(s+t^{2})^{\frac{3}{2}}}e^{-\frac{\xi^{2}}{2(s+t^{2})}}(1-\frac{\xi^{2}}{s+t^{2}}).

It gives

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\|\Delta e^{s\Delta}f\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R})}\frac{1}{s^{\theta}}\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\approx\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\sqrt{s}}\frac{1}{s+t^{2}}\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi}{\sin(\pi\theta)}\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{t^{1+2\theta}}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi}{\sin(\pi\theta)2\theta}.

Therefore,
[f]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
.

3.2.
An extension of the Da Prato-Grisvard theorem for
L^{\infty}
-m.r.

In this subsection we provide a counterpart of Theorem
3.1
for
L^{\infty}
-m.r.

Theorem 3.3
.

Let
-{\mathcal{A}}
be the generator of a bounded analytic semigroup
{\mathcal{T}}
on a Banach space
{\mathcal{X}}
.

Let
w
be a weight function on
(0,\infty)
such that
[w]_{P+Q,L^{\infty}}<\infty

(see,\eqref{P+Q on Linfty})
.

Denote by
A
the part of
{\mathcal{A}}
in
X:=K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})
, where
\Phi:=L^{\infty}(w)
.

Then,
-A
is the generator of a bounded analytic semigroup
T
on
X
and the following assertions hold.

(i)

For any
q\in(1,\infty)
, if
f\in L^{q}_{loc}([0,\infty);X)
and
x\in(X,D_{A})_{\frac{1}{q^{\prime}},q}
, then the function

(36)

u(t):=T(t)x+\int_{0}^{t}T(t-s)f(s)\mathrm{d}s,\qquad t>0,

is a unique strong solution to
(CP)_{A,f,x}
.

(ii)

The operator
A
on
X
has
L^{\infty}
-m.r.  on finite intervals. Consequently, for any
\tau\in(0,\infty)
there exists
a constant
C_{\tau}>0
such that for any
f\in L^{\infty}_{loc}([0,\infty);X)
and
x\in D(A)
the corresponding strong solution
u
to
(CP)_{A,f,x}
satisfies

(ii)

The operator
A
on
X
has
L^{\infty}
-m.r.  on finite intervals. Thus, for any
\tau\in(0,\infty)
there exists a constant
C_{\tau}>0
such
that for any
f\in L^{\infty}_{loc}([0,\infty);X)
and
x\in D(A)
the corresponding strong solution
u
to
(CP)_{A,f,x}
satisfies

\displaystyle\|u^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,\tau;X)}+\|Au\|_{L^{\infty}(0,\tau;X)}

\displaystyle+\|u\|_{Lip([0,\tau];X)}

(37)

\displaystyle\leq C_{\tau}\left(\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(0,\tau;X)}+\|Ax\|_{X}\right).

Furthermore, the following homogenous estimates hold on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
:
there exists a constant
C>0
such that for every
f\in\bigcup_{q>1}L^{q}_{loc}([0,\infty);X)
with
[f(\cdot)]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
and every
x\in D(A)
, the strong solution
u
to
(CP)_{A,f,x}
satisfies

(38)

\|\,[u^{\prime}]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}+\|\,[Au]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}\leq C\left(\|\,[f]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}+[Ax]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}\right)

and for all
t,s>0

[u(t)-u(s)]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}\leq C|t-s|\left(\|\,[f]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}+[Ax]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}\right),

where
[\cdot]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}
stands for the homogeneous part of the norm
\|\cdot\|_{X}
of
X
.

(iii)

If, in addition,
0\in\rho({\mathcal{A}})
,
then
A
on
X
has
L^{\infty}
-m.r. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
and, furthermore, the solution operator
U
is bounded on
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+};X)
.

Consequently, for every
f\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+};X)
and
x\in D(A)
the corresponding strong solution
u
satisfies (
38
) with the norm
\|\cdot\|_{X}
instead of the seminorm
[\cdot]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}
and, in addition,

\|u\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+};X)}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+};X)}+\|x\|_{X}.

(iv)

The above statements
(ii)
and
(iii)
remain true if (unweighted)
L^{\infty}
-m.r. is replaced with (weighted)
L^{\infty}_{v}
-m.r. for every power weight
v(t):=t^{\mu}
with
\mu\in(0,1)
.

Proof.

The proof follows the lines of the proof of Theorem
3.1
. We provide only main supplementary observations which should be made.

Combining Lemma
2.18
(ii), Proposition
2.23
and Lemma
2.30
(ii) we get that
-A
is the generator of a bounded analytic semigroup
T
on
X
and
T(t)
is the restriction of
{\mathcal{T}}(t)
to
X
(
t\geq 0
). If
{\mathcal{A}}
is not bounded operator, then the semigroup
T
is not
C_{0}
-semigroup on
X
, that is,
D(A)
is not dense in
X
.

For (iii), by Lemma
2.18
(ii), the operator
P
(and
Q
) is bounded on
\Phi
. Consequently, by Proposition
2.23
, up to the equivalence of the norm, the space
X:=K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})
can be represented as follows:

X=\big\{x\in{\mathcal{X}}:\,\textrm{ess\,sup}_{t>0}w(t)\|{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{T}}(t)x\|_{{\mathcal{X}}}<\infty\big\}

and

\|x\|_{X}\simeq\|x\|_{\mathcal{X}}+[x]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}=:\|x\|_{\mathcal{X}}+\textrm{ess\,sup}_{t>0}w(t)\|{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{T}}(t)x\|_{{\mathcal{X}}},\qquad x\in{\mathcal{X}}.

Since
\frac{d}{\mathrm{d}t}({\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{T}}(t))={\mathcal{A}}^{2}{\mathcal{T}}(t)
(
t>0
) and
\|{\mathcal{A}}^{2}{\mathcal{T}}(s)\|_{{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{X}})}\lesssim s^{-2}

(s>0)
, for every
t>0
and
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
we obtain that

(39)

-{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{T}}(t)x=\int_{t}^{\infty}{\mathcal{A}}^{2}{\mathcal{T}}(s)x\mathrm{d}s=2\int_{t/2}^{\infty}{\mathcal{A}}^{2}{\mathcal{T}}(2s)x\mathrm{d}s.

Therefore, combining (
39
) with (
17
), for all
t>0
and
x\in X
:

\displaystyle w(t)\|AT(t)x\|_{\mathcal{X}}

\displaystyle\leq 2w(t)\int_{t/2}^{\infty}\|{\mathcal{A}}^{2}{\mathcal{T}}(2s)x\|_{{\mathcal{X}}}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=2w(t)\int_{t/2}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{sw(s)}\textrm{ess\,sup}_{s>t/2}w(s)s\|{\mathcal{A}}^{2}{\mathcal{T}}(2s)x\|_{{\mathcal{X}}}

\displaystyle\leq 6[w]_{P+Q,L^{\infty}}\textrm{ess\,sup}_{s>t/2}w(s)s\|{\mathcal{A}}^{2}{\mathcal{T}}(2s)x\|_{{\mathcal{X}}}.

Moreover, note that

\displaystyle\textrm{ess\,sup}_{s>t/2}w(s)s\|{\mathcal{A}}^{2}{\mathcal{T}}(2s)x\|_{{\mathcal{X}}}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{s>0}\textrm{ess\,sup}_{t>0}w(s)t\|{\mathcal{A}}^{2}{\mathcal{T}}(s+t)x\|_{{\mathcal{X}}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{t>0}\|t{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{T}}(t)x\|_{X}

Therefore, for every
x\in X
we get

(40)

[x]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}\leq 6[w]_{P+Q,L^{\infty}}\sup_{t>0}[tAT(t)x]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}.

For further references, we point out that to get (
40
) we are not making use of the assumption that
0\in\rho(A)
. Assuming it, we get that
[\cdot]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}
is equivalent to
\|\cdot\|_{X}
on
X
, see Proposition
2.23
, so the Kalton-Portal characterization (see Lemma
2.14
), shows that
A
has
L^{\infty}
-m.r. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
. The boundedness of
U
is a direct consequence of Dore
[
21
, Theorem 5.2]
; cf. Remark
2.1
. It completes the statement (iii).

Now we can proceed to the proofs of (i) and (ii). The same rescaling argument as that applied in the proof of Theorem
3.1
(ii) shows that
A
has
L^{\infty}
-m.r. on finite intervals. It gives the first assertion of (ii).
Furthermore, by Dore’s extrapolation result,
[
21
, Theorem 7.1]
, for every
q\in(1,\infty)
,
A
has
L^{q}
-m.r. on finite intervals.

For (i), let
f\in\bigcup_{q>1}L^{q}_{loc}([0,\infty);X)
and
x\in(X,D_{A})_{\frac{1}{q^{\prime}},q}
. Since the function
\mathbb{R}_{+}\ni t\mapsto T(t)x\in X
is bounded and analytic, and its derivative is in
L^{1}_{loc}([0,\infty);X)
,
u
given by (
36
) is a strong solution to
(CP)_{A,f,x}
. It completes the proof of (i).

For the second assertion of (ii), fix
f\in\bigcup_{q>1}L^{q}_{loc}([0,\infty);X)
with
[f]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
and let
x=0
. We already know that
Uf(t)\in D(A)
for a.e.
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
; cf. Remark
2.1
(
2
). For such
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, by (
40
), we have that

[AUf(t)]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}\leq 2[w]_{P+Q,L^{\infty}}\sup_{s>0}[sAT(s)AUf(t)]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}.

Set
C_{\mathcal{A}}:=\sup_{t>0}\|t{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{T}}(t)\|_{{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{X}})}
. Then,

\displaystyle\sup_{s>0}[sAT(s)A

\displaystyle Uf(t)]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{s>0}\textrm{ess\,sup}_{\tau>0}w(\tau)\Bigl\|\int_{0}^{t}s{\mathcal{A}}^{2}{\mathcal{T}}(s+t-r){\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{T}}(\tau)f(r)\mathrm{d}r\Bigr\|_{{\mathcal{X}}}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{s>0}C_{\mathcal{A}}^{2}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{4s}{(s+t-r)^{2}}\textrm{ess\,sup}_{\tau>0}w(\tau)\|{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{T}}(\tau)f(r)\|_{\mathcal{X}}\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=4C_{\mathcal{A}}^{2}\;\textrm{ess\,sup}_{0<r<t}[f(r)]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}.

Therefore,

(41)

\|[AUf]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}\leq 24[w]_{P+Q,L^{\infty}}C^{2}_{\mathcal{A}}\,\|[f]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}.

The similar argument to that of the proof of Theorem
3.1
(ii) shows that
Uf
satisfies Lipschitz’ condition on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
with a constant less or equal to
\|\,[f-AUf]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}
.

For a non-zero initial value
x\in D(A)
,
if
u
denotes the corresponding strong solution to
(CP)_{A,f,x}
, then
for a.e.
t>0
we have

\displaystyle[Au(t)]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}

\displaystyle\leq\textrm{ess\,sup}_{s>0}w(s)\|{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{T}}(s)AT(t)x\|_{\mathcal{X}}+[AUf(t)]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{t>0}\|{\mathcal{T}}(t)\|_{{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{X}})}[Ax]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}+[AUf(t)]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}.

Thus, by (
41
),

\|\,[u^{\prime}]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}\lesssim[Ax]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}+\|\,[f]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}.

It readily gives the desired estimates. Therefore, the proof of (ii) is complete. Finally, the statement (iv) is now a direct consequence of
[
32
, Proposition 17.2.36]
.
∎

Remark 3.4
.

(a) By Corollary
2.20
, the conclusions of Theorem
3.3
hold for all power weights
w(t):=t^{1-\theta}
with
\theta\in(0,1)
, that is, for the parts of
{\mathcal{A}}
in the classical interpolation spaces
({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})_{\theta,\infty}
with
\theta\in(0,1)
.

(b) The estimate (
38
) in which the seminorm
[\cdot]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}
is replaced with the norm
\|\cdot\|_{X}=\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{X}}+[\cdot]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}
, in general, does not hold. More precisely, for any
p\in(1,\infty)\setminus\{2\}
there is a bounded, injective operator
{\mathcal{A}}
on
L^{p}(0,1)=:{\mathcal{X}}
with dense range such that for all
\theta\in(0,1)
there exists a function
f\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+};{\mathcal{X}})
,
AUf\notin L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+};{\mathcal{X}})
, where
Uf(t):=\int_{0}^{t}T(t-s)f(s)\mathrm{d}s

(t\geq 0)
. See Remark
3.2
(b).

(c) We do not know whether for the negative Laplacian
{\mathcal{A}}:=-\Delta
on
{\mathcal{X}}=C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
its part in
X:=({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})_{\theta,\infty}
,
\theta\in(0,1)
, has
L^{\infty}
-m.r. on the whole
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

3.3.
Maximal regularity estimates via completion and extrapolation

In this subsection we provide abstract results which in particular allow to
extrapolate
the
L^{p}
-m.r. estimates with respect to the homogeneous part
[\cdot]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}
of interpolation norm (
21
). Moreover, it throws a light on some relevant results from the literature; see Section
5
.
Roughly, the
L^{p}
-norms with respect to which we measure the time regularity of
[Au]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}
in (ii) of Theorems
3.1
and
3.3
can be replaced by the norm corresponding to a much larger class of Banach function spaces over
(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathrm{d}t)
than the Lebesgue spaces
L^{p}
. It provides a closer correspondence between the time regularity of
[Au]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}
and
[f]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}
.
We make this statement more precise in the following results, relying on an extrapolation result
[
16
, Theorem 5.1]
; cf. also
[
38
, Theorem 5.1 and Corollary 5.1]
.

For the definitions of the
weighted
variant
E_{v}
of an arbitrary rearrangement-invariant Banach function space
E
over
\mathbb{R}_{+}
and its Boyd’s indices
p_{E}
and
q_{E}
, which are involved in the formulation of such extensions, we refer the reader to
[
16
, Section 4]
. We point out that Boyd’s indices can be computed explicitly for many examples of concrete Banach function spaces; see e.g. [7, Chapter 4]. Moreover, a weight
v
on
(0,\infty)
belongs to the class
A^{-}_{p}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
,
p\in(1,\infty)
, if and only if

(42)

\sup_{0\leq a<b<c}\frac{1}{(c-a)^{p}}\int_{b}^{c}w\,\mathrm{d}t\left(\int_{a}^{b}w^{1-p^{\prime}}\mathrm{d}t\right)^{p-1}<\infty

(see
[
16
, Definition 2.1(d)]
; cf. also
[
16
, Theorem 2.3]
). It is readily seen that each class
A^{-}_{p}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
,
p\in(1,\infty)
, contains all non-increasing, positive functions on
(0,\infty)
. Note that for
v\equiv 1
on
(0,\infty)
,
E_{v}=E
with equal norms.

We begin with an observation, which can be easily derived from Lemma
2.3
, and is used in formulating the following result. Namely, for any generator
-A
of the semigroup
T
on a Banach space
X
and for any
X
-valued step function
f
,
Uf(t):=\int_{0}^{t}T(t-s)f(s)\mathrm{d}s\in D(A)
for all
t\geq 0
.

Proposition 3.5
.

Let
p\in[1,\infty)
and let
-A
be the injective generator of a
C_{0}
-semigroup
T
on a Banach space
(X,\|\cdot\|_{X})
. Suppose that
\|\cdot\|_{*}
is another, weaker norm on
X

(
not necessarily complete
)
, such that for some constant
M
and all
x\in X
and
t>0
we have
\|T(t)x\|_{*}\leq M\|x\|_{*}
.

Suppose that
A
satisfies the following
L^{p}
-m.r. estimate on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
with respect to
\|\cdot\|_{*}
: for any step function
f
the mild solution
u:=Uf
to
(CP)_{A,f,0}
satisfies
:

(43)

\bigl\|\,\|Au\|_{*}\bigr\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}\leq C\bigl\|\,\|f\|_{*}\bigr\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}

for some constant
C>0
independent on
f
.

Then, the following statements hold
:

(a)
The canonical extension of
A
on a Banach completion
\widetilde{X}
of
(X,\|\cdot\|_{*})
has
L^{p}
-m.r. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

(b)
If, in addition, the operator
A
has
L^{p}
-m.r. on finite intervals, then for any
f\in L^{1}_{loc}([0,\infty);X)
the corresponding mild solution
u:=Uf
to
(CP)_{A,f,0}

has the right-hand derivative
u^{\prime}_{+}(t)
in
X
for a.e.
t>0
and for a.e.
t>0

u(t)\in D(A)\quad\textrm{ and }\quad u^{\prime}_{+}(t)+Au(t)=f(t).

Moreover, if
f\in\bigcup_{r>1}L_{loc}^{r}([0,\infty);X)

(
or even
f\in L^{1}_{loc}([0,\infty);X)
when
p=1)
, then
u
is a strong solution to
(CP)_{A,f,0}
and for every rearrangement invariant Banach function space
E
with non-trivial Boyd’s indices
p_{E},q_{E}\in(1,\infty)
and for every weight function
v\in A_{p_{E}}^{-}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
there exists a constant
C_{E,v}
such that

(44)

\bigl\|\,\|u^{\prime}\|_{*}\bigr\|_{E_{v}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}+\bigl\|\,\|Au\|_{*}\bigr\|_{E_{v}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}\leq C_{E,v}\bigl\|\,\,\|f\|_{*}\bigr\|_{E_{v}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}.

Proof.

Let
(\widetilde{X},\|\cdot\|_{\widetilde{X}})
be the standard Banach completion of
(X,\|\cdot\|_{*})
, that is,
\widetilde{X}
is a space of all equivalence classes
[\mathbf{x}]
of Cauchy sequences
\mathbf{x}:=(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\in X^{\mathbb{N}}
with respect to
\|\cdot\|_{*}
, and
\|[\mathbf{x}]\|_{\widetilde{X}}:=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\|x_{n}\|_{*}
. Recall that the map
J:X\ni x\mapsto[(x)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}]\in\widetilde{X}
is an isometric isomorphism of
(X,\|\cdot\|_{*})
onto a dense subspace of
\widetilde{X}
.

From the boundedness of
T
on
(X,\|\cdot\|_{*})
, it is easily seen that for all
t>0
the formula

\widetilde{T}(t)[(x_{n})]:=[(T(t)x_{n})],\qquad[(x_{n})]\in\widetilde{X},

defines a family of uniformly bounded operators
\widetilde{T}(t)
,
t>0
, on
\widetilde{X}
, which satisfies the semigroup property. Since
\widetilde{T}(t)Jx=JT(t)x
for all
t>0
and
x\in X
, the density
JX
in
\widetilde{X}
gives that
\widetilde{T}
is a
C_{0}
-semigroup on
\widetilde{X}
. We denote the negative generator of
\widetilde{T}
by
\widetilde{A}
. Therefore, Lemma
2.2
applies to
\widetilde{A}
. For the simplicity, we set
\widetilde{U}
instead of
U_{\widetilde{A}}
(resp.
U
instead of
U_{A}
) to denote the solution operator associated with
\widetilde{A}
on
\widetilde{X}
(resp.
A
on
X
).

It is clear that the function
Jf
is in
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\widetilde{X})
and
JUf(t)=\widetilde{U}Jf(t)
for all
t>0
. Moreover, for any
x\in X
and for
\lambda>0
large enough, we have
J(\lambda+A)^{-1}x=(\lambda+\widetilde{A})^{-1}Jx
. Hence, for any
x\in D(A)
, taking
J
on the both sides of the equation

\lambda(\lambda+A)^{-1}x-x=(\lambda+A)^{-1}Ax

gives

\lambda(\lambda+\widetilde{A})^{-1}Jx-Jx=(\lambda+\widetilde{A})^{-1}JAx,

which is equivalent to
Jx\in D(\widetilde{A})
and
\widetilde{A}Jx=JAx
. Applying it to
x=Uf(t)\in D(A)
, we get that
JUf(t)=\widetilde{U}Jf(t)\in D(\widetilde{A})
and
\widetilde{A}\widetilde{U}Jf(t)=JAUf(t)
for a.e.
t>0
.
Therefore, for any step function
f:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to X
we have

\widetilde{U}Jf(t)\in D(\widetilde{A})\textrm{ for a.e. }t>0\quad\textrm{ and }\quad\|\widetilde{A}\widetilde{U}Jf\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}_{+};{\widetilde{X}})}\leq C\|Jf\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}_{+};{\widetilde{X}})}.

We show that it holds for all functions
\tilde{f}\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\widetilde{X})
. Then, by Lemma
2.2
(ii),
\widetilde{A}
on
\widetilde{X}
has
L^{p}
-m.r. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Note that the set

\bigl\{\tilde{f}\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\widetilde{X}):\widetilde{U}\tilde{f}(t)\in D(\widetilde{A})\textrm{ a.e. }t>0\textrm{ and }\|\widetilde{A}\widetilde{U}\tilde{f}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\widetilde{X})}\leq C\|\tilde{f}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\widetilde{X})}\bigr\}

is closed in
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\widetilde{X})
.
Indeed, suppose that a function
g\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\widetilde{X})
can be approximated in the norm of
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\widetilde{X})
by functions
g_{n}
from this set. Then,
\widetilde{A}\widetilde{U}g_{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, is a Cauchy sequence in
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\widetilde{X})
. This implies that
\widetilde{A}\widetilde{U}g_{n}(t)
converges, modulo taking a subsequence, almost everywhere on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
. In particular, by the closedness of
\widetilde{A}
, we get that
\widetilde{U}g(t)\in D(\widetilde{A})
and
\widetilde{A}\widetilde{U}g(t)=\lim_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\widetilde{A}\widetilde{U}g_{n}(t)
for a.e.
t>0
. By Fatou’s property of
L^{p}
, we get
\|\widetilde{A}\widetilde{U}g\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\widetilde{X})}\leq C\|g\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\widetilde{X})}
. It shows the desired closedness.
Since
JX
is a dense subset of
\widetilde{X}
, it completes the proof of (a).

The first assertion of (b) directly follows from Proposition
2.6
and the observation provided in Remark
2.7
.
For the second one, first note that by Dore’s extrapolation result
[
21
, Theorem 7.1]
(resp. combined with (a)), the operator
A
on
X
(resp.
\widetilde{A}
on
\widetilde{X}
) has
L^{r}
-m.r. on finite intervals (resp. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
) for every
r\in\{p\}\cup(1,\infty)
.
In particular, if
f\in\bigcup_{r>1}L_{loc}^{r}([0,\infty);X)
(or even
f\in L^{1}_{loc}([0,\infty);X)
when
p=1
),
Uf
is a strong solution to
(CP)_{A,f,0}
. Consequently,
\widetilde{U}Jf=JUf
is a strong solution to
(CP)_{\widetilde{A},Jf,0}
and, since the norm
\|\cdot\|_{*}
is weaker than
\|\cdot\|_{X}
,
(\widetilde{U}Jf)^{\prime}=J(Uf)^{\prime}
and
JAUf=\widetilde{A}\widetilde{U}Jf
. Therefore, applying
[
16
, Theorem 5.1]
(see also
[
16
, Theorem 4.3]
) to
\widetilde{A}
on
\widetilde{X}
, if (in addition)
Jf\in E_{v}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\widetilde{X})
, i.e.
\|Jf\|_{\widetilde{X}}=\|f\|_{*}\in E_{v}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
, then
\widetilde{A}\widetilde{U}Jf
and
(\widetilde{U}Jf)^{\prime}
are in
E_{v}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\widetilde{X})
and (
44
) holds. In the other case, that is, when
f\notin E_{v}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\widetilde{X})
,
\|\,\|f\|_{*}\|_{E_{v}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}=\infty
, the estimate (
44
) is obviously valid. Thus, the proof is complete.
∎

It is not clear whether the direct counterpart of the above result holds for the case of
p=\infty
. We prove the variant for the
L^{\infty}
-m.r. estimates under slightly modified assumptions, relaying on Lemma
2.14
. The assumptions stated in the following result can be verified in the context we are interested in.
Recall that
S_{\omega}:=\{z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}:|\arg z|<\omega\}
; see Subsection
2.7
.

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
-A
be the injective generator of a
C_{0}
-semigroup
T
on a Banach space
(X,\|\cdot\|_{X})
, which admits a bounded holomorphic extension on a sector
S_{\omega}
for some
\omega>0
. Suppose that
\|\cdot\|_{*}
is another, weaker norm on
X

(
not necessarily complete
)
, such that for some constant
M
and all
x\in X
and
z\in S_{\omega}
we have
\|T(z)x\|_{*}\leq M\|x\|_{*}
. Let
\widetilde{X}
denote the Banach completion of
(X,\|\cdot\|_{*})
.

Suppose that there exists a constant
C>0
such that for every
x\in X
:

\|x\|_{*}\leq C\sup_{t>0}\|tAT(t)x\|_{*}+\limsup_{t\rightarrow\infty}\|T(t)x\|_{*}.

Then, the canonical extension of
A
on
\widetilde{X}
has
L^{\infty}
-m.r. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

In addition, if
A
on
X
has
L^{\infty}
-m.r. on finite intervals, then the statement
(
b
)
of Proposition
3.5
holds.

Proof.

As in the proof of Proposition
3.5
one can show that for all
z\in S:=S_{\omega}
the formula

\widetilde{T}(z)[(x_{n})]:=[(T(z)x_{n})],\quad[(x_{n})]\in\widetilde{X},

defines a family of uniformly bounded operators
\widetilde{T}(z)
,
z\in S
, on
\widetilde{X}
, which satisfies the semigroup property. Since
JX
is dense in
\widetilde{X}
and
\widetilde{T}(z)Jx=JT(z)x
for all
z\in S
and
x\in X
, by Vitali’s theorem
[
4
, Theorem A.5, p.458]
, for all
\mathbf{x}\in\widetilde{X}
the function
S\ni z\mapsto\widetilde{T}(z)\mathbf{x}\in\widetilde{X}
is holomorphic. Therefore, by
[
4
, Proposition A.3]
, we get that
\widetilde{T}
is bounded holomorphic
C_{0}
-semigroup on
\widetilde{X}
. We denote the negative generator of
\widetilde{T}
by
\widetilde{A}
.
In particular,
\widetilde{M}:=\sup_{t>0}\|t\widetilde{A}\widetilde{T}(t)\|_{{\mathcal{L}}(\widetilde{X})}<\infty
.
As in the proof of the case when
p<\infty
, for any
x\in D(A)
and
\lambda>0
large enough, we have
Jx\in D(\widetilde{A})
and
\widetilde{A}Jx=JAx
. Take any
\mathbf{x}\in\widetilde{X}
and
x\in X
. Then,

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{x}\|_{\widetilde{X}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathbf{x}-Jx\|_{\widetilde{X}}+\|x\|_{*}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathbf{x}-Jx\|_{\widetilde{X}}+C\sup_{t>0}\|tAT(t)x\|_{*}+\limsup_{t\rightarrow\infty}\|T(t)x\|_{*}

\displaystyle=\|\mathbf{x}-Jx\|_{\widetilde{X}}+C\sup_{t>0}\|t\widetilde{A}\widetilde{T}(t)x\|_{\widetilde{X}}+\limsup_{t\rightarrow\infty}\|\widetilde{T}(t)Jx\|_{\widetilde{X}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathbf{x}-Jx\|_{\widetilde{X}}+C\sup_{t>0}\left(\|t\widetilde{A}\widetilde{T}(t)(\mathbf{x}-Jx)\|_{\widetilde{X}}+\|t\widetilde{A}\widetilde{T}(t)x\|_{\widetilde{X}}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\limsup_{t\rightarrow\infty}\left(\|\widetilde{T}(t)(Jx-\mathbf{x})\|_{\widetilde{X}}+\|\widetilde{T}(t)\mathbf{x}\|_{\widetilde{X}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq(1+C\widetilde{M}+M)\|\mathbf{x}-Jx\|_{\widetilde{X}}+C\sup_{t>0}\|t\widetilde{A}\widetilde{T}(t)\mathbf{x}\|_{\widetilde{X}}+\limsup_{t\rightarrow\infty}\|\widetilde{T}(t)\mathbf{x}\|_{\widetilde{X}}.

Hence, since
JX
is dense in
\widetilde{X}
, by Lemma
2.14
,
\widetilde{A}
has
L^{\infty}
-m.r. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.
Finally, the proof of the additional statement follows the same arguments used in the proof of Proposition
3.5
(b).
∎

Remark 3.7
.

The natural question arises whether the force term
f
in Proposition
3.5
(b) (in the case
p\neq 1
) can be taken from
L^{1}_{loc}([0,\infty);X)
to obtain the corresponding conclusions on the homogeneous estimates therein.
This question connects with Proposition
2.6
and Remark
2.7
. We do not know if the mild solution
Uf
with
f\in L^{1}_{loc}([0,\infty);X)\setminus\bigcup_{r>1}L_{loc}^{r}([0,\infty);X)
has the full strong derivative a.e. in
X
even if
A
is densely defined. In the setting of Theorem
3.3
(ii), the operator
A
is not densely defined; see Lemma
2.30
(ii). However, Proposition
3.6
remains applicable to the part of
A
in the closure of its domain.

Corollary 3.8
.

Let
-{\mathcal{A}}
be an injective generator of a bounded analytic semigroup on
{\mathcal{X}}
. Let
p\in[1,\infty)
and
\theta\in(0,1)
. Let
A
denote the part of
{\mathcal{A}}
in
X:=K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},D_{{\mathcal{A}}})
with
\Phi:=L^{p}(\mathbb{R}_{+},t^{-p(1-\theta)-1}\mathrm{d}t)
.

Then, for every rearrangement invariant Banach function space
E
with non-trivial Boyd’s indices
p_{E},q_{E}\in(1,\infty)
and for every weight function
v\in A_{p_{E}}^{-}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
there exists a constant
C_{E,v}
such that for any
f\in\bigcup_{r>1}L_{loc}^{r}([0,\infty);X)

(
or even
f\in L^{1}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}_{+};X)
when
p=1)
, the corresponding strong solution
u
to
(CP)_{A,f,0}
satisfies

(45)

\|\,[u^{\prime}]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}\|_{E_{v}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}+\|\,[Au]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}\|_{E_{v}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}\leq C_{E,v}\|\,[f]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}\|_{E_{v}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}.

A similar result holds for
p=\infty
as well. We leave the detailed formulation to the reader; cf. Remark
3.7
.

Proof.

Since
{\mathcal{A}}
is injective,
\|\cdot\|_{*}:=[\cdot]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}
is a weaker norm on
(X,\|\cdot\|_{X})
. By Lemma
2.30
, the semigroup
T
generated by the part
A
of
{\mathcal{A}}
in
X
is a
C_{0}
-semigroup on
X
. Moreover, for all
x\in X
and
t>0

\|T(t)x\|_{*}:=\left\|\,\|{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{T}}(\cdot)T(t)x\|_{\mathcal{X}}\right\|_{\Phi}\leq\|{\mathcal{T}}(t)\|_{{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{X}})}\|x\|_{*}.

The proof of the classical Da Prato-Grisvard theorem, as presented, e.g. in [45, Theorem 3.5.8, p. 146], shows that the estimate (
43
) holds for
p\in[1,\infty)
(for the case of
p=1
, which is omitted therein, see, e.g. (
33
)). Since
A
has
L^{p}
-m.r. on finite intervals, by Proposition
3.5
(b), we obtain the desired claim.
∎

Our next result sheds light on the Banach completion of the space
X
equipped with the homogeneous part of the corresponding interpolation norm
[\cdot]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}
, which is involved in the formulation of the corollary above.
It also completes Proposition
2.23
(see the statements (i) and (ii)) and we utilize it in the remaining part of this section (see Proposition
3.16
), as well as in Section 5 (see Proposition
5.2
). All the completions considered in the statement (iii) below are standard Banach completions.

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
-{\mathcal{A}}
be the generator of a bounded semigroup
{\mathcal{T}}
on a Banach space
{\mathcal{X}}
.
Let
\dot{D}_{\mathcal{A}}
denote the domain of
{\mathcal{A}}
equipped with the seminorm
\|x\|_{\dot{D}_{\mathcal{A}}}:=\|{\mathcal{A}}x\|_{{\mathcal{X}}}
,
x\in D({\mathcal{A}})
. For all
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
and
t>0
let

K(t,x,{\mathcal{X}},\dot{D}_{\mathcal{A}})=\inf\{\|y\|_{\mathcal{X}}+t\|z\|_{\dot{D}_{{\mathcal{A}}}}:y\in{\mathcal{X}},\,z\in D({\mathcal{A}}),\,x=y+z\}.

Then the following assertions hold.

(i)

For any
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
and
t>0

(46)

C^{-1}\|{\mathcal{T}}(t)x-x\|_{{\mathcal{X}}}\leq K(t,x,{\mathcal{X}},\dot{D}_{\mathcal{A}})\leq\Big\|\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}({\mathcal{T}}(s)x-x)\mathrm{d}s\Big\|_{\mathcal{X}}+\|{\mathcal{T}}(t)x-x\|_{\mathcal{X}},

where
C:=\sup_{s\geq 0}\|{\mathcal{T}}(s)\|_{{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{X}})}+1
.
In particular,

\omega_{{\mathcal{A}}}(t,x):=\sup_{0<s\leq t}\|{\mathcal{T}}(s)x-x\|_{{\mathcal{X}}}\simeq K(t,x,{\mathcal{X}},\dot{D}_{\mathcal{A}}),\qquad t>0,\,x\in{\mathcal{X}}.

If, in addition,
{\mathcal{A}}
is injective, then the pair
({\mathcal{X}},\dot{D}_{\mathcal{A}})
forms an interpolation couple of normed spaces.

(ii)

Let
\Phi
be a parameter of the
K
-method. Then

K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},\dot{D}_{\mathcal{A}}):=\bigl\{x\in{\mathcal{X}}:(\cdot)^{-1}K(\cdot,x;{\mathcal{X}},\dot{D}_{\mathcal{A}})\in\Phi\bigr\}=K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})

(
as sets
)
. If, in addition, the Hardy operator
P
is bounded on
\Phi
, then for all
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
:

\bigl\|\,(\cdot)^{-1}{\mathcal{T}}(\cdot)x-x\|_{\mathcal{X}}\bigr\|_{\Phi}\simeq\bigl\|\,(\cdot)^{-1}\omega_{{\mathcal{A}}}(\cdot,x)\bigr\|_{\Phi}\simeq\bigl\|\,\|{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{T}}(\cdot)x\|_{{\mathcal{X}}}\bigr\|_{\Phi}.

(iii)

Let, in addition,
{\mathcal{A}}
be injective and
\Phi
be a Banach function space with an absolutely continuous norm.
Suppose that there exists a Hausdorff topological vector spaces
Y
and two injective linear maps
J:{\mathcal{X}}\rightarrow Y
and
\kappa:\widetilde{\dot{D}_{\mathcal{A}}}\rightarrow Y
such that for any
x\in D({\mathcal{A}})
we have
\kappa\mathbf{x}=Jx
, where
\mathbf{x}=[(x)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}]
.

Let
J{\mathcal{X}}
and
{\mathcal{Z}}:=\kappa\,\widetilde{\dot{D}_{\mathcal{A}}}
be equipped with the image norms, that is,
\|Jx\|_{J{\mathcal{X}}}:=\|x\|_{\mathcal{X}}
for all
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
and
\|\kappa\mathbf{x}\|_{{\mathcal{Z}}}:=\bigl\|\mathbf{x}\bigr\|_{\widetilde{\dot{D}_{\mathcal{A}}}}
for all
\mathbf{x}\in\widetilde{\dot{D}_{\mathcal{A}}}
.

Suppose that
J{\mathcal{X}}\cap\mathcal{Z}=JD({\mathcal{A}})
. Then, the completion of
K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},\dot{D}_{\mathcal{A}})
is topologically isomorphic to
K_{\Phi}(J{\mathcal{X}}^{0},\mathcal{Z})
, where
{\mathcal{X}}^{0}
is the closure of
D({\mathcal{A}})
in
{\mathcal{X}}
.

Proof.

(i)
Let
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
. Take
y\in{\mathcal{X}}
and
z\in D({\mathcal{A}})
with
x=y+z
. By Lemma
2.3
and Hille’s theorem we get for all
t>0
that

z-{\mathcal{T}}(t)z={\mathcal{A}}\int^{t}_{0}{\mathcal{T}}(s)z\mathrm{d}s=\int^{t}_{0}{\mathcal{T}}(s){\mathcal{A}}z\mathrm{d}s.

Therefore, for all
t>0
,

(47)

\displaystyle\|{\mathcal{T}}(t)x-x\|_{{\mathcal{X}}}

\displaystyle\leq\|{\mathcal{T}}(t)-I\|_{{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{X}})}\|y\|_{\mathcal{X}}+t\sup_{0<s\leq t}\|{\mathcal{T}}(s)\|_{{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{X}})}\|{\mathcal{A}}z\|_{{\mathcal{X}}}

\displaystyle\leq\bigl(1+\sup_{0<s\leq t}\|{\mathcal{T}}(s)\|_{{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{X}})}\bigr)K(t,x,{\mathcal{X}},\dot{D}_{\mathcal{A}}).

It yields the left hand-side of (
46
).
For the right hand-side, again by Lemma
2.3
, for any
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
and
t>0
, taking
y:=\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}(x-{\mathcal{T}}(s)x)\mathrm{d}s\in{\mathcal{X}}
and
z:=\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}{\mathcal{T}}(s)x\mathrm{d}s\in D({\mathcal{A}})
, we easily get the desired estimate in (
46
). Further, since
K(\cdot,x,{\mathcal{X}},\dot{D}_{{\mathcal{A}}})
is non-decreasing on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
, (
46
) gives the statement about
\omega_{\mathcal{A}}(\cdot,\cdot)
.

The last statement of (i) follows from Proposition
3.15
(i) below, but the following direct argument also can be applied. Let
N(x):=K(1,x,{\mathcal{X}},\dot{D}_{\mathcal{A}})
,
x\in X
.
To show that
N
is a norm on
{\mathcal{X}}
it suffices to deduce that for all
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
,
N(x)=0
implies
x=0
. Then,
{\mathcal{X}}
equipped with
N
is a normed space such that
{\mathcal{X}}
and
\dot{D}_{\mathcal{A}}
are continuously embedded in it, hence
({\mathcal{X}},\dot{D}_{\mathcal{A}})
is an interpolation couple. By (
46
) we get for all
t>0
that

(48)

\displaystyle\|{\mathcal{T}}(t)x-x\|_{{\mathcal{X}}}\leq(1+t)\bigl(1+\sup_{0<s\leq t}\|{\mathcal{T}}(s)\|_{{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{X}})}\bigr)N(x)=0.

Therefore,
{\mathcal{T}}(t)x=x
for all
t>0
. Consequently,
x\in D({\mathcal{A}})
with
{\mathcal{A}}x=0
, and, by the injectivity of
{\mathcal{A}}
, we get
x=0
. It completes the proof of (i).

For (ii), note that for all
t>0

\Big\|\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}({\mathcal{T}}(s)x-x)\mathrm{d}s\Big\|_{\mathcal{X}}\leq tP\bigl({(\cdot)^{-1}}\|{\mathcal{T}}(\cdot)x-x\|_{\mathcal{X}}\bigr)(t).

Therefore, (ii) follows directly from (i) and the presentation of the proof of Proposition
2.23
, which shows that for all
t>0
and
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
we have

(49)

\widetilde{C}^{-1}\|{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{T}}(t)\|_{{\mathcal{X}}}\leq\frac{1}{t}K(t,x,{\mathcal{X}},\dot{D}_{{\mathcal{A}}})\leq\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}\|{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{T}}(s)x\|_{{\mathcal{X}}}\mathrm{d}s+\|{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{T}}(t)x\|_{{\mathcal{X}}},

where
\widetilde{C}:=\max(\sup_{s>0}\|s{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{T}}(s)\|_{{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{X}})},\sup_{s>0}\|{\mathcal{T}}(s)\|_{{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{X}})})
; cf. Subsection
2.7
.
(iii) First, from the assumption on
\Phi
,
D({\mathcal{A}})
is a dense subset of
K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},\dot{D}_{\mathcal{A}})=K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}}^{0},\dot{D}_{\mathcal{A}})
and
\mathcal{Z}\cap J{\mathcal{X}}^{0}
is a dense subset of
K_{\Phi}(J{\mathcal{X}}^{0},\mathcal{Z})
; see the proof of Proposition
2.30
.
Since the set
\{[(x)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}]:x\in D({A})\}
is a dense subset of
\widetilde{\dot{D}_{\mathcal{A}}}
and the isomorphism
\kappa
between this completion and
{\mathcal{Z}}
is compatible with
J
, we get that
JD({\mathcal{A}})
is dense in
\mathcal{Z}
. Of course,
JD({\mathcal{A}})
is also dense in
J{\mathcal{X}}^{0}
, and consequently
JD({\mathcal{A}})\subset JK_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},\dot{D}_{\mathcal{A}})
is a dense subset of a Banach space
K_{\Phi}(J{\mathcal{X}},\mathcal{Z})
.
Therefore, by Lemma
5.1
, it is sufficient to show that
there exists a constant
c>0
such that for any
x\in D({\mathcal{A}})
:

{c}^{-1}\|x\|_{K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},\dot{D}_{\mathcal{A}})}\leq\|Jx\|_{K_{\Phi}(J{\mathcal{X}},\mathcal{Z})}\leq c\|x\|_{K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},\dot{D}_{\mathcal{A}})}.

By our assumption
J{\mathcal{X}}\cap\mathcal{Z}=JD({\mathcal{A}})
, one can show even more, namely, for any
x\in D({\mathcal{A}})
and all
t>0
:

K(t,x,{\mathcal{X}},\dot{D}_{\mathcal{A}})=K(t,x,J{\mathcal{X}},\mathcal{Z}).

Indeed, for any
x\in D({\mathcal{A}})
, if
x^{0}\in{\mathcal{X}}^{0}
and
z\in\mathcal{Z}
are such that
Jx=Jx^{0}+z
, then, by our assumptions, there exists
x^{1}\in D({\mathcal{A}})
with
z=Jx^{1}
and
\|z\|_{\mathcal{Z}}=\|x^{1}\|_{\dot{D}_{{\mathcal{A}}}}
. It easily leads to the desired equality for the corresponding
K
-functionals, and completes the proof of (iii).
∎

Remark 3.10
.

The point (iii) of Lemma
3.9
shows that our approach to studying global-in-time estimates for the strong solutions of
(CP)_{A,f,x}
, with respect to the homogeneous parts of interpolation norms, is an alternative to that proposed in
[
51
, Section 2]
.
We proceed to the completion after interpolation. In fact, in
[
51
]
the procedure is the opposite; in a sense, the normed space
Y
considered therein can naturally be taken as a subspace of the completion of
D({\mathcal{A}})
in the norm
\|{\mathcal{A}}\cdot\|_{\mathcal{X}}
.
Following the setting of Proposition
3.5
, if the force term
f
satisfies, e.g. the assumptions of
[
51
, Proposition 2.14]
, this is,
f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}_{+};X)
with
[f]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}_{+})

(p\in[1,\infty])
, then
Jf\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\widetilde{X})
. Hence, knowing that
\widetilde{A}
on
\widetilde{X}
has
L^{p}
-maximal regularity on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
, we get that
(U_{\widetilde{A}}Jf)^{\prime}
is in
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\widetilde{X})
. Since
u:=U_{\mathcal{A}}f
is a strong solution to
(CP)_{A,f,0}
with
u,u^{\prime}\in L^{p}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}_{+};X)
, we get that
[u^{\prime}(t)]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}=\|J(U_{A}f)^{\prime}(t)\|_{\widetilde{X}}=\|(U_{\widetilde{A}}Jf)^{\prime}(t)\|_{\widetilde{X}}
for a.e.
t>0
, that is,
[u^{\prime}]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
, which is a desired conclusion therein.

In the proofs of the estimates in (
45
), we use the Banach completion as a tool. To make statement (a) of Proposition
3.5
more adequate for application, it is useful to ensure that if a normed space
X
is a subspace of a Hausdorff vector space
Y
then there exists a completion of
X
in
Y
. That is,
a completion
\widetilde{X}
such that
\widetilde{X}\subset Y
, where the inclusion map is continuous (denoted
\widetilde{X}\hookrightarrow Y
). If such a completion of
X
in
Y
exists
we also say that
X
admits a completion in
Y
.
Following
[
5
]
, a
completion
of
X
is a Banach space
\widetilde{X}
containing an isometric copy of
X
as a dense subspace and satisfying

\|x\|_{\widetilde{X}}=\|x\|_{X}
for all
x\in X
.
The problem of the existence of a completion of
X
in
Y
is resolved by the following result.

Theorem 3.11

(
[
5
, Theorem 3.1]
)
.

If a normed space
X
is a subspace of a Hausdorff vector space
Y
