, then
X
admits a completion in
Y
if and only if the following conditions are satisfied
:

(i)

X\hookrightarrow Y
and every Cauchy sequence in
X
converges in
Y
.

(ii)

every Cauchy sequence in
X
convergent to
0
in
Y
is also convergent to
0
in
X
.

Note that Theorem
3.11
is stated in
[
5
]
without a proof. As we require certain details, we outline the essential steps of the proof briefly in the following lemma leaving the remaining details to the reader. Let
X
be a normed space and
Y=(Y,\tau)
a Hausdorff vector space such that
X\hookrightarrow Y
.
The
Cauchy completion

X^{ac,Y}
(abbreviated as
X^{ac}
) is defined as the space of all
x\in Y
for which there exists a Cauchy sequence
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in
X
that converges to
x
in
Y
. It should be noted that
X^{ac}
is, in general, not a completion of
X
in the classical sense. It is straightforward to verify that
X^{ac}
is a normed space, equipped with the norm

\|x\|_{X^{ac}}:=\inf_{(x_{n})}\sup_{n\geq 1}\|x_{n}\|_{X},

where the infimum is taken over all sequences
(x_{n})
satisfying the aforementioned conditions.

Lemma 3.12
.

Let
X
be a normed space and
Y=(Y,\tau)
a Hausdorff vector space. If
X\hookrightarrow Y
and every Cauchy sequence in
X
converges in
Y
, then
X\hookrightarrow X^{ac}\hookrightarrow Y
, and the inclusion map
{\rm{id}}\colon X\to X^{ac}
has norm at most
1
. Moreover,
X^{ac}
is a Banach space. If, in addition, every Cauchy sequence in
X
that converges to
0
in
Y
also converges to
0
in
X
, then
X^{ac}
is the completion of
X
in
Y
.

We also require the following result. For clarity, all Cauchy completions below are considered in
Y
. Additionally, we use the symbol ’
\overset{1}{\hookrightarrow}
’ to indicate that the inclusion map has norm less than or equal to
1
.

Lemma 3.13
.

Let
X_{0}
and
X_{1}
be normed spaces, and let
Y=(Y,\tau)
be a Hausdorff vector space such that
X_{j}\hookrightarrow Y
and every Cauchy sequence in
X_{j}
converges in
Y
for
j=0,1
. Then, the pair
(X_{0}^{ac},X_{1}^{ac})
forms a Banach couple such that
X_{0}^{ac}+X_{1}^{ac}=(X_{0}+X_{1})^{ac}
with equal norms.

Proof.

From Lemma
3.12
, it follows that
X_{j}^{ac}
are Banach spaces and
X_{j}^{ac}\hookrightarrow Y
(
j=0,1
).
Thus,
(X_{0}^{ac},X_{1}^{ac})
forms a Banach couple. To show the required isometric formula, we start with an auxiliary observation. Suppose that
(Z_{0},Z_{1})
is a couple of normed spaces which satisfies the same conditions as
(X_{0},X_{1})
and, in addition,
Z_{0}\overset{1}{\hookrightarrow}Z_{1}
.
We claim that

(50)

Z_{0}^{ac}\overset{1}{\hookrightarrow}Z_{1}^{ac}\,.

Clearly, we have
Z_{0}\overset{1}{\hookrightarrow}Z_{1}\overset{1}{\hookrightarrow}Z_{1}^{ac}\,
. For a given
x\in Z_{0}^{ac}
, choose an arbitrary Cauchy sequence
(x_{n})
in
Z_{0}
such that
x_{n}\to x
in
Y
.
By the completeness of
Z_{1}^{ac}
, it follows that
x_{n}\to y
in
Z_{1}^{ac}
and so
x_{n}\to y
in
Y
. Hence
y=x
and

\|x\|_{Z_{1}^{ac}}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\|x_{n}\|_{Z_{1}^{ac}}\leq\sup_{n\geq 1}\|x_{n}\|_{Z_{0}}\,.

This estimate, combined with the fact that
(x_{n})
is an arbitrary sequence in
Z_{0}
with the mentioned properties, implies that

x\in Z_{1}^{ac}
with

\|x\|_{Z_{1}^{ac}}\leq\|x\|_{Z_{0}^{ac}}\,,

and so the claim follows.

To establish
X_{0}^{ac}+X_{1}^{ac}=(X_{0}+X_{1})^{ac}
(equal norms)
observe that
X_{j}\overset{1}{\hookrightarrow}X_{j}^{ac}
(
j=0,1
) yields

(51)

X_{0}+X_{1}\overset{1}{\hookrightarrow}X_{0}^{ac}+X_{1}^{ac}\,.

Since
X:=X_{0}^{ac}+X_{1}^{ac}
is a Banach space, its Cauchy completion in
Y
coincides isometrically with
X
.
Combining this with (
51
), we obtain

(X_{0}+X_{1})^{ac}\overset{1}{\hookrightarrow}X_{0}^{ac}+X_{1}^{ac}\,.

Now, observe that
X_{0}+X_{1}
is a normed space and
X_{j}\overset{1}{\hookrightarrow}X_{0}+X_{1}
(
j=0,1
). Then, by applying the first part of the proof to
(Z_{0},Z_{1})=(X_{j},X_{0}+X_{1})
,
j=0,1
,
we obtain
X_{j}^{ac}\overset{1}{\hookrightarrow}(X_{0}+X_{1})^{ac}
. Consequently, this yields the reverse inclusion

X_{0}^{ac}+X_{1}^{ac}\overset{1}{\hookrightarrow}(X_{0}+X_{1})^{ac}\,,

which completes the proof.
∎

As an application, we obtain the following corollary.

Proposition 3.14
.

Let
\vec{X}=(X_{0},X_{1})
be a normed couple and let
Y=(Y,\tau)
be a sequentially complete Hausdorff vector space such that

X_{j}\hookrightarrow Y
for
j=0,1
. If
(X_{0}+X_{1})^{ac}
is a completion of
X_{0}+X_{1}
in
Y
, then for
any parameter
\Phi
of the
K
-method, the following statements hold
:

(i)

The normed space
K_{\Phi}(\vec{X})
admits a completion in
Y
. More precisely, the space
K_{\Phi}(\vec{X})^{ac}\subset Y
is a completion of
K_{\Phi}(\vec{X})
.

(ii)

The inclusion map from
{K_{\Phi}(\vec{X})}^{ac}
into
K_{\Phi}(X_{0}^{ac},X_{1}^{ac})
is an isometry.
If, in addition,

K_{\Phi}(\vec{X})\text{ is dense in }K_{\Phi}(X_{0}^{ac},X_{1}^{ac}),

then

(52)

K_{\Phi}(\vec{X})^{ac}=K_{\Phi}(X_{0}^{ac},X_{1}^{ac})

with equal norms. In particular, if
K_{\Phi}
is a regular functor and
X_{0}^{ac}\cap X_{1}^{ac}=(X_{0}\cap X_{1})^{ac}
, then (
52
) is true.

Note that the condition
X_{0}^{ac}\cap X_{1}^{ac}=(X_{0}\cap X_{1})^{ac}
in (ii) above holds, e.g. when
X_{0}\hookrightarrow X_{1}
.

Proof.

(i) Fix a parameter
\Phi
of the
K
-method. Observe that

(53)

K_{\Phi}(X_{0},X_{1})\hookrightarrow X_{0}+X_{1}\hookrightarrow(X_{0}+X_{1})^{ac}\hookrightarrow Y.

Therefore, since
Y
is sequentially complete, it suffices to verify that the condition (ii) of Theorem
3.11
is satisfied.
Let
(x_{n})
be a Cauchy sequence in
K_{\Phi}(X_{0},X_{1})
such that
x_{n}\to 0
in
Y
. Clearly, for each
n,m\in\mathbb{N}

and all
t>0
, we have

\big|K(t,x_{n};\vec{X})-K(t,x_{m};\vec{X})\big|\leq K(t,x_{n}-x_{m};\vec{X})\,.

Thus,
(K(\cdot,x_{n};\vec{X}))
is a Cauchy sequence in the Banach space
\Phi
. Therefore, there exists
f\in\Phi
such that

\|K(\cdot,x_{n};\vec{X})-f\|_{\Phi}\to 0\,.

Since
\Phi\hookrightarrow L^{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathrm{d}t)
, it follows from the Riesz theorem that there exists a subsequence
(x_{n_{k}})
of
(x_{n})
satisfying

K(t,x_{n_{k}};\vec{X})\to f(t)\quad\text{for a.e. $t>0$}\,.

By (
53
),
x_{n}\to 0
in
X_{0}+X_{1}
and, consequently, for every
t>0
,

K(t,x_{n};\vec{X})\to 0\quad\text{as $n\to\infty$}\,.

Thus,
f(t)=0
for a.e.
t>0
, that is,
x_{n}\to 0
in
K_{\Phi}(X_{0},X_{1})
as desired.

(ii) By Lemma
3.13
, for every
t>0
, we have

({X_{0}+tX_{1}})^{ac}=X_{0}^{ac}+tX_{1}^{ac}

with equal norms, where
X_{0}+tX_{1}
denotes
X_{0}+X_{1}
equipped with the norm
K(t,\cdot\,;\vec{X})
.
Hence, for any
x\in X_{0}+X_{1}
, we have

K(t,x;X_{0},X_{1})=\|x\|_{(X_{0}+tX_{1})^{ac}}=K(t,x;X_{0}^{ac},X_{1}^{ac}),\quad\,t>0\,.

In particular, this implies that for all
x\in K_{\Phi}(X_{0},X_{1})
, we have

\|x\|_{K_{\Phi}(X_{0},X_{1})}=\|x\|_{K_{\Phi}(X_{0}^{ac},X_{1}^{ac})}\,.

Since
K_{\Phi}(\vec{X})\hookrightarrow K_{\Phi}(X_{0}^{ac},X_{1}^{ac})
and
K_{\Phi}(\vec{X})
is a dense subset of its completion,
we obtain the desired claim, as well as the first part of the additional statement. For the second part, recall that the
regularity of
K_{\Phi}
implies that
X_{0}\cap X_{1}
and
X_{0}^{ac}\cap X_{1}^{ac}
are dense in
K_{\Phi}(\vec{X})
and

K_{\Phi}(X_{0}^{ac},X_{1}^{ac})
, respectively. Moreover, the assumption that
X_{0}^{ac}\cap X_{1}^{ac}=(X_{0}\cap X_{1})^{ac}

ensures that
X_{0}\cap X_{1}
is dense in
X_{0}^{ac}\cap X_{1}^{ac}
. Therefore,
X_{0}\cap X_{1}
is dense in

K_{\Phi}(X_{0}^{ac},X_{1}^{ac})
, as required. This completes the proof.
∎

We apply the result above to the context considered in this paper and formulate the
a priori
conditions in terms of the
injective operator
{\mathcal{A}}
on
{\mathcal{X}}
, in particular, which guarantee that the spaces
X=K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})
from the formulation of
Theorems
3.1
and
3.3
equipped with the homogeneous part of the corresponding interpolation norms admit completions in a given vector space.

In what follows if
{\mathcal{A}}
is an injective linear operator on a Banach space
{\mathcal{X}}
, then
\dot{D}_{\mathcal{A}}
denotes the domain
D({\mathcal{A}})
of
{\mathcal{A}}
equipped with the
norm
\|x\|_{\dot{D}_{\mathcal{A}}}:=\|{\mathcal{A}}x\|_{{\mathcal{X}}}
.
Recall that a linear operator
{\mathcal{A}}
on a topological vector space
Y
is called
closable in
Y
if admits a closed extension. One can show that it holds if and only if for every sequence
(x_{n})
in
D({\mathcal{A}})
such that
x_{n}\rightarrow 0
and
{\mathcal{A}}x_{n}\rightarrow y\in Y
one has
y=0
.

Proposition 3.15
.

Let
{\mathcal{X}}
be a Banach space and let
Y=(Y,\tau)
be a sequentially complete Hausdorff vector space. Let
{\mathcal{A}}
be an injective operator on
{\mathcal{X}}
. Then the following statements hold
:

(i)

If
{\mathcal{A}}
is closable in
{\mathcal{X}}
, then the pair
({\mathcal{X}},\dot{D}_{\mathcal{A}})
is a couple of normed spaces.

(ii)

If
{\mathcal{X}}\hookrightarrow Y
,
\dot{D}_{\mathcal{A}}\hookrightarrow Y
and
{\mathcal{A}}
is closable in
Y
, then
\dot{D}_{\mathcal{A}}
has a completion in
Y
.

(iii)

If

{\mathcal{X}}\hookrightarrow Y
,
\dot{D}_{\mathcal{A}}\hookrightarrow Y
,
{\mathcal{A}}
is closable in
Y
, and
{\mathcal{X}}+\dot{D}_{\mathcal{A}}
admits a completion in
Y
, then for any parameter
\Phi
of the
K
-method,
the normed space
K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},\dot{D}_{\mathcal{A}})
also admits a completion in
Y
.

Proof.

(i) It is easy to verify (cf.
[
12
, Proposition 2.1.7, p. 94]
for the case of Banach spaces) that if
X_{0}
and
X_{1}
are normed spaces linearly embedded into a vector space

\mathcal{V}
, and the following consistency condition holds: whenever a sequence
(x_{n})
in
X_{0}\cap X_{1}
converges to
y_{j}
in the normed space
X_{j}
for
j=0,1
,
we have
y_{0}=y_{1}
, then
(X_{0},X_{1})
forms a couple.
Since
{\mathcal{A}}
is a closable operator, it is clear that the normed spaces
\dot{{D}}_{\mathcal{A}}
and
{\mathcal{X}}
satisfy the consistency condition. Thus, it follows that
({\mathcal{X}},\dot{D}_{\mathcal{A}})

forms a couple of normed spaces.

For (ii), let
(x_{n})
be a Cauchy sequence in
\dot{D}_{\mathcal{A}}
such that
x_{n}\to 0
in
Y
. Since
({\mathcal{A}}x_{n})
is a Cauchy
sequence in
{\mathcal{X}}
, there exists
y\in{\mathcal{X}}
such that

\|{\mathcal{A}}x_{n}-y\|_{{\mathcal{X}}}\to 0\,.

Hence
{\mathcal{A}}x_{n}\to y
in
Y
by
{\mathcal{X}}\hookrightarrow Y
. Since
x_{n}\to 0
in
Y
and
{\mathcal{A}}
is closable in
Y
, it follows that
y=0
. Therefore, we have

\|x_{n}\|_{\dot{D}_{\mathcal{A}}}=\|{\mathcal{A}}x_{n}\|_{{\mathcal{X}}}\to 0\,.

Thus, the result follows from the Theorem
3.11
. Finally, the statement (iii) is a direct consequence of Proposition
3.14
. The proof is complete.
∎

We conclude with a result that demonstrates that under certain conditions, the normed space
{\mathcal{X}}+\dot{D}_{\mathcal{A}}
has a completion in a given space
Y
.

Proposition 3.16
.

Let
{\mathcal{X}}
be a Banach space,
(Y,\tau)
a sequentially complete Hausdorff vector space, and
{\mathcal{A}}
an injective operator on
{\mathcal{X}}
such that
{\mathcal{X}}\hookrightarrow Y

and
\dot{D}_{\mathcal{A}}\hookrightarrow Y
. Assume that
{\mathcal{A}}
is closable in
Y
and generates a bounded semigroup
{\mathcal{T}}
on
{\mathcal{X}}
such that for every sequence
(x_{n})\subset{\mathcal{X}}
such that
x_{n}\rightarrow 0
in
Y
,
{\mathcal{T}}(t)x_{n}\rightarrow 0
in
Y
for all
t\in(0,1]
.
Then, the space
{\mathcal{X}}+\dot{D}_{\mathcal{A}}
admits a completion in
Y
.
In particular, the conclusion of Proposition
\ref{Cauchy}\,(iii)
holds.

Proof.

Since
{\mathcal{X}}\hookrightarrow Y
and
{\mathcal{A}}
is closable in
Y
,
{\mathcal{A}}
is closable in
{\mathcal{X}}
. Therefore, it follows from Proposition
3.15
(i) that
({\mathcal{X}},\dot{D}_{\mathcal{A}})

forms a couple of normed spaces. This implies that
{\mathcal{X}}+\dot{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathcal{A}}
is a normed space, and we have

{\mathcal{X}}+\dot{D}_{\mathcal{A}}\hookrightarrow Y\,.

From Lemma
3.9
(ii), for every
x\in{\mathcal{X}}+\dot{D}_{\mathcal{A}}
, we have

(54)

\|x\|_{{\mathcal{X}}+\dot{D}_{\mathcal{A}}}=K(1,x;{\mathcal{X}},\dot{D}_{\mathcal{A}})\simeq\sup_{0<t\leq 1}\|{\mathcal{T}}(t)x-x\|_{{\mathcal{X}}}.

Let
(x_{n})
be a Cauchy sequence in
{\mathcal{X}}+\dot{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathcal{A}}
such that
x_{n}\to 0
in
Y
. From the above equivalence,
it follows that
({\mathcal{T}}(\cdot)x_{n}-x_{n})
is a Cauchy sequence in the Banach space
C_{b}((0,1];{\mathcal{X}})
. Thus, there exists
g\in C_{b}((0,1];{\mathcal{X}})
such that

(55)

\sup_{0<t\leq 1}\|{\mathcal{T}}(t)x_{n}-x_{n}-g(t)\|_{{\mathcal{X}}}\to 0\quad\,\text{as $n\to\infty$}\,.

Therefore, by our assumptions, for all
t\in(0,1]
we get that
{\mathcal{T}}(t)x_{n}-x_{n}-g(t)\rightarrow 0
and
{\mathcal{T}}(t)x_{n}\rightarrow 0
in
Y
.
Consequently,
g(t)=0
for every
t\in(0,1]
, and combining (
54
) with (
55
) yields
\|x_{n}\|_{{\mathcal{X}}+\dot{D}_{\mathcal{A}}}\rightarrow 0
.
Hence, by Theorem
3.11
, we get that
{\mathcal{X}}+\dot{D}_{\mathcal{A}}
admits a completion in
Y
, which finishes the proof.
∎

Note that by combining Propositions
3.16
and
3.15
, and Corollary
3.14
, a conclusion similar to that of Lemma
3.9
(iii) holds,
provided
Y
,
{\mathcal{X}}
,
{\mathcal{A}}
, and
{\mathcal{T}}
satisfy the assumptions of Proposition
3.16
. More precisely, for any
\Phi
as in (iii) of Lemma
3.9
, the
Cauchy completion
K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},\dot{D}_{\mathcal{A}})^{ac}
of
K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},\dot{D}_{\mathcal{A}})
coincides with
K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},(\dot{D}_{\mathcal{A}})^{ac})\subset Y
.

We conclude with two observations regarding Theorems
3.1
and
3.3
. From the perspective of interpolation theory, Theorems

3.1
,
3.3
, and
2.28
can be considered as an extension of the Levy-type result describing those parameters
\Phi
of the
K
-method
for which the corresponding interpolation spaces
K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{Y}})
contain an isomorphic copy of
\ell_{1}
or
c_{0}
; see e.g.
[
12
, Theorems 4.6.25 and 4.6.28]
.

In general, one cannot expect that the conclusions of Theorem
3.1
hold for other parameters
\Phi
of the
K
-method. In fact, for some parameters
\Phi
(e.g.
those corresponding to the real interpolation method, i.e.
\Phi=L^{q}(\mathbb{R}_{+},t^{\mu}\mathrm{d}t)
with
\mu\in(-1,q-1)
and
q\in(1,\infty)
), the
U\!M\!D
property of the
underlying space
{\mathcal{X}}
is inherited by
K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},D_{{\mathcal{A}}})=({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})_{1-\frac{\mu+1}{q},q}
; see Rubio de Francia
[
49
]
.

4.
The interpolation approach

In this section we provide a useful interpolation framework for the study of the maximal regularity estimates in other interpolation spaces than those considered
in Theorems
3.1
and
3.3
.

Let
(X_{0},X_{1})
be a Banach couple and let
A_{j}
be a closed linear operator on
X_{j}
(
j=0,1
). Let
D_{A_{j}}
for
j=0,1
denote the domain
D(A_{j})
of
A_{j}

equipped with the graph norm.
Note that
(D_{A_{0}},D_{A_{1}})
forms a Banach couple.
Suppose
A_{0}x=A_{1}x
for all
x\in D(A_{0})\cap D(A_{1})
. We define

(56)

Ax:=A_{0}x_{0}+A_{1}x_{1}

for all
x=x_{0}+x_{1}
with
x_{j}\in D(A_{j})
for
j=0,1
. It is easy to see that
A
is the unique linear mapping with domain

D(A)=D(A_{0})+D(A_{1})\subset X_{0}+X_{1}
such that
A|_{D(A_{j})}=A_{j}
for
j=0,1
. Given an interpolation functor
F
, let
A_{F}
denote the part of
A

in the corresponding interpolation space
F(X_{0},X_{1})
. In the first result we identify the domain of the part
A_{F}
of
A
in
F(X_{0},X_{1})
for an arbitrary
interpolation functor
F
.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
(X_{0},X_{1})
be a Banach couple, and let
A_{j}
be a closed operator on
X_{j}
for
j=0,1
. Assume that
A_{0}x=A_{1}x

for all
x\in D(A_{0})\cap D(A_{1})
, and suppose there exists
\lambda\in\rho(A_{0})\cap\rho(A_{1})
such that
(\lambda-A_{0})^{-1}
and

(\lambda-A_{1})^{-1}
coincide on
X_{0}\cap X_{1}
. Then,
\lambda\in\rho(A)
and for any interpolation functor
{F}
the part
A_{{F}}
of
A
in
{F}(X_{0},X_{1})

is a closed operator and

{F}(D_{A_{0}},D_{A_{1}})=D_{A_{{F}}}

up to equivalence of norms.

Proof.

Since
(\lambda-A_{0})^{-1}
and
(\lambda-A_{1})^{-1}
agree on
X_{0}\cap X_{1}
, the map
S
given by

Sx:=(\lambda-A_{0})^{-1}x_{0}+(\lambda-A_{1})^{-1}x_{1}

for all
x=x_{0}+x_{1}
with
x_{0}\in X_{0}
and
x_{1}\in X_{1}
is a (well-defined) linear operator on
X_{0}+X_{1}
.
Clearly,
S|_{X_{j}}=(\lambda-A_{j})^{-1}
for
j=0,1
and
S(X_{0}+X_{1})=D(A_{0})+D(A_{1})
.
In particular,
S
is an admissible with respect to the couples
(X_{0},X_{1})
and
(D_{A_{0}},D_{A_{1}})
. In particular, it gives that
S
is bounded on
X_{0}+X_{1}
and its restriction
S_{|}
to
F(X_{0},X_{1})
is a bounded operator from
F(X_{0},X_{1})
to
F(D_{A_{0}},D_{A_{1}})
. We show that
S_{|}
is invertible. Indeed, since
(\lambda-A_{0})x=(\lambda-A_{1})x
for all
x\in D(A_{0})\cap D(A_{1})
,
S
is injective. It is readily seen that
S^{-1}=\lambda-A
, where
A
is given by (
56
) above. It shows that
\lambda\in\rho(A)
and

S^{-1}|_{D(A_{j})}=\lambda-A_{j},\quad\,j=0,1\,.

Therefore, the operator
S^{-1}
is admissible with respect to the couples
(D_{A_{0}},D_{A_{1}})
and
(X_{0},X_{1})
.
Further, for a given
y\in F(D_{A_{0}},D_{A_{1}})\subset D(A_{0})+D(A_{1})
, there exists
x\in X_{0}+X_{1}
such that
Sx=y
. By the interpolation property of
F
, we conclude that
x=S^{-1}y\in F(X_{0},X_{1})
. It yields the desired claim on
S_{|}
.
To show that
A_{F}
is a closed operator on
F(X_{0},X_{1})
let
(x_{n})
be a sequence in
D(A_{F})

with
x_{n}\to x
in
F(X_{0},X_{1})
and
A_{F}x_{n}=Ax_{n}\to y
in
F(X_{0},X_{1})
. Clearly, for each
n\geq 1
, we have

\lambda(\lambda-A)^{-1}x_{n}-(\lambda-A)^{-1}Ax_{n}=x_{n}\,.

Since
S_{|}=(\lambda-A)_{|}^{-1}\colon F(X_{0},X_{1})\to F(D_{A_{0}},D_{A_{1}})
is continuous, it follows that

\lambda(\lambda-A)^{-1}x-(\lambda-A)^{-1}y=x\,.

Hence,
x\in D(A)\cap F(X_{0},X_{1})
and
(\lambda-A)^{-1}(\lambda x-y)=x
yields
\lambda x-y=(\lambda-A)x
, so
Ax=y
as required. Further, combining
D_{A_{j}}\hookrightarrow X_{j}
(
j=0,1
) with the interpolation property of
F
we get

F(D_{A_{0}},D_{A_{1}})\hookrightarrow F(X_{0},X_{1})\,.

Note that
D({\mathcal{A}}_{F})=S(F(X_{0},X_{1}))=F(D_{A_{0}},D_{A_{1}})
, and for all
x\in F(D_{A_{0}},D_{A_{1}})
, one has

\gamma\|x\|_{F(D_{A_{0}},D_{A_{1}})}\leq\|x-Ax\|_{F(X_{0},X_{1})}\leq\|x\|_{F(X_{0},X_{1})}+\|Ax\|_{F(X_{0},X_{1})}\,,

where
\gamma=1/\|S_{|}\,\|_{{\mathcal{L}}(F(X_{0},X_{1}),F(D_{A_{0}},D_{A_{1}}))}
.
Combining this with the above inclusion, we conclude that

F(D_{A_{0}},D_{A_{1}})=D_{A_{F}}

up to equivalence of norms, and this completes the proof.
∎

Theorem 4.2
.

Let
(X_{0},X_{1})
be a Banach couple.
Let
-A_{0}
and
-A_{1}
be the generators of the bounded holomorphic semigroups
T_{0}
and
T_{1}
on
X_{0}
and
X_{1}
, respectively, which are consistent in the sense that

T_{0}(t)x=T_{1}(t)x
for all
x\in X_{0}\cap X_{1}
and
t>0
. Suppose that
A_{0}
and
A_{1}
are consistent on
D(A_{0})\cap D(A_{1})
and have
L^{p}
-m.r. on
(0,\tau)
for some
\tau\in(0,\infty]
and
p\in[1,\infty)
.

Then, for any interpolation functor
F
such that, up to the equivalence of the norms,

(57)

F(\ell^{p}(X_{0}),\ell^{p}(X_{1}))=\ell^{p}(F(X_{0},X_{1})),

the part of the operator
A_{0}+A_{1}
in
F(X_{0},X_{1})
has
L^{p}
-m.r. on
(0,\tau)
.

Below, for any bounded linear operator
S
on a Banach space
X
and any sequence
\mathbf{x}=(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in
X
we set

(58)

\mathbf{K}_{S}\mathbf{x}:=(y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}},\qquad y_{n}:=\sum_{k=1}^{n}S^{n-k}(I-S)x_{k},\qquad n\in\mathbb{N}.

Proof.

Let
X_{0}+X_{1}
be equipped with the standard sum norm
\|\cdot\|_{X_{0}+X_{1}}
.
On
X_{0}+X_{1}
we define the family of operators
(T(t))_{t>0}
as follows: for
x=x_{0}+x_{1}
with
x_{0}\in X_{0}
and
x_{1}\in X_{1}
set

T(t)x:=T_{0}(t)x_{0}+T_{1}(t)x_{1}.

By the consistency of semigroups
T_{0}
and
T_{1}
, one can check that
T
is a well-defined family of bounded operators on
X_{0}+X_{1}
, satisfying the semigroup property and having a bounded analytic extension to some sector around positive real axis (note that
T
is a
C_{0}
-semigroup if
T_{0}
and
T_{1}
are). For
T
to be a semigroup (according to the definition in
[
4
, p. 128]
; see Remark
2.1
), we need to check that for
x\in X
if
T(t)x=0
for all
t>0
then
x=0
. Recall that the resolvent of
-A_{i}
is represented by the Laplace transform of
T_{i}
, that is, for all
\lambda
large enough, say
\lambda>\omega>0
, we have

(59)

(\lambda+A_{i})^{-1}x=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}T_{i}(t)x\mathrm{d}t,\qquad x\in X_{i}\,,

with convergence in the norm of
X_{i}
(
i=0,1
).
Clearly, for all
\lambda>\omega
the operators
(\lambda+A_{0})^{-1}
and
(\lambda+A_{1})^{-1}
coincide on
X_{0}\cap X_{1}
. Since
A_{0}
and
A_{1}
are consistent on
D(A_{0})\cap D(A_{1})
, Proposition
4.1
yields that
(\omega,\infty)\subset\rho(A)
, where
A
is given by (
56
).

Since
X_{0}
and
X_{1}
are continuously embedded in
X_{0}+X_{1}
, by (
59
), for all
\lambda>\omega
and for all
x=x_{0}+x_{1}\in X_{0}+X_{1}
, we get

(\lambda+A)^{-1}x=(\lambda+A_{0})^{-1}x_{0}+(\lambda+A_{1})^{-1}x_{1}=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}T(t)x\mathrm{d}t,

where the integral is convergent in the norm of
X_{0}+X_{1}
. According to
[
4
, Definition 3.2.5]
,
T
is a semigroup on
X_{0}+X_{1}
with the generator
-A
.
Let for some
p\in[1,\infty)
,
A_{i}
has
L^{p}
-m.r. on
(0,\tau)
. By the rescaling argument (see the proof of Lemma
2.4
(ii)), we can consider only the case
\tau=\infty
.
By the Kalton-Portal result
[
37
, Proposition 2.2]
,
the operators
\mathbf{K}_{T_{i}(t)}
,
t\in(0,1)
, are uniformly bounded on
l^{p}(X_{i})

(i=0,1)
.

Let
F
be an interpolation functor. Set
X_{F}:=F(X_{0},X_{1})
and let
T_{F}(t):=T(t)_{|X_{F}}
,
t\geq 0
. Of course,
T_{F}
is a semigroup on
X_{F}
. Denote its generator by
-B
.
We show that
B
is equal to the part
A_{F}
of
A
in
X_{F}
. Indeed, since

X_{F}
is continuously embedded in
X_{0}+X_{1}
, by the same argument as above,

(\lambda+B)^{-1}y=\int^{\infty}_{0}e^{-\lambda t}T_{F}(t)y\mathrm{d}t=\int^{\infty}_{0}e^{-\lambda t}T(t)y\mathrm{d}t=(\lambda+A)^{-1}y=x.

for each
x\in X_{F}
and all
\lambda
large enough. In particular, it gives that for
x\in D(B)\subset X_{F}
and
y\in X_{F}
such that
x=(\lambda+B)^{-1}y
, we have
x\in D(A)
and
Ax=y-\lambda x\in X_{F}
and
Bx=Ax
. Hence,
D(B)\subset D(A_{F})
and
B\subset A_{F}
.
Conversely, if
x\in D(A_{F})
, i.e.
x\in X_{F}\cap D(A)
with
Ax\in X_{F}
, then
y:=\lambda x+Ax\in X_{F}
. Therefore,
(\lambda+B)^{-1}y=(\lambda+A)^{-1}y=x
.
Consequently,
x\in D(B)
and
Bx=A_{F}x
. It yields that
B=A_{F}
.

Since for each
t>0
the operators
\mathbf{K}_{T_{0}(t)}
and
\mathbf{K}_{T_{1}(t)}
are consistent on
\ell^{p}(X_{0})\cap\ell^{p}(X_{1})
,
\mathbf{K}_{T(t)}
is an admissible operator on a Banach couple
(\ell^{p}(X_{0}),\ell^{p}(X_{1}))
. Consequently,
its restriction to
F(\ell^{p}(X_{0}),\ell^{p}(X_{1}))
, which, of course, coincides with the operator
\mathbf{K}_{T_{F}(t)}
, is a bounded operator on
F(\ell^{p}(X_{0}),\ell^{p}(X_{1}))
.
Since we suppose that
F

splits

\ell^{p}
, i.e.
F(\ell^{p}(X_{0}),\ell^{p}(X_{1}))=\ell^{p}(X_{F})
(equivalent norms), again by
[
37
, Proposition 2.2]
,
A_{F}
has
L^{p}
-m.r. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
in
X_{F}
.
∎

We conclude with a simple application of Proposition
4.2
. Let
[\,\cdot\,]_{\theta}
,
\theta\in(0,1)
, stands for the functor of the complex method; see, e.g.
[
9
, Chapter 4]
. Recall that those functors split
\ell^{p}
spaces, that is, (
57
) holds; see
[
9
, Theorem 5.1.2, p. 107]
.

Corollary 4.3
.

Let
{\mathcal{A}}
be a linear operator on a Banach space
{\mathcal{X}}
with the
L^{p}
-m.r. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
for some
p\in[1,\infty)
.

Then, for all interpolation functors
F
, which splits
\ell^{p}
, the part of
{\mathcal{A}}
in
F({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})
has
L^{p}
-m.r. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.
In particular, In particular, this is the case when

F\in\bigl\{[\,\cdot\,]_{\theta},\,(\,\cdot\,)_{\theta,q}\,:\theta\in(0,1),q\in(1,\infty)\bigr\}.

Proof.

Recall that for any
\lambda\in\rho({\mathcal{A}})
, the resolvent operator
R(\lambda,A)
is an isomorphism between
({\mathcal{X}},\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{X}})
and
(D_{\mathcal{A}},\|\cdot\|_{D_{\mathcal{A}}})
. It is straightforward to see, that the
L^{p}
-m.r. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
of the part of
{\mathcal{A}}
in
D_{\mathcal{A}}
follows from that one of
{\mathcal{A}}
in
{\mathcal{X}}
. Therefore, taking
A_{0}:={\mathcal{A}}
on
X_{0}:={\mathcal{X}}
and
A_{1}
as the part of
{\mathcal{A}}
on
X_{1}:=D_{\mathcal{A}}
, the proof follows from Theorem
4.2
.
∎

Note that a similar consequence of Theorem
4.2
, analogous to that of Corollary
4.3
, can be formulated in the context considered
in Proposition
3.5
, namely by taking
X_{0}
as
(X,\|\cdot\|_{X})
,
X_{1}
as the completion
\widetilde{X}
of
(X,\|\cdot\|_{*})
, and
(A_{0},A_{1}):=(A,\widetilde{A})
,
provided that
(X,\widetilde{X})
forms a Banach couple. We do not elaborate further on this topic here.

We illustrate Corollary
4.3
by applying it to the Stokes systems with Dirichlet boundary conditions.
There is an extensive literature on the maximal regularity of the Stokes operator on various domains
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
under several boundary conditions. See the expository article by Hieber and Saal
[
31
]
(which also includes results by Grubb and Solonnikov
[
28
]
).

For Dirichlet boundary conditions, the most general result, to our knowledge, is provided in
[
24
, Corollary 2.2]
, which states that if the domain
\Omega
has a
uniform

C^{3}
-boundary and the Helmholtz decomposition exists for
L^{q}(\Omega)^{n}
, then the corresponding Stokes operator has
L^{p}
-m.r. on finite intervals for any
1<p<\infty
(see also
[
25
]
).
It appears that this result does not determine whether, for such general domains
\Omega
, the corresponding Stokes operator has
L^{p}
-m.r. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
. However, this is the case for a large class of domains
\Omega
. For instance, it was proven in
[
20
, Theorem 7.6]
, that for
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+},\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the corresponding Stokes operator admits a bounded
H^{\infty}
calculus. Similarly, for bounded domains, exterior domains, or bent half-spaces (whose boundary is of class
C^{3}
), analogous results were proven in
[
43
]
. Consequently, by the Dore-Venni theorem (see a variant in
[
48
, Theorem 4]
), the corresponding Stokes operator has
L^{p}
-m.r. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
for any
1<p<\infty
.

The description of the corresponding interpolation spaces between the solenoidal part
{\mathcal{X}}:=L^{q}_{\sigma}(\Omega)
of
L^{q}(\Omega)^{n}
and the domain
D_{\mathcal{A}}
of the corresponding Stokes operator
{\mathcal{A}}
was given by Amann in
[
3
]
(for so-called
standard
domains). Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
denote any of the following domains:
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
, exterior domain, bent half-space or bounded domain whose boundary is of class
C^{3}
.

Recall that by
[
3
, Lemma 3.2 and Theorem 3.4]
for every
\theta\in(0,1)
and
q\in(1,\infty)

[{\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}}]_{\theta}=[L^{q}(\Omega)^{n},W^{2,q}_{0}(\Omega)^{n}]_{\theta}\cap L^{q}_{\sigma}(\Omega)=:H^{2\theta,q}_{0,\sigma}(\Omega)

({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})_{\theta,r}=(L^{q}(\Omega)^{n},W^{2,q}_{0}(\Omega)^{n})_{\theta,r}\cap L^{q}_{\sigma}(\Omega)=:B^{2\theta}_{q,r,0,\sigma}(\Omega)

For the exact description of those spaces (how it depends on the relation between parameters
p
and
\theta
) we refer the reader to
[
3
, (2.19), p. 40]

By means of Corollary
4.3
, for any
p,q,r\in(1,\infty)
and
s\in(0,2)
, the Stokes operator on the resulting interpolation spaces
X:=H^{s,q}_{0,\sigma}(\Omega)
and
X=B^{s}_{q,r,0,\sigma}(\Omega)
has
L^{p}
-m.r. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

5.
Illustrative examples

In this section, we illustrate selected results from previous sections by considering the negative Laplacian
{\mathcal{A}}:=-\Delta
on
{\mathcal{X}}:=L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with
p\in[1,\infty)
and
{\mathcal{X}}=C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Our main aim is to provide an intrinsic description of the homogeneous part
[\cdot]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}
of the norm of the real interpolation spaces
X=({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})_{\theta,q}
with
\theta\in(0,1)
and
q\in[1,\infty]
.
In particular, it allows to identify the completion
\widetilde{X}
of
X
with respect to
[\cdot]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}
and
show that Theorems
3.1
,
3.3
, Propositions
3.5
,
3.6
and Corollary
3.8
, extend and unify relevant results from the literature; see Corollary
5.5
.

For
p\in[1,\infty]
, let
{\mathcal{A}}={\mathcal{A}}_{p}
denote the realization of the negative Laplacian
-\Delta
on
{\mathcal{X}}={\mathcal{X}}_{p}
, where
{\mathcal{X}}_{p}:=L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for
p\in[1,\infty)
and
{\mathcal{X}}_{\infty}:=C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Recall that
D_{{\mathcal{A}}}=\{f\in{\mathcal{X}}_{p};\Delta f\in{\mathcal{X}}_{p}\}
. Moreover, for each
\theta\in(0,1)
, if
\Phi:=L^{q}(\mathbb{R}_{+};t^{q(1-\theta)-1}\mathrm{d}t)
for
q\in[1,\infty)
, or
\Phi:=L^{\infty}(t^{1-\theta})
, then

(60)

X:=K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})=({\mathcal{X}},D_{\mathcal{A}})_{\theta,q}=B^{2\theta}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),

see, e.g.
[
9
, Theorem 6.7.4, p.160]
.
The representation of the (inhomogeneous) Besov spaces
X=B^{2\theta}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
stated in Proposition
2.23
is a description via
thermic extension
; see e.g.
[
54
, Section 2.6.4]
. Since
{\mathcal{A}}
is injective, the corresponding homogeneous part

[\phi]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}}:=\bigl\|\Delta e^{\cdot\Delta}\phi\bigr\|_{\Phi({\mathcal{X}})},\qquad\phi\in B^{2\theta}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),

of the interpolation norm
\|\cdot\|_{X}
is, in fact, the norm on
X
.
We expect that the completion
\widetilde{X}
of
(X,[\cdot]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}})
, which we discuss in Proposition
3.5
, can be identified with the
homogeneous
Besov space
\dot{B}^{2\theta}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. We do not know any references for it. Since it is crucial below, we provide a rigorous argument for it.

There are several definitions of homogeneous Besov spaces available in the literature and each of them offers advantages in specific situations. For the classical definition, we refer the reader to
[
52
]
(see also
[
9
]
,
[
27
]
,
[
46
]
), while a more recent framework is introduced in
[
6
]
. In the classical approach, we deal with equivalence classes of distributions modulo polynomials, which presents some difficulties when it comes to solving nonlinear PDEs (for example, the lack of product rules). However, this approach guarantees the completeness of these spaces. In contrast, for the second definition, we only have completeness for a range of parameters, but the spaces are more suitable for applications. Let us state these definitions, along with a few properties and the relationships between them.

Let
(\phi_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
be the Littlewood-Paley resolution of the identity on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}
, that is,
\phi
is a smooth radial function such that
\phi\geq 0
,
\operatorname{supp}\phi\subset\{\xi:2^{-1}\leq|\xi|\leq 2\}
,
\phi_{k}:=\phi(2^{-k}\cdot)
(
k\in\mathbb{Z}
) and
\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\phi_{k}(\xi)=1
for
\xi\neq 0
. Let
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
p,q\in[1,\infty]
. The classical homogeneous Besov space
\dot{B}^{s}_{p,q,1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is defined as those tempered distributions
f\in{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
modulo polynomials
{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that

\|f\|_{\dot{B}^{s}_{p,q}}:=\bigg(\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}2^{sjk}\|{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}(\phi_{k}\cdot{\mathcal{F}}f)\|^{q}_{p}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{q}}<\infty

(with modification for
q=\infty
).
The more recent approach is presented in
[
6
]
. Let
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}_{h}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
denote the space of tempered distributions
f
such that for any
\Theta\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}\|{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}[\Theta(\lambda\cdot){\mathcal{F}}f]\|_{\infty}=0.

The homogeneous Besov space
\dot{B}^{s}_{p,q,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
consists of those distributions
f
in
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}_{h}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that
\|f\|_{\dot{B}^{s}_{p,q}}<\infty
.
Let
{\mathcal{S}}_{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
denote the space of all Schwartz functions which annihilate polynomials
{\mathcal{P}}
. For
q=\infty
we set:

\dot{b}^{s}_{p,1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}):=\overline{{\mathcal{S}}_{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{\dot{B}^{s}_{p,\infty,1}}

and

\dot{b}^{s}_{p,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}):=\overline{{\mathcal{S}}_{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{\dot{B}^{s}_{p,\infty,2}}.

We also refer the reader, for instance, to Bourdaud
[
10
]
and Moussai
[
41
]
for a discussion on further
realizations
of homogeneous Besov spaces.

Below, we omit ’
(\mathbb{R}^{n})
’ in the symbols of the Besov spaces and other spaces over
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. This should not result in any contradictions.
Recall that, the spaces
\dot{B}^{s}_{p,q,1}
and
\dot{B}^{s}_{p,q,2}
are well-defined (they do not depend on the choice of the sequence
(\phi_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
) and normed. The space
\dot{B}^{s}_{p,q,1}
is complete for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
(see
[
52
, Theorem 5.1.5, p. 240]
), and the space
\dot{B}^{s}_{p,q,2}
is complete, when
s<\frac{n}{p}
, or
s=\frac{n}{p}
and
q=1
(see
[
6
, Theorem 2.25, p.67]
).

To demonstrate that both classes, up to completion and isomorphism, contain the same objects, we employ the following standard set-theoretical argument. We skip the straightforward proof of this.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
(B,\|\cdot\|_{B})
and
(\dot{B},\|\cdot\|_{\dot{B}})
be normed spaces. Suppose that there exists a linear injection
j:B\rightarrow\dot{B}
such that for some
c>0
for all
b\in B

(61)

c^{-1}\|b\|_{B}\leq\|jb\|_{\dot{B}}\leq c\|b\|_{B}

and
jB
is a dense subset of
\dot{B}
. Then the completions of
B
and
\dot{B}
are topologically isomorphic.

Let
J:{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}\to{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}/{\mathcal{P}}
be the quotient map, that is, given by

(62)

Jf:=f+{\mathcal{P}},\qquad f\in{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime},

where
{\mathcal{P}}
is the collection of all polynomials on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Note that
J
is an isometry from
\dot{B}^{s}_{p,q,2}
into
\dot{B}^{s}_{p,q,1}
.
For
q\neq\infty
, since
J{\mathcal{S}}_{\infty}
is dense in
\dot{B}^{s}_{p,q,1}
and
\dot{B}^{s}_{p,q,1}
is complete (
[
52
, Theorem 5.1.5, p.240]
), applying Lemma
5.1
we see that the completion of
\dot{B}^{s}_{p,q,2}
is isometrically isomorphic to
\dot{B}^{s}_{p,q,1}
. Therefore, the spaces
\dot{B}^{s}_{p,q,1}
and
\dot{B}^{s}_{p,q,2}
are isomorphic whenever the former one is complete. Analogous statements hold for
\dot{b}^{s}_{p,2}
and
\dot{b}^{s}_{p,1}
.
From now on let

\dot{B}^{s}_{p,q}:=\dot{B}^{s}_{p,q,1}\simeq\widetilde{\dot{B}^{s}_{p,q,2}},\qquad q\neq\infty,

and

\dot{b}^{s}_{p}:=\dot{b}^{s}_{p,1}\simeq\widetilde{\dot{b}^{s}_{p,2}}.

We apply the same argument to show that
\widetilde{X}
and
\dot{B}^{2\theta}_{p,q}
for
q\neq\infty
are isomorphic, where
X
is given by (
60
). Analogously,
\widetilde{X}
and
\dot{b}^{2\theta}_{p}
for
q=\infty
are isomorphic.
Since

{\mathcal{S}}_{\infty}\subset{\mathcal{S}}\subset X
, by Lemma
5.1
above, it is sufficient to show that for all
f\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\infty}

(63)

[f]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}_{p}}:=\bigl\|\,\|\Delta e^{(\cdot)\Delta}f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}_{p}}\bigr\|_{\Phi}\approx\|Jf\|_{\dot{B}^{s}_{p,q}}=\bigg(\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}2^{sjk}\|{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}(\hat{\phi}_{k}\cdot{\mathcal{F}}f)\|^{q}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{p}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{q}},

where
\Phi
,
{\mathcal{A}}_{p}
,
{\mathcal{X}}_{p}
have the meaning specified above.
This is addressed in the subsequent result, Proposition
5.2
.

We are now in a position to obtain (
63
). The statement could be proved by combining the steps of the proof of
[
9
, Theorem 6.7.4]
and
[
13
, Theorem 3.5.3]
. The proof presented here is more direct.

Proposition 5.2
.

Let
{\mathcal{A}}_{p}
be the realization of the negative Laplacian in
{\mathcal{X}}_{p}
, where
p\in[1,\infty]
. Let
\dot{D}_{{\mathcal{A}}_{p}}
be the domain of
{\mathcal{A}}_{p}
equipped with the norm
\|{\mathcal{A}}_{p}\cdot\|_{{\mathcal{X}}}
. Let
J
be as in (
62
).
Then for every
q\in[1,\infty]
and
\theta\in(0,1)
there exists a constant
c>0
, such that for any
f\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
we have

(64)

c^{-1}[f]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}_{p}}\leq\|Jf\|_{\dot{B}^{2\theta}_{p,q}}\leq c[f]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}_{p}},

where
\Phi=L^{q}(t^{q(1-\theta)-1})
for
q\neq\infty
and
\Phi=L^{\infty}(t^{1-\theta})
for
q=\infty
.
More precisely,

c^{-1}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}2^{2qk\theta}\|\check{\phi}_{k}\ast f\|^{q}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{p}}\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{q(1-\theta)-1}\|\Delta e^{-t\Delta}f\|^{q}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{p}}dt\leq c\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}2^{2qk\theta}\|\check{\phi}_{k}\ast f\|^{q}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{p}}

in the case
q\neq\infty
and

c^{-1}\sup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}2^{2k\theta}\|\check{\phi}_{k}\ast f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}_{p}}\leq\sup_{t>0}t^{1-\theta}\|\Delta e^{-t\Delta}f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}_{p}}\leq c\sup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}2^{2k\theta}\|\check{\phi}_{k}\ast f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}_{p}}

when
q=\infty
.

Moreover, the completion of
K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},\dot{D}_{{\mathcal{A}}_{p}})
is isomorphic to
\dot{B}^{2\theta}_{p,q}

(
resp.
\dot{b}^{2\theta}_{p})
, when
q\neq\infty

(
resp.
q=\infty)
.

Proof.

First, we consider the case
q\neq\infty
.
For the left inequality in (
64
), note that for all
f\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\infty}
:

{\mathcal{F}}f(\xi)=(e^{t|\xi|^{2}}-1)^{-1}{\mathcal{F}}(e^{-t\Delta}-I)f(\xi),\qquad\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\,t>0.

Taking
t=2^{-2k},k\in\mathbb{Z}
, and using the fact that for any
p\in[1,\infty]
, functions

m_{k}(\xi):=\phi_{k}(\xi)(e^{|2^{-k}\xi|^{2}}-1)^{-1},\qquad\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n},

are symbols of
L^{p}
-Fourier multiplier operators, whose norms might be bounded by a constant independent of
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, we get

\|\check{\phi}_{k}\ast f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}_{p}}\lesssim\|e^{-2^{-2k}\Delta}f-f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}_{p}}\leq\omega_{{\mathcal{A}}_{p}}(2^{-2k},f)\quad(k\in\mathbb{Z}).

By Lemma
3.9
,
\bigl\|\,(\cdot)^{-1}\omega_{{\mathcal{A}}_{p}}(\cdot,f)\bigr\|_{\Phi}\simeq\bigl\|\,\|{\mathcal{A}}_{p}e^{(\cdot){\mathcal{A}}_{p}}f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}_{p}}\bigr\|_{\Phi}
, hence we obtain:

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}2^{2qk\theta}\|\check{\phi}_{k}\ast f\|^{q}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{p}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}2^{2qk\theta}\|e^{-2^{-2k}\Delta}f-f\|^{q}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{p}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{-q\theta-1}\omega_{{\mathcal{A}}_{p}}(t,f)^{q}dt

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{q(1-\theta)-1}\|\Delta e^{-t\Delta}f\|^{q}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{p}}dt.

For the second inequality, note that

\|\Delta e^{-t\Delta}f\|^{q}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{p}}\lesssim\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}2^{2kq}\|\check{\phi}_{k}\ast f\|^{q}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{p}},

as in
[
9
, Theorem 6.7.4]
, and, by the sectoriality of
{\mathcal{A}}_{p}
,

\|\Delta e^{-t\Delta}f\|^{q}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{p}}\lesssim\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{t^{q}}\|\check{\phi}_{k}\ast f\|^{q}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{p}}.

Both inequalities are justified, since
\lim_{j\to\infty}\sum_{|k|<j}\check{\phi}_{k}\ast f=f
in the
{\mathcal{S}}
topology (see
[
50
, Proposition 2.11, p.270]
) and therefore also in the norm of
{\mathcal{X}}_{p}
.
This yields

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{q(1-\theta)-1}\|\Delta e^{-t\Delta}f\|^{q}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{p}}dt

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\Big(\int_{0}^{2^{-2k}}t^{q(1-\theta)-1}2^{2kq}\|\check{\phi}_{k}\ast f\|^{q}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{p}}

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{2^{-2k}}^{\infty}t^{-q\theta-1}\|\check{\phi}_{k}\ast f\|^{q}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{p}}\Big)

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}2^{2kq\theta}\|\check{\phi}_{k}\ast f\|^{q}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{p}}.

For the case
q=\infty
the proof might be performed analogously, but the left inequality in (
64
) can be proved in a slightly easier manner by a suitable choice of a Fourier multiplier. Indeed, note that

{\mathcal{F}}f(\xi)=-t\frac{1}{t|\xi|^{2}}e^{-t|\xi|^{2}}{\mathcal{F}}\Delta e^{-t\Delta}f(\xi),\qquad\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\,f\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\infty},\,t>0.

Taking
t=2^{-2k}
and using the fact that for any
p\in[1,\infty]
functions

m_{k}(\xi):=\phi_{k}(\xi)\frac{1}{|2^{-k}\xi|^{2}}e^{|2^{-k}\xi|^{2}},\qquad\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n},

are symbols of
L^{p}
-Fourier multiplier operators, whose norm might be bounded by a constant independent of
k
, we get

\|\check{\phi}_{k}\ast f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}_{p}}\lesssim 2^{-2k}\|\Delta e^{-2^{-2k}\Delta}f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}_{p}},\qquad k\in\mathbb{Z}.

Hence,

\sup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}2^{2k\theta}\|\check{\phi}_{k}\ast f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}_{p}}\lesssim\sup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}2^{-2k(1-\theta)}\|\Delta e^{-2^{-2k}\Delta}f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}_{p}}\leq\sup_{t>0}t^{1-\theta}\|\Delta e^{-t\Delta}f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}_{p}}.

The completion of
K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},\dot{D}_{{\mathcal{A}}})
is the same as the completion of
{\mathcal{S}}_{\infty}
, so the last statement follows from Lemma
5.1
.
∎

For
1<p<\infty
we can express the homogeneous part
[\cdot]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}_{p}}
of the norm of
K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},D_{{\mathcal{A}}_{p}})
in terms of the modulus of continuity. By
\dot{H}^{2}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

we denote the homogeneous Sobolev space; see
[
9
, p.147]
.

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
1<p<\infty
and let
{\mathcal{A}}_{p}
,
{\mathcal{X}}_{p}
be as above. Then, the following statements hold:

(i)

The norms
\|\cdot\|_{\dot{H}^{2}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}
and
\|\cdot\|_{\dot{D}_{{\mathcal{A}}_{p}}}
are equivalent on
\dot{D}_{{\mathcal{A}}_{p}}
.

(ii)

For any
\phi\in K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},\dot{D}_{{\mathcal{A}}_{p}})
we have

K(t,\phi,{\mathcal{X}},\dot{D}_{{\mathcal{A}}_{p}})\simeq K(t,\phi,{\mathcal{X}},\dot{H}^{2}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))\simeq\omega^{2}_{p}(\sqrt{t},\phi):=\sup_{|h|\leq t}\|\delta_{h}^{2}\phi\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})},

where
\delta^{2}\phi:=\phi-2\phi(\cdot-h)+\phi(\cdot-2h)

and the equivalence constants are independent on
\phi
.

Proof.

(i) The inequality
\|\phi\|_{\dot{D}_{{\mathcal{A}}_{p}}}\leq\|\phi\|_{\dot{H}^{2}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}
follows readily from the triangle inequality. The reverse inequality follows from the well-known fact that for any
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
and
p\in(1,\infty)
the functions
m(\xi)=\frac{\xi_{i}\xi_{j}}{|\xi|^{2}}
,
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, are
L^{p}
-Fourier multiplier symbols.

(ii) The equivalence
K(t,\cdot,{\mathcal{X}},\dot{D}_{{\mathcal{A}}_{p}})\simeq K(t,\cdot,{\mathcal{X}},\dot{H}^{2}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
follows easily from (i) and
K(t,\cdot,{\mathcal{X}},\dot{H}^{2}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))\simeq\omega^{2}_{p}(\sqrt{t},\cdot)
is the standard result (see
[
34
, Theorem 1]
)
∎

We present a consequence of Proposition
3.16
, which we find to be of considerable interest.

Corollary 5.4
.

Let
n\geq 3
and
p\in(1,\frac{n}{2})
. Then, for any parameter
\Phi
of the
K
-method, the space
K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}}_{p},\dot{{\mathcal{D}}}_{{\mathcal{A}}_{p}})
admits a completion in
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})+L^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, where
r=\frac{np}{n-2p}
.

Indeed, the condition
p\in(1,\frac{n}{2})
implies, by Proposition
5.3
(i) and the Sobolev inequality
[
11
, Corollary 9.13, p. 283]
(cf. Proposition 9.9 therein), that

\dot{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathcal{A}_{p}}\hookrightarrow L^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\hookrightarrow L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})+L^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).

The closedness of
{\mathcal{A}}_{p}
in
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})+L^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and the properties of
{\mathcal{T}}_{p}
stated as assumptions in Proposition
3.16
can be read from the proofs of Proposition
4.1
and Theorem
4.2
. Thus, the statement follows directly from Proposition
3.16
.

As a consequence of Proposition
5.2
and Corollary
5.4
,
if
p\in(1,\frac{n}{2})
, then for each
\theta\in(0,1)
and
q\in[1,\infty)
the homogeneous Besov space
\dot{B}^{2\theta}_{p,q}
is isomorphic to a subspace of
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})+L^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, where
r=\frac{np}{n-2p}
.

Note that for any
p\in(1,\infty)
,
q\in[1,\infty)
and
\theta\in(0,1)
, the statement (iii) of Lemma
3.9
apply to
{\mathcal{A}}:={\mathcal{A}}_{p}
,
{\mathcal{X}}={\mathcal{X}}_{p}
and
\Phi
as specified on the beginning of this section. In particular, the completion of
K_{\Phi}({\mathcal{X}},\dot{D}_{\mathcal{A}})
is isomorphic to
K_{\Phi}(J{\mathcal{X}}_{p},\dot{H}^{2}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
.
It is sufficient to combine Lemma
3.9
(iii) with
[
9
, Theorem 3.4.2, p.47]
and
[
9
, Theorem 6.3.2, p.148]
.

The
L^{1}
-m.r. of the negative Laplacian on the homogeneous Besov spaces
\dot{B}^{s}_{p,1,2}\subset{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}_{h}
is stated in
[
19
]
(for any
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
p\in[1,\infty]
). Since the corresponding
force terms space

L^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\dot{B}^{s}_{p,1,2})
for the associated Cauchy problem is not complete when
s>\frac{n}{p}
, the statement of that result is somewhat inconsistent with underlying intentions (e.g. the values of resulting solutions do not necessary belong to
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}_{h}
).

The following corollary provides an extension of that result for the negative
homogeneous
Laplacian on the
classical
homogeneous Besov spaces
\dot{B}^{s}_{p,q}=\dot{B}^{s}_{p,q,1}
for
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
p\in[1,\infty]
and
q\in[1,\infty)
.
These spaces are complete, but in general, they possess realizations in
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}_{\infty}
, and consequently the values of solutions corresponding to all admissible force terms do not belong to
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}
, that is, somewhat lose their physical interpretation.
Since
\dot{B}^{s}_{p,1}\simeq\dot{B}^{s}_{p,1,2}
for
s\leq\frac{n}{p}
and
p\in[1,\infty]
, the corresponding conclusion of this corollary coincides with that from
[
19
]
.

However, for the full range of parameters
p
,
q
and
s
, our preparatory results presented above (Propositions
5.2
and
5.3
) allow us to place such studies within the framework established in Sections
3
and 4; see, e.g. Propositions
3.5
and
3.6
, and Corollary
3.8
.

Let
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
. We define the Bessel potential operator on
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}/{\mathcal{P}}
by the formula

\dot{I}^{\sigma}(f+{\mathcal{P}}):=J{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}|\xi|^{\sigma}{\mathcal{F}}f,\qquad f\in{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}.

This operator is also called the lifting operator due to the fact that it is an isomorphism of
\dot{B}^{s}_{p,q}
onto
\dot{B}^{s-\sigma}_{p,q}
for any
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
p,q\in[1,\infty]
(see
[
52
, Theorem 1, Section 5.2.3, p.242]
). The homogeneous counterpart
\dot{\Delta}
of the distributional Laplacian is defined as
\dot{\Delta}:=-\dot{I}^{2}
.

Corollary 5.5
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
p\in[1,\infty]
and
q\in[1,\infty]
.

(i)
The part of the negative homogeneous Laplacian
-\dot{\Delta}
in
\dot{B}^{s}_{p,q}

has
L^{r}
-m.r. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
for all
r\in(1,\infty)\cup\{q\}
.

(ii)
Let
{\mathcal{A}}_{p}
and
{\mathcal{X}}_{p}
have the meaning specified at the beginning of this section. Let
\mathbb{F}
be the subspace of
L^{1}_{loc}([0,\infty);{\mathcal{X}}_{p})
given by

\mathbb{F}:=\bigcup_{s>0\atop r\in(1,\infty)\cup\{q\}}L^{r}_{loc}([0,\infty);B^{s}_{p,q})

Then for every
f\in\mathbb{F}
the corresponding mild solution
u
of the Cauchy problem
(
CP
{}_{{\mathcal{A}}_{p},f,0})
belongs to
W^{1,1}_{loc}([0,\infty);{\mathcal{X}}_{p})
,
u(t)\in D({\mathcal{A}}_{p})
and

u^{\prime}(t)-\Delta u(t)=f(t)\qquad\textrm{for a.e. }t>0.

Moreover, for any
q\in[1,\infty]
and
\theta\in(0,1)
, if
\|f\|_{\dot{B}^{2\theta}_{p,q}}\in E_{v}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
, for any
E
and
v
which satisfy the conditions specified in Proposition
3.5
(
b
)
, then

\|u\|_{\dot{B}^{2\theta}_{p,q}},\,\|u^{\prime}\|_{\dot{B}^{2\theta}_{p,q}},\,\|\Delta u\|_{\dot{B}^{2\theta}_{p,q}}\in E_{v}(\mathbb{R}_{+}).

In particular, the last statement holds for
E=L^{\alpha}
with
\alpha\in(1,\infty)
and any non-increasing weight function
v
on
(0,\infty)
.

Proof.

(i) First, we prove the statement for any
s\in(0,2)
.
Recall that the part
-\Delta_{B^{s}_{p,q}}
of
{\mathcal{A}}_{p}=-\Delta
(on
{\mathcal{X}}_{p}
) in
B^{s}_{p,q}
has
L^{r}
-m.r. on finite intervals for any
r\in(1,\infty)\cup\{q\}
. Note that each
g\in J{\mathcal{X}}_{p}
(see (
62
)) has exactly one representative in
{\mathcal{X}}_{p}
, so the map
J{\mathcal{X}}_{p}\ni g\mapsto\phi\in{\mathcal{X}}_{p}
, where
\phi\in g\cap{\mathcal{X}}_{p}
is well-defined bijection. Denote its inverse, which of course coincides with
J_{|{\mathcal{X}}_{p}}
, by
j
.
Let
A:=-j\Delta_{B^{s}_{p,q}}j^{-1}
with
D(A):=jD(\Delta_{B^{s}_{p,q}})
and let
X:=jB^{s}_{p,q}
be equipped with the image norm, i.e.
\|j\phi\|_{X}:=\|\phi\|_{B^{s}_{p,q}}
,
\phi\in B^{s}_{p,q}
. Of course,
A
inherits the
L^{r}
-m.r. on finite intervals from
-\Delta_{B^{s}_{p,q}}
.
Set
\|j\phi\|_{*}:=[\phi]_{\Phi,{\mathcal{A}}_{p}}
,
j\phi\in X
, where
\Phi
is the parameter corresponding to the functor
(\,\cdot\,)_{\frac{s}{2},q}
, that is,
\Phi=L^{q}(t^{q(1-\frac{s}{2})-1})
when
q\in[1,\infty)
and
\Phi=L^{\infty}(t^{s/2})
when
q=\infty
.
Combining Theorem
3.1
(ii) with Proposition
3.5
(a) if
q\in[1,\infty)
and the proof of Theorem
3.3
(see (
40
)) with Proposition
3.6
if
q=\infty
, we deduce that the canonical extension
\widetilde{A}
of
A
on a completion of
(X,\|\cdot\|_{*})
has
L^{r}
-m.r. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
for any
r\in(1,\infty)\cup\{q\}
.

By Proposition
5.2
, we can assume that
\widetilde{X}=\dot{B}^{s}_{p,q}
(as sets) with equivalent norms and
\widetilde{A}
is an extension of
A
, i.e.
A\subset\widetilde{A}
.
Note that the operator
\widetilde{A}
is a closure of
A
, that is, the smallest closed extension of
A
. Indeed, since
D(A)
is dense in
\widetilde{X}
and is invariant under the
C_{0}
-semigroup
\widetilde{T}
generated by
\widetilde{A}
, the set of vectors
\{\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{0}^{\epsilon}\widetilde{T}(\tau)j\phi\,\mathrm{d}\tau:\,\epsilon>0,\,j\phi\in D(A)\}\subset D(A)
is dense in
D_{\widetilde{A}}
.
In particular, for any
\widetilde{\phi}\in D(\widetilde{A})
there exists a sequence
(j\phi_{n})
in
D(A)
such that
j\phi_{n}\rightarrow\widetilde{\phi}
and
\widetilde{A}j\phi_{n}=-j\Delta\phi_{n}=\dot{I}^{2}\phi_{n}\rightarrow\widetilde{A}\widetilde{\phi}
in
{\mathcal{X}}
as
n\rightarrow\infty
.
Therefore, to prove that the part
\dot{A}=\dot{A}_{s,p,q}
of
\dot{I}^{2}
in
\dot{B}^{s}_{p,q}
has
L^{r}
-m.r. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
, it suffices to show
that
\dot{A}
is closed. However, since
\dot{I}^{2}
is an isomorphism from
\dot{B}^{s+2}_{p,q}
onto
\dot{B}^{s}_{p,q}
,
D(\dot{A})=\dot{B}^{s}_{p,q}\cap\dot{B}^{s+2}_{p,q}
equipped with the graph norm of
\dot{A}
is a Banach space, it yields the desired claim and completes the proof in the case when
s\in(0,2)
.

For other values of the parameter
s\in\mathbb{R}
, fix any
s_{0}\in(0,2)
and
r\in(1,\infty)\cup\{q\}
. Let
f\in L^{r}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\dot{B}_{p,q}^{s})
. Since
\dot{I}^{s_{0}-s}:\dot{B}^{s_{0}}_{p,q}\to\dot{B}^{s}_{p,q}
is an isomorphism, clearly
\dot{I}^{s-s_{0}}f\in L^{r}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\dot{B}_{p,q}^{s_{0}})
. By the
L^{r}
-m.r. of
A_{p,q,s_{0}}=:\dot{A}
, there exists
\widetilde{u}\in W^{1,1}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\dot{B}^{s_{0}}_{p,q})
with
\widetilde{u}(t)\in D(\dot{A})
,

\widetilde{u}^{\prime}(t)+\dot{A}\widetilde{u}(t)=\dot{I}^{s-s_{0}}f(t)\qquad\textrm{for a.e. }t>0

and

\|\dot{A}\tilde{u}\|_{L^{r}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\dot{B}^{s_{0}}_{p,q})}\leq C\|\dot{I}^{s-s_{0}}f\|_{L^{r}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\dot{B}^{s_{0}}_{p,q})}

for some constant
C
independent on
f
. Since
\dot{I}^{s_{0}-s}\dot{A}\widetilde{u}(t)=A_{p,q,s}\dot{I}^{s_{0}-s}\widetilde{u}(t)
and
\dot{I}^{s_{0}-s}\widetilde{u}^{\prime}(t)=(\dot{I}^{s_{0}-s}\widetilde{u})^{\prime}(t)
for a.e.
t>0
, it follows that the function
u(t):=\dot{I}^{s_{0}-s}\widetilde{u}(t)
,
t>0
, belongs to
W^{1,1}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\dot{B}^{s}_{p,q})
,
u(t)\in D(A_{p,q,s})
and

u^{\prime}(t)+{\mathcal{A}}_{p,q,s}u(t)=f(t)\qquad\textrm{for a.e. }t>0.

Moreover, the following estimate holds

\|{\mathcal{A}}_{p,q,s}u\|_{L^{r}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\dot{B}^{s}_{p,q})}\leq C\|\dot{I}^{s_{0}-s}\|_{{\mathcal{L}}(\dot{B}^{s}_{p,q};\dot{B}^{s_{0}}_{p,q})}\|\dot{I}^{s-s_{0}}\|_{{\mathcal{L}}(\dot{B}^{s_{0}}_{p,q};\dot{B}^{s}_{p,q})}\|f\|_{L^{r}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\dot{B}^{s}_{p,q})},

which completes the proof.

(ii) All the statements in (ii), except for the implication

\|f\|_{\dot{B}^{2\theta}_{p,q}}\in E_{v}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\quad\Rightarrow\quad\|u\|_{\dot{B}^{2\theta}_{p,q}}\in E_{v}(\mathbb{R}_{+})

can be deduced from Corollary
3.8
.
We turn to the proof of the above implication.
As the first part of the proof of (i) shows, the operator
\dot{A}=\dot{A}_{2\theta,p,q}
has
L^{r}
-m.r. on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
0\in\rho(\dot{A})
. Therefore, since a semigroup
\dot{T}
generated by
\dot{A}
on
B^{2\theta}_{p,q}
is uniformly bounded on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
(see
[
21
, Theorem 4.3]
) and holomorphic,
\dot{T}
has the negative exponential growth bound. In particular, by Young’s inequality, the corresponding solution operator
U_{\dot{A}}
is bounded on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\dot{B}^{2\theta}_{p,q})
. Again, the boundedness and analyticity of
\dot{T}
, gives

\sup_{t>0,\,l=0,1}\Big\|t^{l+1}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{l}}{\mathrm{d}t^{l}}\dot{T}(t)\Big\|_{{\mathcal{L}}(\dot{B}^{2\theta}_{p,q})}=\sup_{t>0,\,l=0,1}\|t^{l+1}\dot{A}^{l}\dot{T}(t)\|_{{\mathcal{L}}(\dot{B}^{2\theta}_{p,q})}<\infty.

Thus,
\dot{U}
is a singular operator with the kernel
\dot{T}
satisfying the standard Calderón-Zygmund condition.
The same extrapolation argument as that applied in the proof of Proposition
3.5
(a), based on
[
16
, Theorems 5.1 and 4.3]
, gives that
U_{\dot{A}}
is bounded on
E_{v}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\dot{B}^{s}_{p,q})
. It readily gives the desired implication. The fact, that every positive, non-increasing function on
(0,\infty)
belongs to
A^{-}_{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
is stated in the remark before Proposition
3.5
. This completes the proof.
∎

We conclude with the following observation. Since for any
s^{\prime}>s>0
,
B^{s^{\prime}}_{p,q}\subset B^{s}_{p,q}\subset{\mathcal{X}}_{p}
, the space
\mathbb{F}
in Corollary
5.5
(ii) could be considered as a
limiting
force term space for the
L^{q}
-m.r. property of the negative Laplacian
{\mathcal{A}}_{p}=-\Delta
on
{\mathcal{X}}_{p}
. Recall that, e.g. if
q=1
, then
{\mathcal{A}}_{p}
on
{\mathcal{X}}_{p}
(
p\in[1,\infty]
) does not have
L^{1}
-m.r. on any finite interval; cf. also Theorem
3.1
(iii).

Data availability:
no data was used for the research described in the article.
